**Accepted by Mike (2026-11-13)**, as recommended; §5.3a R1 is instead superseded by M7, which carves the role table from buddy blocks:
§5.2a R3 (AP trampoline's writable alias), R4 (1 GiB pages gated on the BSP's CPUID), R5 (a map needing over 2048 tables stops the boot), R6 (large leaves and MTRRs), R10 (guest-test usercopy uses user addresses); §5.3a R3 (a second proof on one root panics), R4 (O(slots) scans), R5 (`start_position` scans); §6.9a R2 (whole-table scans at boot); §7.1 R3 (a racing pin sees `Conflict` for the whole call), R4 (tables built by undone batches stay uncharged until teardown); §7.3 R1 (handles re-resolved per step), R4 (a protect in steps can refuse a W+X alias), R5 (pieces left by a stopped caller keep their leases).


## 10. M5 design (2026-11-13; revised after its review, §10.7)

M5 lets a job use what M2–M6 made possible. Today a job gets 16 MiB, 4 MemoryObjects and 10 mapping slots, the SDK's `mmap` serves everything from at most four eager arenas of at most 16 MiB, and nothing tells userspace how much RAM exists. Facts below come from a read-only survey (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 2026-11-13) checked against the code; byte costs are field-layout estimates to be measured in step 1.

### 10.1 Decisions

- **Budget (Mike, 2026-11-13; §3 item 3):** each job's MEMORY_PAGES limit is half of the RAM free once init's resident services have started, capped by the platform config's size when one is set. Init computes it once per boot, before the first job, so every job of a boot gets the same budget. *Refinement (§10.7 finding 6; accepted by Mike, 2026-11-13):* a resident reserve is set aside first, so the budget is half of (free − reserve), and all jobs together are held to (free − reserve) by a parent group (item 5).
- **Overcommit (§11; accepted by Mike for the SDK, 2026-11-13, §10.7 finding 3):** four jobs at half each can promise twice what the jobs' parent group allows. An eager create past the parent's limit fails with `RESOURCE_POLICY`, and one that finds no frames fails with `NO_MEMORY`, as now. The SDK reserves on-demand memory (item 8), so, as on Linux, `malloc` of a large block rarely fails and running out shows up on touch: a job over its own budget is ended on the touch (Linux's memory-cgroup OOM kill of the job), and frame exhaustion ends the toucher until M9's killer chooses a victim instead (§8.9).
- **Calls yield the CPU (Mike, 2026-11-13; §10.7 finding 2):** M8's yield moves into M5 instead of capping a call. A call in steps (map, unmap, protect, commit) yields the CPU at its safe points when its quantum has expired, and `MemoryObjectCreate`'s zeroing gets safe points between its steps (stop and yield), so neither a GiB-scale call nor a single CPU can hold the machine (§7.3 R3). How a call resumes after yielding is settled in step 1 against the scheduler's model (item 5).

### 10.2 Deepwyrm

1. **How much RAM: a TaskGroup memory topic, scoped.** A new `object_get_info` topic, `DW_OBJECT_INFO_TASK_GROUP_MEMORY_V1`, on a TaskGroup handle with INSPECT, returns a 32-byte record: the group's own MEMORY_PAGES limit and usage, the smallest headroom (limit − usage) over the group and its limited ancestors, and the free frame count. The free frame count is filled only for the root TaskGroup (the one boot gives init); every other group gets zero there, so a job holding INSPECT on its own group learns its budget and nothing system-wide (§10.7 finding 1). Init reads it on the root group; jobs never hold a TaskGroup handle, and get their budget from init (item 5). Locking: the query holds T (as every info query does) and takes P inside it for the free count, which is the T-then-P order. Free frames are the allocator's free-range sum; admission does not read it (exhaustion is found at allocation), so the record is a snapshot, not a promise. `[[object_info]]` topics carry no phase in the schema, so the topic needs none.
2. **Mapping slots: 10 → 64 per AddressRegion.** Every region has the same count, so all 32 regions pay: about 6.4 KB static per slot, about 410 KB at 64 (versus 64 KB now). *Measured 2026-11-13, replacing the estimate of about 90 KB:* from the production kernel's stack-size section, the map, unmap and protect frames grow with the slot count and with the object, lease and registry pool sizes, because the region and object models copy whole arrays into locals. At 64 slots the stepped map's direct call chain needs about 487 KB of the 512 KiB thread stack (`commit_specs` 147 KB, `prepare_replace` 125 KB, `handle_address_region_mutation` 51 KB), so 64 slots stay off (Deepwyrm `31827e1`) until those working arrays live in static scratch owned under T (each runs inside one hold, so one set suffices) and the frames no longer scale with slots or pools. The whole syscall path's direct-call chain is about 263 KB before M5's pool raises and 287 KB after (the 130 KB `PayloadFinalizer::finalize_chain`, which grows with the registry, is the deepest), so the scratch move covers the finalizer too. Teardown may instead run on the 256 KiB reaper stack. *Built (2026-11-13):* `cargo xtask kernel-check` replaces the old `kernel/tests/x86_64_memory_target_artifact` gate (`#[ignore]`d, refused the pinned lane, required the dropped `DEEPWYRM_ACCEPTED_REQUEST`, assumed 16 thread stacks, built retired selectors; Mike chose a lean check over its repair, 2026-11-13), which is deleted. It builds the production kernel and an analysis copy through the pinned target lane (`DEEPWYRM_EMIT_STACK_SIZES` adds `-Z emit-stack-sizes` and links with `--emit-relocs`), and checks: production is a static ET_EXEC image with no test-only symbols or selector markers (moved from the old gate); the analysis copy's `.text` and `.rodata` are byte-identical to production; and each kernel stack's deepest direct-call chain, plus a 1 KiB allowance for the entry assembly's saved state, leaves a quarter spare. It reads the ELF itself, with no host LLVM tools: frames from `.stack_sizes` (prologue-decoded for precompiled builtins and assembly), call sites from the kept `.text` relocations, calls through `.rodata` slots resolved from the image, the bound runtime's jump to its trampoline and the reaper handoff's callback named by hand. Panic and formatting edges are cut (terminal; inline stack probes make an overflow a guard-page fault, not corruption). Any recursion fails except VT-d `free_level` (one call per page-table level, depth 4). Stacks, from the entry assembly: the 512 KiB thread stack (syscall, preemption gates, exceptions, with any interrupt or exception nested on top), the 256 KiB privilege-entry stack (interrupts from user mode), the 256 KiB reaper stack and the 16 KiB IST stacks. Dynamic dispatch (trait objects, function pointers in writable memory) is not bounded statically; the measured half paints each kernel stack when it is bound and has `dev-boot --check` report and bound every stack's high-water mark after the probes. *First run, at Deepwyrm `0cf12a6` (the pair Wyrmroot `c955a6a` pins):* the thread stack needs 368,896 of 393,216 usable bytes (the map chain: handler 25 KB, region mutation 45 KB, three map layers 41 KB each, `commit_specs` 75 KB, `prepare_replace` 89 KB; reading relocations finds edges the earlier disassembly prototype missed, so its 287 KB was low). **The reaper stack fails:** process teardown (`unmap_inactive_userspace` → `AddressRegion::unmap` → `commit_specs` → `prepare_replace`) is bounded at 301,736 bytes, past the whole 256 KiB stack. With the pre-M5 pool sizes the same chain is 242,776 bytes (inside the stack, without the quarter spare), so M5's pool raise (`2384295`) pushed it over. The privilege-entry and IST stacks use about 1 KB each. *Fixed without static scratch (2026-11-13):* the frames were copies, not working storage. (1) `MappingFinalReleases` was sized by the registry (640 entries, 20 KB, several copies per frame) though one transaction drops at most `2 * BATCH + 1` pins; it is now a fixed 129 entries for up to 64 slots (Deepwyrm `62c8ee9`). (2) Channel finalization returned a 16.6 KB `WakeBatch` by value through four layers and collected drained-transfer releases in a registry-sized array; the finalizer now keeps one batch per chain and the releases go straight to its pending list (`62c8ee9`). (3) The replace plan (19.5 KB at 64 slots) was built in `plan_replace`, returned, and moved into `PreparedReplace`, which was returned again; the caller now owns one plan and lends it to the transaction, and `commit_specs` builds the next mapping set once. With those, 64 slots pass: thread stack 201,344 and reaper 173,024 of their usable bytes. No lock or static scratch was needed, so nothing new is held across page-table publication. The reaper's deepest path is now Channel receive (`collect_changed`, 67 KB), the same batch-by-value pattern, owned with the rest of the pool-sized frames by the OS stack plan's C4.
3. **Per-job MemoryObjects: 4 → 64,** with the production ledger following: `JOB_OWN_MEMORY_OBJECTS = 64`, `JOB_REGION_MAPPINGS = 64`. `MEMORY_PEAK` 81 → 321 and `REGISTRY_PEAK` 346 → 586. A job's leases are bounded by its region's 64 slots, not by its objects plus 61, so `MAPPING_PEAK` is 53 + 4 × 64 = 309, not 565 (§10.7 finding 11). Pools: memory 384, leases 320, registry 640; the bootfs entry below raises `BOOTFS_ENTRY_PEAK` 17 → 18. Step 1 checks each against the geometry tests, which change with them. *Since OS stack plan C1 (Deepwyrm `679fb3d`, 2026-11-13) the kernel holds only its pools, in `kernel/arch/x86_64/pools.toml`; this demand ledger is Wyrmroot's `tools/xtask/src/kernel_demand.rs`, which the product build checks against them. `BOOTFS_ENTRY_PEAK` left with it: the archive's entry bound is Wyrmroot's own parser limit, not a kernel pool. Step 2's baseline changes edit Wyrmroot's ledger, not the kernel.*
4. **Released on unmap (§10.7 finding 5).** M6 has no decommit, so a partial `munmap` of an on-demand mapping would keep the pages under the unmapped part charged until the object dies. For an on-demand object with no handle left (only leases, which is how the SDK holds its mappings), an unmap or a lease's split releases and uncharges the committed pages that no remaining lease covers, in the unmap's own steps. An object anyone still holds a handle to keeps its pages, since the holder may map them again. *Ordering (found 2026-11-13 while designing it):* releasing at one unmap's settlement is not enough, because two leases may map the same object pages: if both are unmapped concurrently, the second unmap's settlement could free frames that the first unmap's shootdown has not yet removed from every TLB. So each on-demand object counts the unmap transactions in flight that removed one of its leases (raised in the hold that removes the lease, lowered at that transaction's settlement), and the release runs only when the count falls to zero and the object has no handle: every lease removal's shootdown has then settled, and the pages no live lease covers are mapped nowhere. The release clears index entries, so it is idempotent; a fault or fill-in can commit only under a live lease, which keeps those pages. Host tests: two leases over the same pages unmapped with interleaved settlements, a partial unmap under a live overlapping lease, and an object whose handle closes after the unmap (it keeps its pages until finalization, as today).
5. **Yield at safe points** (§10.1). Each safe point between steps of a stepped call, of `MemoryObjectCreate`'s zeroing and of the commit and fill-in steps checks for a stop (as now) and for an expired quantum, and on expiry gives the CPU to the next runnable thread before taking its next step. The step's state lives in the call's facade frame, so the design (resuming on the same kernel stack, or a restartable continuation, whichever the scheduler supports) is settled in step 1 against the scheduler and the stop and preemption rules of §8.5, and reviewed before it is built. Host tests drive a chain with a quantum that expires every step.

### 10.3 Wyrmroot

6. **Platform config:** a new bootfs entry, `system/bootstrap/platform-config-v1` (WRPC v1: magic, version, size, `u64 job_memory_bytes_max` and `u64 resident_reserve_bytes`, zero meaning none and the default respectively, reserved fields zero; strict, bounded, fail-closed). The product builder writes it; the dev-boot product sets a cap (item 11's probe needs one) and the default reserve.
7. **Init:** after its resident roles are READY and before it accepts a job, init reads the memory topic on the root group. The reserve is `resident_reserve_bytes`, or by default the larger of 256 MiB and one eighth of free. Init creates one jobs TaskGroup under its own with MEMORY_PAGES = free − reserve, so all jobs together can never take the residents' reserve (§10.7 finding 6), and every job group is created under it. A job's budget is `min((free − reserve) / 2, cap)` in pages, saturated to `DW_TASK_GROUP_LIMIT_UNLIMITED − 1`, since the limit's maximum means unlimited (§10.7 finding 7). The job limit table is built at run time instead of the `JOB_TASK_GROUP_LIMITS` constant; `JOB_MEMORY_OBJECTS` becomes 64. **Failure is never "no cap" (§10.7 finding 8):** if the topic cannot be read, the config is missing, malformed or truncated, or free is not above the reserve, init logs the cause and uses the old 16 MiB budget, still under the jobs group (if the jobs group itself cannot be made, under init's own group; accepted by Mike, 2026-11-14, §10.11). Init gives each job its budget in the job-v2 startup record (a new field), and the shell's `status` shows it.
8. **Repin** Wyrmroot and the SDK to the new Deepwyrm together (memory `sdk-pin-follows-wyrmroot`), with Wyrmroot's consumer of the new topic constant and record in `deepwyrm-syscall`, and Wyrmroot's docs.

### 10.4 SDK

9. **`mmap` by size.** Every anonymous mapping that does not go to an arena gets its own on-demand MemoryObject and mapping: non-fixed requests of 1 MiB or more, and every `MAP_FIXED` request (§10.7 finding 13). Reserving costs one index page, and `munmap`, whole or partial, returns what it covers (item 4). `mprotect` on a dedicated mapping goes straight to the kernel and may split it, at most two more slots; on arena pages it stays `ENOTSUP`.
10. **Arenas scale with the budget (§10.7 finding 4).** Requests under 1 MiB stay in arenas, now on-demand and reserved large (each the job's budget divided by `MAX_ARENAS`, at most 1 GiB), so small allocations are limited by the budget, not by 4 × 4 MiB. The fixed 4096-page bitmaps give way to a two-level bitmap sized at arena creation from the arena's own memory. A freed arena page is re-zeroed on reuse only if it was handed out before, so pages never touched are never committed.
11. **Running out:** the image takes 3 of 64 slots and arenas at most 4, so a program can hold about 57 dedicated mappings at once. Past that, and when a split finds no free slot, `mmap`, `munmap` and `mprotect` return `ENOMEM`, not `EINVAL`.

### 10.5 Guest proof (`dev-boot --check --sdk`, 2 GiB guest)

12. `bin/hello` reports the budget from its startup record and checks it against init's logged computation; init's own read of the topic is checked in the boot log against a second read of the free count and the jobs group's limit. A job's own read of the topic (if it held a group handle) returns zero free frames, tested on the host.
13. It maps an eager object larger than 16 MiB and touches every page, then protects and unmaps it in steps: M4's and M4b's first guest run with eager backing (§9 ledger). Usage, read through `status`, drops back after the `munmap`, including after a partial one (item 4).
14. It times a large eager create and map and prints it, re-measuring L6's 44 ms fixed cost on the direct map and the longest time one step holds the CPU (§9 ledger). Explaining the 44 ms, if it remains, is part of this step, not only measuring it.
15. A job is stopped while a large map is in its steps; once it is reaped, its jobs group's usage is back to what it was and the free count (init's read) matches. That a stop landed between steps, rather than before the first, is shown by the kernel's step counter for the call in the boot log. A published inner step and its output are covered by the same probe stopping after the first step has published.
16. An SDK program allocates past the old 16 MiB ceiling (one `malloc` of 256 MiB and more than 16 MiB of small ones) and frees them, and SQLite runs as before. A second program allocates past its budget and is ended with `RESOURCE_POLICY`; a third fills its 64 slots and gets `ENOMEM`. The dev-boot config's cap is shown to bound the budget.

### 10.6 Steps

1. Deepwyrm: the scoped memory topic, slots and per-job objects with the geometry, release on unmap, yielding at safe points (designed and reviewed first, item 5), and the stack-size check; host tests; development tier.
2. Wyrmroot and SDK repinned; the platform config entry and builder; init's reserve, jobs group, run-time budget and limit table, and the startup-record field; `status`.
3. SDK: `mmap` by size over on-demand objects, scalable arenas and `ENOMEM`; its host tests replace the arena tests.
4. Probes 12–16 in dev-boot, with §10.8's yield coverage (review D-1).
5. Independent security review of the stage; fixes; §9 ledger and an as-built record. *Review done and its batch fixed, 2026-11-14 (§10.11); the stage's as-built record is §10.18.*
6. *Built 2026-11-14 except the Stop failure that stopped it (§10.13).* *Added by step 5 (§10.11):* step the finalization of an on-demand backing (kernel R-1), designed and reviewed first; measure the holds the step report does not see (that finalization, §10.9 item 8's release cost, §10.10 item 8's teardown latency); close probe 14's per-page cost; then a second kernel security pass at default effort over what the first did not cover. Also from §10.11: cap the fallback jobs limit at free − reserve, and check that the SDK run reads `memory-test slots`' exit status. M5 does not close before this step.
7. *Added by step 6's stop (§10.13), 2026-11-14. Built and checked 2026-11-15 (§10.18).* Build the two designs that answer it, each with its host tests, at the development tier:
   - first §10.15 revision 2 (every hold ends drained of the cleanup queue; the stop's handoff classified once), after its Q3 check. It goes first because its hold-end assertion makes a mistake in the new teardown step fail at the hold that caused it;
   - then §10.14 revision 2 with its progress rule (a dead root torn down by mappings, then tables).

   - *§10.14 is built and integrated (2026-11-15, Deepwyrm `c1537d7`, merged as `fdaa5a4`; "As built" in §10.14).* Its boots showed dev-boot's map-stop check can miss the one `churn-map` kill; the probe is made to kill it more than once (Wyrmroot, this step).
   - *§10.15 is built and integrated (2026-11-14, Deepwyrm `b9095c5`; "As built" in §10.15).* Its audit found three more stop defects, all older than this step. They are designed together in §10.16, reviewed independently. *Mike approved §10.16 revision 2 on 2026-11-15, with the retry in the kernel (item 3); it is built in this step.* *Built and integrated 2026-11-15, Deepwyrm `3299f77` ("As built" in §10.16), with three changes from the coordinator's review.*

   Then repeated `--smp` boots on the result, reporting the stop panic's count and `owed-longest-us` against step 6's figures (§10.12, §10.13); a security review of §10.16 as built, by the coordinator (§9; §10.14 and §10.15 have theirs in §10.17); and the stage's as-built record (step 5). *The diagnostic lanes `lane/stop-diag` (`60d965b`, `7fbf0b3`) and `lane/m5s7-teardown-diag` (`69b450b`) were retired and their branches deleted on Mike's word, 2026-11-15; their commits were never integrated.* M5 does not close before this step.

**Step 2 as built (2026-11-14).** Wyrmroot `505198a` and SDK `d62590a`, pinning Deepwyrm `23972de` (the typed `object_get_task_group_memory_info_v1` in `deepwyrm-syscall`). Items 6–8 as designed, with these as-built facts and changes:
- *The record is 48 bytes,* not 32 (the generated `DwTaskGroupMemoryInfoV1`). Bootstrap passes init the root TaskGroup, so init's read carries the free count.
- *Init has no log stream,* so "init logs" (item 7, probe 12) is init's plan held for the boot and reported on request. WRLJ minor 3 adds `QUERY_MEMORY`/`MEMORY`: the plan (budget, jobs limit, reserve, cap, free when planned, source, fallback cause) with live reads of the jobs group's usage and the free count. Probes 12, 13 and 15 read it.
- *The shell's `memory` command, not `status`, prints it* (item 7): `status` is one query to consoled's snapshot, and joining a second query to init into it would give one command two failure paths. Accepted by Mike, 2026-11-14.
- *Startup record:* a job's INIT gains a `u64` budget after its role records, as WRLP 1.14 (`JobV2`) and 1.15 (`JobV2Namespace`), replacing 1.12 and 1.13. The SDK keeps it in `Process::memory_budget` for step 3.
- *Config:* WRPC v1 is 64 bytes; byte counts must be whole pages. `xtask product` sets no cap; dev-boot caps a job at 512 MiB, which binds in the 2 GiB guest (planned free about 1.86 GiB, reserve 256 MiB, jobs limit about 1.61 GiB). Init reads the config from the bootfs it already maps, so it needs no further MemoryObject (§10.7 risk). The kernel demand ledger counts the jobs group (one more TaskGroup and handle).
- *`hello on-demand-exhaust`* now spends the quota after READY, committing an object four times the budget. Committing a whole 512 MiB budget, or mapping a 2 GiB object with the CPU hogs running, takes longer than init's one-second READY timeout, which first showed as `loader-failure`.
Checked: host tests of every changed crate, `xtask test clippy`, `xtask test fmt`, the SDK's host tests; `dev-boot --check` on one CPU, `--smp` and `--sdk` on Deepwyrm `23972de`, Wyrmroot `505198a`, SDK `d62590a`.

**Scheduled for step 4 (observed 2026-11-14).** In one development boot, init's READY timeout ended a job partway through committing about 512 MiB while two CPU hogs ran. The shell's reply then stalled for the check's whole 180 s. Nine deliberate repeats (a job committing toward its quota before READY, ended by the same timeout, three without the hogs and six with them) did not stall, and each returned the jobs group's usage and the free count to their earlier values. Probe 15 (a job stopped while a large commit, map and create are in their steps, on the product and the yield-everywhere kernels) must cover this case and show no stall; a reproduction is diagnosed and fixed in step 4.

**Step 4 findings and decisions (2026-11-14).**
- *The step-2 stall is reproduced and explained.* A job ended by `TaskGroupTerminate` has its whole user address space unmapped inside the terminator's syscall (`complete_task_group_termination` → `attempt_exited_root_teardown` → `unmap_inactive_userspace`, one transaction per mapping, interrupts off). Measured in dev-boot under QEMU TCG (no KVM for this user, so not hardware times) with a diagnostic lane (`lane/term-diag`): 0.59 s for a job with 64 MiB touched on demand, 1.79 s for one with a 256 MiB eager mapping; the unmap is all but about 5 ms of it, about 27 to 35 µs a page. Init's reply then misses wyrmsh's 1 s control deadline, and the late reply leaves the shell's later requests out of step, so the next one hangs (as in step 2, where the READY timeout's termination ran long). Since the time grows with the job's memory, this breaks §10.1's rule that no call holds a CPU at GiB scale.
- *Mike (2026-11-14): queue it and step it.* Termination returns once its targets are stopped and exited; the teardown of each exited root is queued and done in bounded steps where the kernel drains its cleanup work, as release on unmap is (§10.9), with the dead job's charges falling as it goes. Designed in §10.10, reviewed independently, then built. wyrmsh also discards a reply that does not answer its current request. *(Correction, 2026-11-14: it does not. At its one-second deadline wyrmsh prints `unavailable:` and exits, and consoled restarts it with an empty job table; that is why `wait` then answered `foreign`. Step 4 keeps init inside the deadline instead: §10.10 revision 4.)*
- *Probe 14 so far:* an eager 256 MiB create takes about 0.1 s (the direct map's zeroing), but map, protect and unmap each take about 2 s with the hogs running, so L6's "fixed" cost is per-page page-table work, not zeroing. Which per-page operation costs it is found in step 4.
- *Mike (2026-11-14): review D-1's two-thread probe* (a second thread unmaps a parked map's output page; the map returns `BAD_ADDRESS`) *waits for jobs to have threads.* Jobs hold no Process handle, so a job cannot start a thread; the probe moves to the toolchain step that gives jobs threads (§9). The rest of D-1 stays in step 4.

8. *Added by Mike, 2026-11-15 (§10.18).* Bound how long a queued finalization stays owed (§10.19). *The first design was withdrawn the same day: the worker's check found its premise false. Revision 2 measured first (built, `03e3a9d`). Revision 3, the wake of an idle BSP when owed work is queued, was approved by Mike the same day and is the bound.* **Built and checked 2026-11-15** (§10.19, revision 3 as built): Deepwyrm `9032966`.

### 10.7 Design review

Claude Opus 5.5 (default effort), 2026-11-13, at Deepwyrm `988540f`, read-only. It confirmed the pool arithmetic and found six Medium and seven Low items, all taken into the design above:

| # | Sev. | Finding | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Medium | The topic would show system-wide free RAM to any job given INSPECT on a group, and jobs hold no group handle, so probe 9 could not work | Fixed: free count only on the root group; jobs get their budget in the startup record (items 1, 7, 12) |
| 2 | Medium | At GiB budgets, creation's zeroing and stepped calls hold a CPU for seconds without yielding (§7.3 R3, scheduled for M8) | Fixed in M5: Mike moved M8's yield into M5 (§10.1, item 5) |
| 3 | Medium | With on-demand `mmap`, running out ends the job on touch instead of returning `ENOMEM` | Accepted by Mike, 2026-11-13: Linux-style overcommit (§10.1, §11) |
| 4 | Medium | Small allocations still capped at 4 × 4 MiB | Fixed: arenas sized from the budget, scalable bitmaps (item 10) |
| 5 | Medium | `munmap` returned memory only when the whole object went | Fixed: release on unmap for handle-less on-demand objects (item 4); `ENOMEM` for split exhaustion (item 11) |
| 6 | Medium | Jobs together could starve the residents | Fixed: a reserve and a jobs parent group holding all jobs to free − reserve (§10.1, item 7); the change to the rule accepted by Mike, 2026-11-13 |
| 7 | Low | Saturating to `u32::MAX` means unlimited | Fixed (item 7) |
| 8 | Low | Init's failure behaviour unspecified | Fixed: logged fallback to 16 MiB, never no cap (item 7) |
| 9 | Low | No tool measures the stack | Fixed: stack-size artifact check (item 2) |
| 10 | Low | Lock order, the "same truth" claim, the phase and the topic name were wrong | Fixed (item 1) |
| 11 | Low | `MAPPING_PEAK` overstated; `BOOTFS_ENTRY_PEAK` omitted | Fixed (item 3) |
| 12 | Low | The probes could not prove what the §9 ledger needs, and missed the cap, `munmap` usage, slot exhaustion, over-budget and `status` | Fixed (items 12–16) |
| 13 | Low | Large `MAP_FIXED` stayed eager | Fixed (item 9) |

Risks it raised: re-zeroing never-touched arena pages (fixed, item 10); pages a failed syscall's fill-in committed stay charged until unmapped, as on Linux (accepted by Mike, 2026-11-13); the registry scan per admission grows to 640 slots (already M8's, §9); 64 slots in every region's static storage (kept: per-region sizing is not worth a separate pool now); whether init needs another MemoryObject to read the config (step 2 measures it and adjusts the baseline ledger if so).

### 10.8 Yielding between steps: design (2026-11-13)

From a read-only survey (Claude Opus 5.5, 2026-11-13, at Deepwyrm `b29a611`), checked against the code; paths are under `kernel/src/`, P is `arch/x86_64/mm/activation/primordial.rs`.

**What exists.** A thread switch is a real kernel-context switch (`arch/x86_64/kernel_context.S`; `syscall/live.rs` `switch_kernel_context`): the outgoing thread is suspended inside the kernel on its own stack and resumes there, possibly on another CPU. It happens today only at four shallow gates in `live.rs`: the syscall return loop, `SuspendCurrent` in the trampoline (how a blocking wait parks), the timer pre-IRET gate and the page-fault gate. Interrupts are off for a whole syscall (`syscall/msr.rs` FMASK includes IF), so a quantum that expires mid-call is seen only at the return boundary. The safe points between steps check for a Stop and nothing else.

**Why not switch inside the step loop.** The step loops are methods on the per-CPU facade (`&mut self`, `self.cpu` cached), and the switch code's rule is that the CPU's carrier is never borrowed across a switch (`live.rs` ~1989): after a switch the thread may run on another CPU whose facade another thread holds. The phase reservation (`syscall/stationary_runtime.rs`) and the cached `UserCopyRoot` (`address_space.rs` ~1308) are also tied to one CPU and one hold. A user-visible restart (rewinding RIP with rewritten registers) cannot carry the map's pins, allocator placement, undo-on-failure or create's unzeroed allocation, and would change the ABI.

**Design: yield the way a wait parks** (revised after its review, below).

1. **The yield point.** Before the runtime guard is taken (as the return loop does), the safe point services the quantum deadline; then, as today, it polls Stop, and reads the reschedule request, returning Stop, Yield or Continue. Every step loop uses it: map, unmap and protect; the fill-in; the commit; and `MemoryObjectCreate`'s zeroing, which gains safe points between its steps (so a Stop can land there too). A resumed call always takes one step before it honours a new request, so wake preemption cannot keep it parked without progress.
2. **What is parked: data and one allocation, never a pin or a CPU.** On Yield a loop commits its phase and moves its step state into a per-thread parked-call record: the `RegionSteps` chain (snapshot, undo pieces, progress), the request, and for the fill-in the buffer index. A map or create drops its output pins before parking and pins them again on resume; if the user range is gone by then, the call fails as if its first preflight had, undoing what it made (a map unmaps its steps, a create cancels its backing) and returning `BAD_ADDRESS`. So a parked call holds no pin (pins are a global 32-entry tracker), and no `UserCopyRoot`, phase, journal, usercopy window, CPU index or shootdown, all of which end within a step. The only resource in the record is create's `BackingAllocation`. Nothing is held when the record is filled: each step's holds and shootdown finish before the loop advances.
3. **Create's quota reservation is per thread, not per CPU.** §7.3 R7's reservation (`ObjectRegistry` `reserved`, one slot per CPU) would be overwritten by another create on the same CPU while one is parked, and released by the wrong one after migration. It becomes one slot per thread (the thread-identity capacity), taken and released by the creating thread under T, and counted in usage as now.
4. **Switching.** The trampoline's Yield arm runs the end of dispatch (runnable-work notifications, quantum-cancellation drain), prepares the preemption and switches (the frame was never authorized, so there is nothing to revoke), as the return loop does; on resume it re-reads the carrier, reopens the usercopy window after a fresh entry safe point, and calls the facade's continue method, which takes a fresh phase and `UserCopyRoot` at every safe point (never caching the root across one) and re-enters the call: for the fill-in, the whole dispatch chain from the fill-in on. The frame's return is authorised only when the call finishes, as today; §8.5's binding recheck is the safe point's own.
5. **Ownership and termination.** The record moves between the per-thread table and the running call only under T, the lock termination holds, so a Stop racing a resume is settled once. *As built (changed from the reviewed text, which settled the record in `terminal_cleanup`'s collection and beside `finish_terminal_adapter_resources` on every termination path):* a Thread that ends while parked never runs again (retirement removes a non-running Thread from the run queue in the hold that marks it exited), so its record is settled by a sweep under T in every finalizer drain and before every park: the create's reservation ends and its backing is freed with P beneath T. The sweep covers every termination path at once; settling before each park keeps one table slot per kernel stack (review R-1 below).
6. **Tests.** Host tests drive each loop with a reschedule request raised at every step, a Stop at every step, a Stop at the first safe point after a resume, a resume on another CPU, an output range unmapped while parked, termination of a parked thread through each termination path, and two creates on one CPU with one parked; each checks that no pin, lease, frame, charge or reservation is left and that usage never exceeds the limit. The stack check (§10.2 item 2) covers the Yield arm and the resume path. In the guest, probe 15 stops a job parked mid-call; the two-thread probe on one CPU needs a way to place both threads on one CPU, which step 4 finds or the probe runs on a one-CPU guest.

**Design review (2026-11-13).** Claude Opus 5.5 (default effort), at Deepwyrm `3ffa232`, read-only. Parking at the trampoline was found sound in principle. All items are taken into the design above:

| # | Sev. | Finding | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| D1 | High | Create's quota reservation is per CPU; parking lets another create overwrite it, or release the wrong one after migration, so usage can pass the limit for as long as a call is parked | Fixed: per-thread reservation, settled on termination (items 3, 5) |
| D2 | Medium | After a resume, a Stop at the first safe point would panic on the map's output pin with no user root, or the stale root would be reused | Fixed: no pin is parked and the root is fresh at every safe point (items 2, 4) |
| D3 | Medium | Item 5's first version settled in the execution domain, which cannot take P or reach frame roles; the real precedent is `terminal_cleanup` and `finish_terminal_adapter_resources` | Fixed (item 5) |
| D4 | Low | The map parks no authorization pin; "authorize before switching" was wrong for an unfinished call | Fixed (items 2, 4) |
| R1 | Medium | A Stop racing a resume could be settled twice or never | Fixed: the record moves only under T (item 5) |
| R2 | Low | Wake preemption could keep a call parked without progress | Fixed: one step per resume (item 1) |
| R3 | Low | The deadline service takes the time and scheduler locks | Fixed: serviced before the runtime guard (item 1) |
| R4 | Low | Parked output pins could exhaust the global 32-entry pin tracker across jobs | Fixed: nothing is parked holding a pin (item 2) |
| R5 | Low | Parking lengthens the windows of §7.3 R1 and R5 (another thread maps into found room, or retargets a handle) | No new class: needs MODIFY on the region, as accepted by Mike for §7.3 R1 and R5 (2026-11-13) |
| Q4 | — | No step state may embed a CPU | Stated (item 2) |
| Q6 | — | Dispatch tail, exhaustive matches in the test runtimes, fill-in re-entry, record storage (about 5 KB per thread for `RegionSteps<_, 64>`), stack check of the resume path, placing two threads on one CPU | Taken into items 1–6; the record storage is counted in step 1's static-memory measurement |

**As built (2026-11-14).** Deepwyrm `e764e51`, with `3c100e7` (review R-1); `0f3299c` before them fixed `xtask clippy` under `pinned-cargo host` and four lints in release on unmap. A safe point between steps (`at_step_safe_point`) services the quantum deadline before the runtime guard and reports a reschedule request; the loop then lets its pins go, commits its phase and parks (`ParkedCall`: region steps, commit, fill-in cursor, or a create with its unzeroed allocation). The call returns `YieldCurrent`; the trampoline switches as the return path does, then after a fresh entry safe point calls `continue_parked_call`, which takes the record under T. A resumed map or create pins its outputs again or fails with `BAD_ADDRESS`, undoing what it made (`RegionSteps::abandon`). Zeroing gained safe points; a Stop there frees the backing and ends the reservation. The table holds 32 records (the thread-stack count). Checked: kernel host tests (1,317); `xtask clippy`; `kernel-check` (thread stack 209,576 of 393,216 bytes, reaper 173,024 of 196,608); `dev-boot --check` on one CPU, `--smp` and `--sdk`. A diagnostic build that yields at every allowed safe point (lane `lane/yield-force`, commit `21d7b7c`, not for merge) also passed all three boots: 58 to 97 parks per boot (maps, commits and fill-ins), every one resumed, and 5 to 7 real switches mid-call in the one-CPU and SDK boots. No multi-step create and no termination of a parked Thread ran in those boots.

**Security review (2026-11-14).** Claude Opus 5.5, default effort, at Deepwyrm `e764e51`, read-only. No Critical or High defect. The sweep was found sound, resting on four invariants: a Stop is published in the hold that marks its target exited; an exited scheduler-current Thread spins at its safe point until its Stop arrives; a Thread parking after its safe point acknowledges its Stop only after parking; and retirement removes a non-running Thread from the run queue in the termination hold.

| # | Sev. | Finding | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|
| D-1 | Medium | Item 6's loop tests are missing: the loops live in the target-only facade, so no host test drives them, and no guest probe yet parks a create or ends a parked Thread | Scheduled into step 4 (§10.6), below; needs Mike's acceptance of the scheduling, since a host harness for the facade does not exist |
| D-2 | Low | The plan did not record item 5's change or item 4's absent revoke | Fixed: items 4 and 5 above |
| R-1 | Low | An ended Thread's record could outlive its kernel stack until the reaper's drain, so a 33rd park could panic | Fixed in `3c100e7`: ended records are settled before every park |
| R-2 | — | A wake preemption that keeps a request pending while preemption retains the Thread makes every step park and resume | Costs time only; progress holds (item 1) |
| Q-1 | — | Could a fill-in record be made for a terminal request? | No: `handle` intercepts terminal requests before the fill-in |

**Scheduled for step 4 (review D-1).** Probes 13 and 15 run twice: once on the product kernel and once on the yield-at-every-safe-point diagnostic build (rebuilt from `lane/yield-force` onto the step-4 kernel). Probe 13's eager create larger than one zeroing step parks and resumes its zeroing; probe 15 stops a job while a large map, and separately a large create, is parked. Afterwards the jobs group's usage and init's free count are back to their earlier values (no frame, charge or reservation is left) and the diagnostic reports each parked Thread's record settled. A map whose output page is unmapped by a second thread while the map is parked returns `BAD_ADDRESS` with its steps unmapped. *Done in step 4 (2026-11-14), except the two-thread case, which waits for jobs to have threads (§10.6):* the diagnostic rebuilt onto Deepwyrm `c4cae84` (lane `lane/yield-force-s4`, commit `c530860`, not for merge) passed `dev-boot --check` on one CPU, with `--smp` and with `--sdk`, all memory probes included. Parks (map, commit, fill-in, create) per boot: 591/1676/4900/123, 1114/5174/3644/1174 and 1697/1693/121634/70; in each, resumed plus settled-at-Thread-end equals the parks (7,285 + 5, 11,105 + 1, 125,088 + 6), so no record was left. Jobs stopped mid-map and mid-create in each boot left usage and the free count at their bases.

### 10.9 Release on unmap: design (2026-11-13)

From a read-only survey (Claude Opus 5.5, 2026-11-13, at Deepwyrm `a7be811`), checked against the code; paths are under `kernel/src/`. It refines item 4.

**What the survey changed.**
- **No per-page decommit exists.** The on-demand index (`memory/frame_roles/index.rs`) frees only a whole backing (`release_index`, at finalization).
- **A transaction replaces every lease of its region.** `AddressRegion::rebuild` releases all old mappings' leases and requests one per resulting spec; `PreparedReplace::commit` (`memory/object/lease.rs`) moves pins between them. So "a lease of object X was removed" is not a count of slots: it is the object pages the old leases covered and the new ones do not.
- **"No handle" is not "only leases".** A DMA mapping (`dma_map`) holds an internal reference and keeps committed pages mapped in the IOMMU with no lease. A MapAuthorization or a lookup also holds one. Releasing on "no handle" alone could free frames a device can still write.
- **Settlement runs under T alone on the pending path** (`finish_address_region_mutation` merges the held releases without P), but freeing frames needs P (the frame-role manager).

**Design (revision 4, after the reviews below).**
1. **Removed translations.** While `prepare_replace` plans the transaction (so item 3's coverage pin can be retained while failure is still allowed), and applied at its commit: for each on-demand object, the object pages whose (virtual address, object offset) translation some released lease had and no new lease reproduces are the transaction's removed range for that object. This counts a translation removed even where another lease (an alias) still covers the page (third review, L1); the release still frees only pages no live lease covers. A transaction's range is bounded by its mappings; the hull below is two numbers either way (L3).
2. **Pending range.** Each on-demand object slot in `MemoryObjectAuthority` gains `released: Option<(u64, u64)>`, the hull (lowest start, highest end) of removed ranges not yet released. The commit (T+P) widens it. The hull is cleared only when a release finishes it or the object finalizes, so ranges removed while a handle existed are not forgotten (review F5).
3. **Nothing settles early.** Since Deepwyrm `3e857a1` (review F1) a removed lease's pin is held until its transaction's shootdown settles: `HeldReleases::merge` releases it on the settlement, `settle_locally` at once on paths no other CPU can observe (quiesced teardown, primordial rollback). A removal that only shrinks or splits a lease moves the old pin to the new lease and holds none (second review, B-1), so `prepare_replace` also retains one **coverage pin** for each on-demand object with a removed range that holds no removed lease's pin in this transaction, while it can still fail; commit hands it to `UnmappedLeasePins` with the removed leases' pins, and it is released with them. `rollback` and every error return of `prepare_replace` release coverage pins like its extra pins (never final: the leases are untouched), and the Drop assertion covers them (L2). While any removed translation of an object is unacknowledged, its internal references therefore exceed its live leases' pins, without relying on the global shootdown lock S (L1). Each object needs at most max(its held pins, 1) entries, which is at most its released leases, so `UnmappedLeasePins` keeps its `MAX_MAPPING_BATCH` bound and no frame grows (third review, R1).
4. **Candidates (as built, after Mike's decision below).** The object registry notes every non-final release of a MemoryObject reference that leaves it no handle: a held or coverage pin released on its settlement (`merge`, `settle_locally`, so every teardown path, review F4), a handle close, a DMA unmap, a lookup or authorization released. The notice is the slot index in a ring sized by the registry, with one `queued` flag per slot set on push and cleared only when that entry is popped (second review, B-2): a slot is queued at most once at any time, whatever finalizes or reuses it meanwhile, so the ring cannot overflow, and it is apart from the cleanup queue and its one-final-release-per-slot bound (review F2). A popped slot is judged by whatever occupies it then. Finalization drops the record and its hull, so a new occupant starts empty. Since every release that can make item 5's check pass leaves no handle, it posts a notice; dropping a notice that cannot release now loses nothing.
5. **Release.** The release queue is served where the cleanup queue is drained, which holds T and P, one step per drain. A step runs after the drain's cleanup pops, pops the head slot and clears its flag. If the slot holds no on-demand object whose registry slot is `Live` (a final held-pin release leaves it `Finalizing` until the cleanup queue finalizes it), the step ends: finalization frees everything (L2). Otherwise the step requires that the object is live, on demand, has no handle (`handle_refs == 0`, a new registry accessor) and no internal reference beyond its live leases' pins (no removal in flight, DMA mapping, authorization or lookup). If so, it builds the object's live-lease intervals once, then visits at most 4096 index entries of the hull from the low end, an absent subtree counting as one entry and skipping its span (third review, R2, Q1), and frees each committed page no live lease covers: each is unlinked from the index, its frame freed, and the object's charge lowered. Index nodes stay (they go at finalization). The object's charge falls by checked subtraction in a new registry helper beside `record_charged_pages` (which only adds), lowering the slot's weight, so its TaskGroup's usage, which sums those weights, falls too (L3). The hull's low end moves past the examined index span, covered pages included; a later removal widens it again. If hull remains, the slot is pushed back to the tail with its flag set. If the check fails, the hull is kept for the next candidate.
6. **Why that is safe.** A page is freed only when no live lease covers it, so no PTE maps it after the transactions that removed its leases. Internal references equal to live leases' pins means every such transaction's held pins (its removed leases' and its coverage pin) were released, which happens only on its settlement, so no TLB holds it. No handle, DMA mapping or authorization means nothing can map, commit or DMA it again except through a live lease, whose faults commit fresh zeroed pages. Commits through a handle (`memory_object_commit`, `dma_map`'s fill-in) cannot happen without a handle, and no path makes a handle from an internal reference. The check is repeated at every step.
7. **Kept, not released:** an object with a handle keeps its pages; so does one with a DMA mapping, an in-flight authorization or a lookup. When the last of those goes, its release posts a notice (item 4) and the next drain releases what no live lease covers. *Mike (2026-11-13): candidacy at last handle close and DMA unmap is built (option 2), rather than accepting the wait until the next removal or finalization.* The remaining delay is one drain: retention of the charge, never a use of a freed frame.
8. **Cost.** A step visits at most 4096 index entries in one hold. *As built,* finding the uncovered runs rescans the lease pool for each cover and gap it passes, at most `LEASES` × (the object's leases + gaps) slot checks, about 10^5 at production pools; and one drain may drop up to the registry's capacity of notices that cannot release now, each a lookup over the object and lease pools (about 4.5 × 10^5 checks). Both are bounded; whether they matter is measured with the rest of M5's latency at step 5's exit, where building the intervals once per step is the change if it does (code review L3). A release spans as many steps as its hull needs; the hull can span the whole object (review F3: pages committed through the handle before it closed count too), which is why the release is stepped rather than bounded per transaction.
9. **Tests (host).** Two leases on one object over the same pages, unmapped by two transactions with interleaved settlements (the first settlement queues nothing that can pass the check; the second frees the pages); a partial unmap under a live overlapping lease (nothing freed); a partial unmap with nothing overlapping (the removed pages freed and uncharged, the rest kept); a handle closed after the unmap, then another lease of the object unmapped (the first hull released too); an object with a DMA mapping and no handle (kept); a candidate whose object finalized first (dropped); a hull larger than one step (released over several drains, the flag set until the last); duplicate candidates (one queue entry); a partial unmap (shrink) of a handle-less object's lease, with a queued candidate stepped while that unmap's shootdown is pending (nothing freed until it settles, then the removed pages); a queued slot finalized and reused before its pop (one entry, judged against the new occupant, whose candidacy is not lost); a step on a slot left `Finalizing` by a process exit holding the last lease (ends, no fail stop); an alias in one region whose translation is removed while another lease covers the page (coverage pin held, nothing freed until both are gone); a rolled-back transaction (coverage pins released, none final); teardown of one of two processes mapping a handle-less object, through each teardown path (the dying process's exclusive pages freed). Each checks the charge, the frame count and that no page a live lease covers changed.

**Review (Claude Opus 5.5, independent, read-only, 2026-11-13, of revision 1 at Deepwyrm `e7a5586`).** Mechanism sound against the code given the global shootdown lock S and per-address-space serialization; build after F1–F3.
- **F1 (High, existing code, not §10.9):** `PreparedReplace::commit` released removed leases' pins before the shootdown's acknowledgement; only a final release was held. A handle close elsewhere could then finalize the object and free frames a remote TLB still mapped, breaking H0 §12.6. Confirmed (`drain_finalizers` does not wait for a pending shootdown). **Fixed** in Deepwyrm `3e857a1`: the pins are held in `HeldReleases` until the settlement; regression test `a_family_g_unmap_holds_its_final_release_until_merged` fails on the old commit. The reviewer's extension to `dma_unmap` does not hold: its VT-d IOTLB invalidation drains and polls to completion before the pin returns (`arch/x86_64/vtd.rs` `iotlb_command`). Lookup pins need nothing more: with lease pins held, no lookup release can be the last while a translation is unacknowledged.
- **F2 (Medium):** release requests had no type or capacity on the cleanup queue, whose bound assumes one final release per registry slot, and were not deduplicated. **Fixed in design:** item 4's flagged queue, sized by the pool.
- **F3 (Low):** the per-release cost bound was false (a hull can span the whole object). **Fixed in design:** item 5 steps the release, item 8 restates the cost.
- **F4 (Low):** two teardown paths were not covered. **Fixed in design:** item 4.
- **F5 (Low):** the hull was cleared even when the release did not run, and "one interval per old lease" was wrong with aliases. **Fixed in design:** item 2 keeps the hull; item 1 no longer claims it.
- The reviewer's restatement of why in-flight state matters (a request queued earlier, drained during this transaction's pending shootdown) is now carried by item 3's held pins rather than a counter.

**Second review (Claude Opus 5.5, independent, read-only, 2026-11-13, of `3e857a1` and revision 2 at Deepwyrm `bc09bd6`).** The F1 fix is sound: every production commit of a replace routes removed-lease pins through `HeldReleases` or a provably local `settle_locally`; `merge` is reached on every path; capacity cannot overflow. Revision 2 was not ready to build:
- **B-1 (High, design):** a removal that only shrinks or splits a lease holds no pin, so internal references equal live leases during its pending shootdown and a queued candidate's step could free its pages; such an unmap also produced no candidate. **Fixed in design:** item 3's coverage pin.
- **B-2 (Medium, design):** the dedup flag and queue had no lifecycle across finalization and slot reuse (overflow, or a lost candidacy). **Fixed in design:** item 4's slot-indexed queue.
- **Question (primordial retirement on SMP), resolved:** each CPU's kernel execution root is a private PML4 whose low half stays empty (`arch/x86_64/mm/activation/address_space.rs` `KernelExecutionRoot`), the kernel uses no PCID or global user pages, and the primordial Process is quiesced first, so no other CPU can hold a primordial user translation at its teardown; `settle_locally` there is sound.
- **Note (primordial map, Low):** construction's map asserts its releases are empty, which now also counts held pins; every primordial segment and the stack is its own object mapped once, so none is held. Kernel-driven, unreachable from user input; left as is, since a held pin there would be a construction bug and the assertion fails stop on it.
- Item 7's delay is retention only, not a hazard, but can last the object's life if no later removal happens.

**Third review (Claude Opus 5.5, independent, read-only, 2026-11-13, of revision 3 at Deepwyrm `93e1496`).** No Critical, High or Medium defect; ready to build once its Lows are in the design. Checked and held: every lease removal goes through `PreparedReplace::commit`; no other path removes coverage (protect requires READ, map refuses overlap, a region cannot finalize with mappings, termination uses the teardown paths); the release check is exact (one pin per lease, every other holder raises a counter); faults, handle commits and DMA fill-in resolve through live leases or handles in each hold; user pins block removal; stepping misses nothing; the slot queue's lifecycle is sound; charging is consistent. Lows, all **fixed in design** (revision 4): L1, an alias in one region could lose a translation with no pin held, safe today only through the global lock S (item 1 counts removed translations, item 3 holds the coverage pin); L2, rollback must release coverage pins, and a step must require a `Live` registry slot or it fails stop on a `Finalizing` one, reachable by a process exit (items 3 and 5); L3, wording (items 1 and 5). R1 (stack) taken: coverage pins only for objects with no held lease pin, keeping the 64 bound. R2 and Q1 taken (item 5). Q2 is item 7.



**As built (Deepwyrm `c7daf0e`, review fixes `ef4326b`; 2026-11-13).** Revision 4 plus Mike's candidacy decision (item 4 is now the registry's notice ring rather than a memory-side queue). The region computes removed translations (`removed_translations`, `memory/address_region/region.rs`); `prepare_replace` widens each on-demand record's hull and retains coverage pins (`memory/object/lease.rs`); the registry posts notices (`object/mod.rs`); `MemoryObjectAuthority::release_unmapped_step` (`memory/object.rs`) and `FrameRoleManager::decommit_on_demand_range` (`memory/frame_roles/index.rs`) release; the live drain runs one step after the cleanup pops (`primordial.rs` `release_unmapped_step`). `xtask kernel-check`: thread stack 209,384 of 393,216 usable bytes (was 201,344), reaper unchanged at 173,024. Host tests in `arch/x86_64/mm/journal/release_tests.rs`, `object/tests.rs` and `memory/address_region/tests.rs`; removing the coverage pin, its rollback release or the L1 fix each fails one.

**Code review (Claude Opus 5.5, independent, read-only, 2026-11-13, of `c7daf0e` with `3e857a1`).** No Critical, High or Medium defect: no path frees a page a live lease, DMA mapping, handle or unacknowledged translation can reach; the coverage pin and 64-entry bound hold; the release check is exact; the charge subtraction cannot underflow; freed frames are reused only through zeroing paths, as at finalization. Lows:
- **L1 (fixed):** `removed_translations` computed pages by subtracting a wrapping delta, which panics with overflow checks when a mapping's object offset exceeds its address (release builds wrapped correctly). Now computed forward from the offset, checked; test `removed_translations_follow_the_offset_even_above_the_address`.
- **L2 (fixed where host-reachable):** added a hull released over several drains with requeue, two unmaps of the same pages settling in turn, a protect that removes nothing, and a two-level absent-node skip. The three teardown paths reach candidacy through `settle_locally` and the registry hook, which the host tests exercise; the paths themselves run only in the live kernel, which `dev-boot --check` boots and exits processes on. A lookup or authorization blocks the release through the same internal count as the DMA-style test.
- **L3 (scheduled):** item 8's cost, measured at M5 step 5.
- Plan text brought up to date (items 4, 7, 8).

### 10.10 Queued, stepped teardown of an exited root: design (2026-11-14)

Mike's decision of §10.6's step-4 findings. From a read-only survey (Claude Opus 5.5, 2026-11-14, at Deepwyrm `7b0f701`), checked against the code; P is `arch/x86_64/mm/activation/primordial.rs`.

**What exists.** Five paths tear an exited Process's root down at once, through `attempt_exited_root_teardown` (P ~5713): `complete_task_group_termination`, `complete_process_termination` and `complete_thread_termination` on the terminator's kernel stack, and `finish_terminal_successor` and `continue_terminal_after_primordial_root` on the terminal reaper stack (a Process's own exit, and a CPU leaving an exited root, design §11.2.12 rule 3). Each holds T, takes P for `unmap_inactive_userspace`, which removes every mapping in one transaction per mapping, then `finish_quiesced_process_root_retirement` (S beneath P, `retire_quiesced_root`, the root pin's release, `drain_finalizers`). None may fail (a failure panics). A root with a resident CPU (`LeftToResident`) is not recorded anywhere: the resident tears it down when it leaves. The boot paths (`finish_terminal_teardown`, `RetirePrimordialToIdle`, `unmap_primordial_userspace`) tear down boot images, which are bounded, and are not changed. Waiters see `EXITED` before the teardown (`collect_retired_pins`), but cannot run until the terminator lets T go.

**Design (revision 4, after three reviews below and step 4's runs).**
1. **Owed, not done.** Where the five paths decide `TearDown` today, they instead queue the Process as *teardown owed* and return: a `SlotQueue` (`object/slot_queue.rs`) indexed by the Process's registry slot, holding the full `ProcessKey` (slot and generation), one flag per slot, so a Process is queued at most once and the queue cannot overflow. **The bootstrap (primordial) Process is never queued** (second review C2): its teardown stays synchronous on every path, as do the child branch of `complete_primordial_launch` (first review D3) and the boot paths; all are bounded by boot images. `LeftToResident` is unchanged: the resident's leaving reaches the same decision and queues. Termination's status, its `EXITED` wakes and its reply are unchanged.
2. **Where a step may run** (first review D1, second review C1). A step reuses `unmap_inactive_userspace`'s machinery, which opens a page-table mutation session on the CPU's active root. It runs where that root is either a Process root whose Process is not EXITED (syscall returns, between family-G steps, the BSP's timer gate when it interrupted such a Process, a page fault's return, item 7), or the primordial publisher root once the bootstrap has exited and its userspace is gone (the idle visit, item 5; the reaper already tears down from that root). Elsewhere the drain skips the stage and keeps the queue. An entry is never dropped for being skipped. *Revision 4 (third review D-2, R-2):* a step also needs an **allowance**, a per-CPU flag (`step_report.rs`) set at the timer gate when it interrupted user code, at a page fault refused for memory (item 7), when a syscall returns `NO_MEMORY` or `NO_RESOURCES` (its return drain is item 7's syscall arm), at the BSP's idle-suspend poll and in the idle visit; the step consumes it. Every other drain (syscall entry, step safe points, the reschedule check, the terminal handoff) skips the stage, so one entry from user mode takes at most one step. Syscall entry grants none: when it did, init paid for every dead job's teardown and missed the shell's deadline.
3. **One step.** The step pops the head entry and revalidates it: a `ProcessKey` whose slot was finalized or reused (`process_info` fails) or whose root is already retired is dropped (second review R-e); a CPU resident on its root, requeued (its leaving queues it anyway); the CPU's own current Process, requeued; otherwise EXITED, quiesced and bound are asserted, since nothing else changes a queued Process. It then removes at most 2 MiB (`FAMILY_G_STEP_BYTES`; 16 MiB before revision 4, see below) of the lowest remaining mapping with `region.unmap(start, len)` under P (a front shrink of one spec; no large pages, so no table split), settles locally (no resident, no PCID) and requeues at the tail. A step that finds no mapping retires the root (S beneath P, `retire_quiesced_root`, the root pin's release) and does not requeue; since the primordial is never queued, the publisher root is never retired by a step. Everything is rebuilt each step from the key; nothing is held between steps. A step costs the 2 MiB unmap plus one region rebuild, O(mappings + leases) (first review R5). *Revision 4 (third review Q-3):* the "own current Process" case is an assertion, since a queued Process has exited and the current one has not.
   *Step size (revision 4, step 4's runs).* A family-G step and a teardown step hold T throughout. At 16 MiB a step held T for about 30 ms under QEMU TCG; with four CPUs, a job mapping 256 MiB in a loop kept init's few syscalls queued behind it long enough to miss wyrmsh's one-second deadline (the `memory` probe right after `spawn churn-map`; lock timing in a diagnostic lane showed no wait over 75 ms, but a wait of about 30 ms on nearly every T acquisition). At 2 MiB the probes pass and a 256 MiB map runs about twice as fast (640 to 320 ms). The journal batch stays 16 MiB; the step is an eighth of it.
4. **No recursion, bounded stacks** (first review Q2, R3; second review R-f). The step's retirement is its own function that pushes its releases onto the cleanup queue; `finish_quiesced_process_root_retirement` keeps its drain for the synchronous paths. `drain_finalizers` runs the stage once, after `release_unmapped_step`, then pops the cleanup queue until it is empty again. The step is `#[inline(never)]`. `xtask kernel-check` names `native_runtime_idle_scheduler` as the target of the indirect idle-scheduler edge, so the reaper-into-idle path and its drains are bounded, and must pass.
5. **Progress when idle** (first review D1; second review C1, R-c, Q-b). Once the bootstrap has exited and its userspace is gone, an idle BSP with teardown owed makes a visit before it halts: it switches from the kernel root to the primordial publisher root with the reaper's switch (`prepare_terminal_primordial_root_switch`), takes one step, and switches back, keeping the idle bookkeeping (`physically_executing` idle, `last_scheduled` the primordial identity) the reaper's `FinishGenericChild` sets; then it opens an interrupt window (enabling interrupts briefly) before the next visit, or halts when nothing is owed. *Revision 4 (third review D-1, R-1):* one visit per pass of the idle loop, which goes back through the timer service and `schedule_next_on` between visits; the visit uses `on_root_switch_flight` and the guard-free execute, as `terminate_current` does. While teardown is owed the loop does not halt: it opens a one-instruction window (`sti; nop; cli`), services the rendezvous latch and goes round. The same holds where the BSP idles in place on a blocked Thread's live root (idle-suspend, which D-1 found missing): its runtime answers `idle_work_remains`, and the suspend loop then opens the window and polls again instead of halting. A self-IPI to end the coming halt was tried first and cannot work: the local APIC driver refuses its own ID as a destination, and the boot panicked under `--smp`. No visit happens while the bootstrap still has userspace, so the visit can never be resident on a root another CPU is tearing down. Jobs start only after init runs, and the bootstrap exits as it hands off to init, so this costs no progress for jobs; a service that ended before that is stepped at syscall returns. *Corrected 2026-11-15 (§10.19): the last clause is no longer what the code does. An ordinary syscall return takes no teardown step. A step needs the per-CPU allowance, which is granted only at the BSP's user-mode timer gate, at a fault refused for memory while work is owed, at a syscall that returns `NO_MEMORY` or `NO_RESOURCES`, at the BSP's suspend-idle poll and at the idle visit; every syscall entry ends a leftover allowance. A service that ended before the bootstrap's exit is stepped at those points.*
6. **The end of the boot** (first review D2; second review R-a, R-g). Power-off needs nothing: it calls none of `complete_primordial_launch`'s checks and ends the machine. `complete_primordial_launch` (reached only when the bootstrap is the last Process, never in a WYR1 product) first finishes the owed queue on the primordial root it holds; no Process is resident anywhere then, since all have exited, so every entry is steppable and the loop ends. *Revision 4 (third review R-4): not built.* That function runs on the primordial's own root while its userspace is still mapped, where item 2 forbids a step, and another CPU may still be leaving a child root. With teardown owed it reaches its existing active-lease error instead. It is never reached in a WYR1 product.
7. **A touch frees owed memory first; syscalls refuse** (first review R1, R2; second review C3, R-b, R-d; Mike, 2026-11-14). A page fault refused for `MEMORY_PAGES` quota or for frames, while teardown is owed, takes one teardown step on the faulting Process's root and returns to the faulting instruction, so the fault comes again (a preemption point each time) and a touch ends its job only when nothing is owed. No loop guard remains in the gate since §8.10, so a re-fault is not terminal. A syscall refused for `MEMORY_PAGES` or object quota or for frames, and a `ProcessCreate` or `TaskGroupCreate` that finds its pool full, take one teardown step and then fail exactly as today: one step, never a loop, so no syscall holds the CPU for more than one step for it. Other classes (`WAIT_CLASS`) are untouched. Init, after a job it ended has exited, does not start another job until the jobs group's usage has stopped falling, without blocking its other requests. *Revision 4 (Mike, 2026-11-14: a bounded park):* wyrmsh waits one second for a launch reply, and tearing down a full 512 MiB budget at 2 MiB a step takes about that long under TCG, so the pause is bounded. After reaping a job it ended (classification authorized or task-group teardown), init reads the jobs group's usage and, until a read 20 ms or more later is not lower or 500 ms have passed, reads no new request from any session; peer closes, reaping, parked launches and WAIT replies go on (`system-init/src/job_memory.rs` `Settle`). The fault arm (third review R-3) runs the whole `drain_finalizers` after the fault's session drops, and returns the new outcome `UserPageFaultOutcome::Released`, which the gate treats as a return to the instruction. A fault can be put off for as long as other Processes keep exiting; each retry is preemptible, and the exits are made by holders of launch rights (accepted with item 7's choice).
8. **Shootdown lock S and fairness** (first review R4, R6). No drain site holds S (`finish_address_region_mutation` releases S before its drain), so a retirement's wait for S ends; S's spin services acknowledgements while it waits. A dead job's teardown is paid by others' syscall returns, faults and timer ticks, one step at a time, bounded in total by its size; that latency is measured with the rest of M5's at step 5.
9. **What stays visible.** A Process with teardown owed is EXITED and its waiters are woken as now; its usage falls step by step. Nothing tells userspace that a teardown has finished: probe 15 and init poll the memory topic.
10. **Safety** (checked by both reviews). Between steps the Process is EXITED and quiesced, so every live mutation of its root is refused and no CPU can become resident; the user-pin assertion runs every step; parked calls of the dead Process are settled earlier in the same drain; a step's unmap is one ordinary transaction. Second review Q-a: a step at a family-G safe point or the timer gate selects the dead Process for return validation and restores the current Process before it returns, under P, and §10.8 takes a fresh user root after every safe point, so no cached root crosses it.
11. **Tests.** Host: the queue (`slot_queue.rs`), and a partial-unmap step on a region with several mappings, a handle-held object and an alias, checking lease generations and charges after each step. *Revision 4:* the partial-unmap host test is not built: the step (`unmap_inactive_pieces`) runs only in the live facade, which has no host harness. The guest probes cover it (13 and 15 end jobs holding 64 and 256 MiB and require usage back exactly). Whether step 5 needs a host harness for it is decided in step 5's review. Guest (probe 15): terminate jobs holding 64 MiB and 256 MiB and require the reply within the shell's deadline and usage and free back to their bases; a job whose last Thread exits on its own with a large mapping, on one CPU with nothing else runnable (the idle visit); a job that touches its whole budget right after a 256 MiB kill (item 7); the step-2 stall case three times; the step report's `teardown` steps; `--smp` for S.

**Third design review (Claude Opus 5.5, default effort, 2026-11-14, of revision 3 at Deepwyrm `c9e8f68`, read-only).** C1, C2, R-c, R-e, R-f, R-g, Q-a and Q-b of the second review were found resolved, R-a partly (R-4), C3 not (D-2).

| # | Sev. | Finding | Disposition |
|---|---|---|---|

Kernel host tests (1,327) and `xtask clippy` pass. `dev-boot --check` passes on one CPU (longest step 8.2 ms, a map) and with `--smp` (longest 48.9 ms, a fill-in step, which keeps its own size); the step report shows Stops inside a map, a create and commits.

No Critical, High or Medium defect was confirmed in either.

The stage spends the allowance first; a syscall entry ends a leftover one; only the BSP's timer gate grants |

With 16 sessions polled one per tick, a client's turn can still come about 400 ms after a pause ends: step 6's latency measurement |

Its older half stands: `MAP_FIXED` over a partly mapped range fails with `EEXIST`. Scheduled (Mike, 2026-11-14): the `MAP_FIXED` row of root `TOOLCHAIN_POSIX_LAYER_DESIGN.md` §11, step 3 |
