So the BSP with a hog on it already takes about four steps per quantum, the floor item 1 asked for, and "one step per tick" (the coordinator's reading above, and review R5's wording) is not what kept finalization owed for 2.9 s. Also established: a quantum is armed on every return to user mode whether or not another Thread is runnable, so the BSP's gate does run once per 5 ms; an AP's gate drains nothing; a kernel-origin timer interrupt does not reach the gate. And two limits of the figures: `owed-longest-us` is the longest time the queue stayed non-empty, not one object's wait, so jobs ended back to back can hold it up while every object finishes quickly; and a finalize step taken while another step is being timed on its CPU is not counted (`timed_nested`), so `call-steps finalize` is a lower bound.

**Revision 2: measure what a bound would bound, then decide (2026-11-15).** The cause of the 2.9 s is not known, so no bound is designed yet. First the `[steps]` report gains what tells the cases apart, as lasting report lines, not a diagnostic lane:
1. *One object's wait:* the longest time from an on-demand object's finalization being queued to its completion (`finalize-wait-longest-us`), with the pages it freed.
2. *Starvation:* the longest time between two finalize steps while the queue was non-empty, and from an object being queued to the first step after it (`finalize-gap-longest-us`).
3. *Where steps come from:* finalize steps counted whether or not another step is being timed, split by what drained: the BSP's timer gate, a syscall return, a fault, an idle loop.
4. No change to when or how many steps are taken, to the kernel's locking, or to the ABI. Host tests for the report's arithmetic where it is portable.
Then one boot on one CPU and twenty `--smp`, each reporting the new lines beside `owed-longest-us`. If the longest wait of one object and the longest gap are both short (the 2.9 s being the queue refilled by back-to-back jobs), the finding is the metric and nothing needs a bound; if a gap is long, the report says which drain was missing, and the bound is designed for that and brought to Mike before it is built.

**Revision 2 as built and measured (2026-11-15).** Deepwyrm `3d59c89` and `0ade84d` (merged `03e3a9d`), by the worker (Claude Opus 5.5) in lane `lane/m5s8-finalize-floor`; reviewed and checked by the coordinator at `0ade84d`: kernel host tests (1,388), `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236, 87), `kernel-check`, `fmt`. Report bookkeeping only: nothing about when or how many steps are taken, the locking or the ABI changed.
- *New `[steps]` lines.* `finalize-wait-longest-us=… pages=…` (one object, queueing to completion); `finalize-gap-longest-us` (the longest time with finalization owed and no step running, an open gap at the report included); `finalize-steps gate=… syscall=… fault=… idle=… other=…` (every step, by a per-CPU tag set at each kernel entry and resume point; `syscall` includes the BSP's drains at syscall safe points); `finalize-step-longest-us` (over every step, from before P is taken). The clock is the report's own (`monotonic_now`); the state is relaxed atomics written under T and a per-CPU tag. Wyrmroot's `dev-boot` forwards the lines unchanged.
- *The open slot.* All five stepped loops (region steps, the fill-in's two loops, commit, create's zeroing) left `started[cpu]` open at their normal exit; `step_report::call_ended` now runs at every exit. Each call's last step is now counted, so `call-steps` and `call-longest-us` for map, unmap, protect, fill, commit and create are not comparable with earlier records (one CPU: map 347 to 658 steps). `stops-between-steps`, `parked-ended` and the owed figures do not use the slot.
- *Tests (host).* The wait, gap and step bookkeeping over scripted sequences; the slot closed at a call's end, a last step counted, a parked call resumed on another CPU counted once (four fail without the change); a step through the real authority reporting each object's slot, frames and completion. The tags and the call sites have no host harness.

*Measured (development tier, QEMU TCG, Wyrmroot `b0d76c5`, host load 2.8 to 7.0; 32 boots, all passing, no panic).* At `3d59c89`: one on one CPU and twenty `--smp` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794759152272144222` to `…0730427181054`). At `0ade84d`: one and ten (`…1296109550934` to `…2011322837063`).

| | One CPU | `--smp` |
|---|---|---|
| One object's longest wait (always a whole 512 MiB budget) | 0.17 s | 0.20 to 0.39 s |
| Longest gap between finalize steps while owed | 5.3 ms | 2.2 to 7.5 ms |
| `owed-longest-us finalize` | 0.18 s | 0.20 to 0.89 s |
| Finalize steps in a boot | 1,589 to 1,644 | 3,571 to 6,246 |
| Longest finalize step, over all steps (`0ade84d`) | 0.79 ms | 1.06 to 2.52 ms |
| Longest step of any call (`0ade84d`) | 5.1 ms (map) | 8.9 to 12.5 ms (map; unmap once) |

- *No starvation, and one object is quick.* A whole budget finalizes in 0.4 s at most, about one step a millisecond, most from syscall returns and the BSP's gate next.
- *The long owed stretches are overlap.* In the three boots over 0.4 s (0.89, 0.57, 0.45 s) the longest single wait was 0.37, 0.24 and 0.38 s: each object finished in ordinary time and the queue did not empty between them. `owed-longest-us` is therefore not a latency; `finalize-wait-longest-us` is.
- *The 2.9 s boot is not explained.* It did not recur in 30 `--smp` boots. Overlap fits it (about eight budgets back to back) and so would the case below; neither is shown.

**What a bound would bound (read-only check by the worker, `0ade84d`; §9).** Nothing wakes the BSP when an owed finalization or teardown is queued: `defer_finalization`, `drain_cleanup_queue` and `queue_owed_teardown` publish no wake, and `publish_bsp_service_wake` is called only for the q35 checks and when an AP changes a deadline. A BSP halted idle is then woken only by chance (a runnable-work wake that picks it) or by its hardware arm: the earliest wait or Timer deadline, else the clock's maintenance arm. Meanwhile only syscall returns and refused faults on other CPUs step; an AP's gate and an idle AP do not. Once awake the BSP steps without halting until the queue is empty, in both idle loops. Nothing was found against an idle AP taking finalize steps (`idle_finalize_step` assumes nothing of the BSP); not tested.

**Revision 3. Approved by Mike, 2026-11-15 ("build the wake"); built the same day with a narrower rule than item 1 (as built, below).**
1. *The wake.* A hold on a CPU other than the BSP that queues an owed finalization or an owed teardown publishes the BSP's wake, once for the hold however many it queued. A BSP halted idle then wakes, finds `idle_work_remains` true and steps without halting until the queues are empty, as it does today once awake. On a BSP that is not idle the wake changes nothing: its gate, its syscall returns and its idle loops drain already. The BSP's own queueing needs none.
2. *The bound.* After a queueing the next step is taken within the wake's delivery plus the BSP's way to its next drain: at once when it is idle, within a quantum when it is in user code, at its return when it is in the kernel (the longest step of any call measured is 12.5 ms). So the gap is bounded by about a quantum plus one step in every state, and `finalize-gap-longest-us` shows it.
3. *Report.* `[steps]` counts the wakes published for owed work, so a boot shows the path ran. The teardown step's timing no longer overwrites an open slot of another call (§9): both steps are recorded.
4. *Not changed.* How many steps a drain takes, the AP gates, whether an idle AP steps (it still does not), the allowance, the ABI.
5. *Tests (host).* Whether a hold publishes the wake, as a pure function of the CPU and of what the hold queued; the teardown step's timing inside another call's step. The wake's delivery and the idle loops' reaction are target-only: boots, where the wake count and the gap line are the evidence. No boot forces a halted BSP at the moment of queueing; said as such.

*To be established by the worker before it builds (stop if one fails):* which primitive wakes a halted BSP without asking it for timer service, and that it may be published from the queueing holds (what they hold, and the lock order against what the primitive takes); that both idle loops, once woken by it, reach `idle_work_remains` and step rather than halt again; that a wake arriving just before the BSP halts is not lost (the idle commit's rescan); and what the wake costs a BSP that is busy.

**Revision 3 as built (2026-11-15).** Deepwyrm `b72eb02`, `fec1c26` and `9032966`, all signed, the first two by one Opus worker and the third by another; each reviewed by the coordinator, who re-ran the checks. Main was fast-forwarded to `9032966` and the lane retired. Wyrmroot `b0d76c5` and the SDK `56eb579` are unchanged; `abi/` and `crates/` are untouched, so their pin stays (§10.18).

- *The rule is narrower than item 1.* `b72eb02` woke the BSP for every hold off it that queued owed work. The coordinator's review found that this lets a job on another CPU interrupt the BSP at the rate of its own syscalls. As built (`fec1c26`), a hold off the BSP wakes it once, at its end, in two cases only: it queued into a queue of owed finalization or owed teardown that was empty, and that queue is still non-empty when the hold ends; or it retired the bootstrap's root and teardown is owed when the hold ends.
- *Why that is enough.* The BSP halts only when it has nothing it can step. Both idle loops go round without halting while finalization is owed. The suspend-idle loop also goes round while teardown is owed. The main idle loop halts with teardown owed only while the bootstrap's root is not yet retired, because its visit refuses until then (§10.10 item 5); the second case covers the moment that changes. So a queue that was already non-empty is either keeping the BSP awake or cannot be stepped by it, and needs no wake. The BSP's own holds need none: any interrupt ends its halt and both loops go back to their check.
- *What it costs a job to cause a wake.* A drain takes its finalize step in the hold that queued (`drain_finalizers`), so an object of at most one step (512 entries) is finished within the hold and sends nothing. Each wake therefore needs an object of more than one step, or a process exit.
- *Mechanism.* One byte per CPU, `OwedHoldState` (`kernel/src/syscall/hold_end.rs`), with the pure decision `owed_work_wake`. A hold marks events in it: `note_owed_finalizations` when the queue held nothing, `queue_owed_teardown` when the queue was empty, `finish_quiesced_process_root_retirement` for the primordial Process. `check_hold_end` settles them under T against what is owed then. `HoldEndCheck::after_release`, a new hook the guard calls after it releases T, delivers the wake: a Wake in the BSP's mailbox and the rendezvous IPI. The mailbox publication is an unconditional store, so it cannot be refused by a busy mailbox, and the IPI is always sent.
- *A wake that could not be sent stays pending* (`9032966`; coordinator's review of `fec1c26`). Under the from-empty rule nothing else would retry it: the queue stays non-empty, so no later hold marks it. The sending CPU's next hold end tries again. Each failed attempt is counted once as unsent.
- *A wake just before the halt is not lost.* An IPI taken before the idle commit leaves the latch that makes `commit_current_idle` return `RescanRequired`, and both loops then go round; one still pending at `sti; hlt` ends the halt. A BSP that is not idle only latches it.
- *Nothing else off the BSP turns its idle checks true* (worker's reading, checked in review). The checks read the two queues and the bootstrap's root region. The queues are pushed only by `defer_finalization` (seen through `note_owed_finalizations`), by `queue_owed_teardown`, and by the teardown step's requeue, which puts back an entry the step took in the same hold and marks nothing: the queue was already non-empty when that hold began. The root region is cleared by `finish_quiesced_process_root_retirement` (marked), by `retire_owed_root` (never the primordial), and by `finish_terminal_teardown`, which is the end of the system and completes every owed finalization itself.
- *Cost.* Every hold end now looks up the current CPU twice, once under T and once after the release (a read of `IA32_GS_BASE` each), and loads the byte. Not measured on its own; the step lines of the boots below are within the earlier spread.
- *Report.* `[steps]` has `owed-wakes=N unsent=M queue=Q retirement=R`. A teardown step taken inside another call's timed step keeps that call's start, and both are recorded (§9).
- *Tests (host).* The decision as a 13-row table, which fails against `b72eb02`'s rule. Six tests that take `OwedHoldState` through whole holds (mark, settle, take, put back); they fail with the put-back made a no-op and with the retirement cause dropped. The report's counts. The nested teardown timing. No host harness reaches the per-CPU statics, the hold-end call or the send.
- *Checks (coordinator, at `9032966`).* `host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` exit 0 with 1,397 passed; `xtask clippy` exit 0 with the ratchet unchanged (236 and 87); `xtask kernel-check` passed; `fmt --check` clean.
- *Boots (development tier, QEMU TCG, not hardware times).* At `fec1c26`, by the worker: one on one CPU and ten with `--smp` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794764868869923523` to `…5578827511364`), host load 1.3 to 14.6. All passed with no panic. The `--smp` boots sent 20 to 49 wakes each (21 to 63 under `b72eb02`'s rule), the one-CPU boot none; the longest gap between finalize steps was 6.0 ms, and the longest wait of one object 0.38 s. At `9032966`: the worker's one-CPU and `--smp` boots (`…8034851354736`, `…8110655095909`) and the coordinator's `--smp` boot of the merged kernel (`…8326605431790`, host load 13: 39 wakes, gap 5.2 ms) passed with no panic. Every boot had `unsent=0` and `retirement=0`.
- *Not shown.* No boot has the BSP halted at the moment work is queued, so no boot shows the wake shortening a gap: the bound of item 2 rests on the reading above and on the host tests. The retirement wake and the retry of an unsent wake ran in no boot. No probe drops small on-demand objects off the BSP, so the rate rule's effect is not measured. The 2.9 s boot (§10.18) is still unexplained; the case this closes would fit it and is not shown to be it. Whether a probe is built for the first of these is Mike's decision at M5's close (§9). *The first of these was measured the same day: the probe, below.*

**The probe (Mike, 2026-11-15: "Build the probe").** Wyrmroot `243c074`, `b84044e` and `1dc2efe`, signed, by an Opus worker; reviewed by the coordinator, who re-ran the checks (`xtask test fmt`, `clippy`, `unit`: exit 0, 1,233 unit tests) and one `--smp` boot. Main was fast-forwarded to `1dc2efe` and the lane retired. The triple is Deepwyrm `9032966` (code), Wyrmroot `1dc2efe`, SDK `56eb579`.

- *What it does.* `hello close-spin` commits a 512 MiB on-demand object (the commit stops at the job's quota, 131,071 pages), calibrates a user-mode loop against the clock, closes the object, and spins half a second with no syscall and no fault; then it prints the time it spun and the APIC ID before and after. `dev-boot --check` runs it three times before the CPU hogs start, with usage settled before each run, so each close finds the queue empty and the BSP is idle unless the job is on it.
- *What the check requires.* The kernel's report must have the `owed-wakes` line, with `unsent=0` and at least one wake for each run that stayed on one AP. The longest gap is printed, not bounded: a bound of half the spin was built first and could not fail for a missing wake (below), and a threshold near 10 ms would be thin on a loaded host.
- *Measured (development tier, QEMU TCG, host load 3 to 8).* Longest gap between finalize steps over the boot:

| Kernel | `--smp` boots | Longest gap | One CPU |
|---|---|---|---|
| With the wake, `9032966` | 6 with a 1 s spin (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794771149307209265` to `…1587824420995`), 3 with 0.5 s (`…3338863854730`, `…3422184282605`, and the coordinator's `…3695962571625`) | 4.4 to 6.8 ms | 5.2 and 5.3 ms |
| Without, `fac8f86` | 6 with a 1 s spin (`…1757374557977` to `…2197297387005`) | 14.5 to 22.2 ms | 5.5 ms |

  Every one of these boots passed with no panic. In a boot of the three runs alone, with the wake, the gap was 1.5 ms (`…3099453926276`).
- *Why the gap without the wake is 25 ms at most, not a second* (the worker's diagnostic commits on `fac8f86`, a ring of who took each step; runs `…2858608755900`, `…2960902012873`; the coordinator read the second run's raw lines). The first step after the close is the close's own return, on the job's AP. Nothing is sent to the BSP and no other AP steps while the job spins. The BSP stays halted until its own timer interrupt, 4 to 24 ms later. From then on the queue is stepped until it is empty, in about 90 ms: by the BSP's idle loop, or inside the syscalls and timer gates of system-init when it runs on the BSP. The deadline is inferred to be init's: its resident loop waits with a deadline of `RESIDENT_TICK_NS`, 25 ms, on every pass (Wyrmroot `userspace/system-init/src/resident_loop.rs`), every wait deadline sits on the BSP's timer, init was the only Process that ran next, and the gaps fit a 25 ms tick in random phase. Which deadline fired was not recorded.
- *So.* Revision 3's premise holds: nothing wakes the BSP for queued work and nobody else steps. Its size was overstated: in today's product the BSP's next deadline is never more than 25 ms away, so the missing wake cost at most about 25 ms per queueing. The wake brings that to about a quantum and no longer depends on init's tick.
- *The check catches a kernel without the wake.* One `--smp` boot on the diagnostic branch (`fe2b9c9`: `fac8f86` plus the diagnostic commits) failed with "the kernel reports no owed-work wakes" (`…3565921421391`).
- *Seen in passing.* While finalization is owed, one syscall of a thread on the BSP passes several BSP safe points and each takes a finalize step: about seven steps a syscall, 2.3 ms here, and up to four at each timer gate. That is how the queue empties in 90 ms, and it is paid by whichever thread runs on the BSP. Each step is still one bounded hold (§10.12).
- *Not shown.* That each counted wake belongs to a probe run: the count is for the whole boot (28 to 48), so a kernel that skipped the wake for one run would pass. That nothing else holds a periodic BSP deadline shorter than 25 ms. The retirement wake and the retry of an unsent wake still rest on host tests only. The 2.9 s boot is unexplained, and this case, bounded at 25 ms while init ticks, does not explain it.
- *Left over.* The diagnostic lane `lane/m5s8-no-wake-baseline` (`fe2b9c9`) and the branch `diag/m5s8-with-wake` (`44323c2`) held unmerged diagnostic commits that were never to be integrated. *Mike, 2026-11-15: delete them. Both branches are deleted and the lane retired; the audit shows no lane. The diagnostic boots' logs stay under `.tmp/dev-boot/`.*

## 11. M9 direction (Mike, 2026-11-13)

Mike: overcommit is handled the way Linux handles it, tailored to this architecture, OOM killer included. The design comes after M5, M7 and M8; this records the mapping it starts from.

| Linux | Deepwyrm and Wyrmroot |
|---|---|
| Overcommit modes (`vm.overcommit_memory`): heuristic (refuse one allocation larger than RAM plus swap), always, or strict commit accounting | Eager objects are already strictly committed (frames taken at create, `NO_MEMORY` when none). On-demand objects are the overcommitted reservations; the heuristic mode refuses one larger than total RAM. A strict mode, if wanted, charges an on-demand object's full size at create. The mode is a platform setting, heuristic by default |
| `oom_badness`: a task's resident pages plus swap and page tables, adjusted by `oom_score_adj` | A job's charged pages (its TaskGroup's usage, §8.4, which already counts touched pages, index nodes and page tables), adjusted by a per-job score Wyrmroot sets at launch; resident services are protected the way `-1000` protects a task |
| The kernel picks and kills inside the allocation path, and the OOM reaper frees the victim's memory early | Policy stays in Wyrmroot (root `CLAUDE.md` §6; §6.7): when an allocation finds no frames, the kernel parks the allocating thread instead of ending it and signals a low-memory event; Wyrmroot's OOM service, holding the jobs' TaskGroup handles, picks the worst job and terminates it, and the parked thread retries once frames are free. If the service cannot act in bounded time (it may itself need memory), the kernel falls back to ending the allocating Process, as it does today |
| PSI and userspace killers (`systemd-oomd`, Android's `lmkd`) act before exhaustion | A watermark signal before frames run out, so the service can act early |

Questions for M9's design, not decided here: what the kernel reserves so the OOM service can always run; whether a parked fault counts against the stop and preemption rules of §8.5; how the per-job score is carried; and whether kernel-internal allocations (page tables, index nodes) wait or fail.

## 12. M7: fragmentation that stays fast (started 2026-11-15)

Mike, 2026-11-15: start M7 with the buddy allocator's design; the coordinator reads and designs, and an Opus worker gets a plan to implement, not design work. §6.8 is the direction this follows.

### 12.1 Steps

Each step is designed here by the coordinator before it is built, approved by Mike, built by an Opus worker, then reviewed by the coordinator (the security review included, since the allocator and the boot path change) and booted with `dev-boot --check --smp --sdk`.

1. **The buddy allocator** (§12.2). **Built 2026-11-15**, Deepwyrm `1aeb551`. Closes §9's R4 (allocation costs runs × free ranges) and §5.3a's R5 (the start index's position lookup scans the initial ranges).
2. **The role table** (§6.8 item 4, §9's R1), in two parts built one after the other: 2a, a smaller slot (§12.3); 2b, storage in pieces (§12.4). Designed 2026-11-15 and approved by Mike the same day ("Approve, build 2a"; 2b follows 2a). **2a built 2026-11-16**, Deepwyrm `c19513d` (§12.6, as built). The fix for the BSP's timer re-arm, a fault found by the four-CPU icount boot, was built before 2b (§12.7, Deepwyrm `18fed40`). 2b was then built as §12.4 has it and **not merged**: it fails the stop rule (§12.9, which holds the counts and the revised design). Mike approved the revision ("Approve 12.9") and it was **built 2026-11-16**, Deepwyrm `9d62d5c` (§12.9, as built). Step 2 is done.
3. **The object index for fragmented eager backings** (§6.8 item 1, §9's R2). Designed 2026-11-16 in §12.10, in two parts: a fragmented guest to measure with, then the index. Mike approved it the same day ("Approve and build part 1"). **Part 1 built and measured 2026-11-16** (§12.10, part 1 as built). **Part 2 built 2026-11-16**, Deepwyrm `9114920` (§12.10, part 2 as built). Step 3b, allocating a fragmented backing in steps, is named there; it is designed in §12.11 (2026-11-16), in two parts like step 3 (measure the hold, then build), and approved by Mike the same day; it carries the items §9 gives it. **Step 3b part 1 built 2026-11-16** (Deepwyrm `1f01518`) and **part 2, items 1 to 10, built 2026-11-16** (Deepwyrm `42159ae`; §12.11, part 2 as built). **Item 11 and the check's two rules built 2026-11-16** (Deepwyrm `2ebf6ba`, Wyrmroot `b53b404`; §12.11, item 11 as built). Step 3b is complete.
4. **Compaction** (§6.8 item 3). **Designed 2026-11-16 in §12.12**, in three parts: a count, the cheaper paths the count selects, and a decision on whether to build page migration at all. Mike approved it the same day ("Approve all four"): page migration is not built. What this item said before the design: its design also looks for a cheaper free (§9: a finalize step costs 12% more instructions than before M7, cause not measured; Mike, 2026-11-16). Mike the same day, on how low-level code here may be written: "don't be afraid to use C++ or even C for this as I know rust tends to get kinda bloated the lower you want to go at hardware". The same day, when the coordinator said a C free would cost the same because it merges blocks too: "Odd thing to claim for sure without profiling it." So the design does not argue which language is cheaper: it builds the free path in Rust and in C or C++, boots both under `--icount` on the same workload, and chooses from the counts. A build that gains a C or C++ compiler gets it through the pinned toolchain, which the design must cover. **Built 2026-11-16 and 2026-11-17** (§12.12, the "as built" records; Deepwyrm `65d03fe`, `5eebd74`, `2fd831d`, `702f176`): node access, the free bitmap, the allocator's paths without bounds checks in Rust (it beat C in every timed region), and one walk for a leaf in lookups, release steps and decommits. Step 4 is done, and M7 with it (§12.13).

### 12.2 Step 1: the buddy allocator: design (2026-11-15; approved by Mike the same day and built: see "As built" at the end)

Read for this design, at Deepwyrm `eb5f17e`: `kernel/src/memory/physical.rs` (all), `memory/growable.rs`, `memory/frame_roles.rs` (the slot, record and manager types), `memory/frame_roles/manager.rs` (construction, `allocate`, `allocate_below`, `grown_layout`, `adopt_storage`, `check_invariants`, `cancel`, the start index), `memory/frame_roles/chain.rs` (`take_run`, `free_chain`), `memory/frame_roles/index.rs` (the frame takes and frees), the test helpers in `frame_roles/test_support.rs`, `chain_tests.rs`, `index_tests.rs` and `tests.rs` (growth), `arch/x86_64/mm/activation.rs` (`grow_frame_roles`, `allocate_zeroed_architecture_frame`), `activation/build.rs` (the ceilings), `arch/x86_64/vtd.rs` (its ceiling), and `lib.rs` (the boot order). Not read: the rest of `manager.rs`, the publisher, and the boot map's sanitizer; nothing below depends on them.

**What the code does today.**
- `PhysicalFrameAllocator` keeps the ranges it may ever hand out (`initial`) and a free list sorted by address. `allocate_run` takes the lowest range that fits (first fit); `free_run` inserts and merges. Allocation, the free's overlap check, `largest_free_pages` and `available_frames` are all linear in the free ranges. `available_frames` is on a syscall path (the TaskGroup memory query).
- The free list starts inline (4,503 entries) and, at `grow_frame_roles` right after activation, moves into the grown role table's run, sized by `free_range_bound` at one `PageRange` (16 bytes) per allocator page.
- Who allocates what, by grep over the production tree: single pages (`allocate(1)`: tables, roots, index nodes and on-demand pages through `take_run(1)`); single pages below a ceiling (`allocate_below(1, …)`: the AP trampoline below 1 MiB and the boot root below 4 GiB, both while the root is built, before activation; VT-d's table pool below the units' address width, after growth, at boot only); the grown table's own run (`allocate(layout.page_count())`, before the table is adopted); and scattered backings (`take_run(wanted.min(largest))`, any length). So after growth nothing asks for a contiguous run of a given length except `take_run`, which accepts whatever the largest free piece is.

**Decisions.**

1. *Two phases.* The range list stays as the boot allocator, unchanged, until the role table grows; the buddy allocator takes over at that moment and serves for the rest of the kernel's life. Reason: buddy metadata is per page and has to live in RAM reached through the direct map, which does not exist before activation. The boot phase makes a bounded number of allocations (the inline list already bounds it at 4,096 live runs). A side effect that matters for testing: every existing host test that does not grow the table sees the same allocator as before.
2. *Metadata outside the frames.* Free-list links live in a per-page array in the grown table's run, not inside the free frames. A kernel bug that left a mapping to a freed frame then cannot corrupt the allocator, and nothing reads a freed frame's contents. Cost: 9 bytes per allocator page (a 1-byte tag and two 4-byte links), against the 16 bytes per page of the range list it replaces. The grown table goes from 104 to 97 bytes per page.
3. *Runs keep their exact length.* A request for `n` pages takes the smallest free block that holds it, keeps the first `n` pages as the run and returns the rest to the free lists at once. A run is therefore still an arbitrary range freed whole (M1's rule at the role level is untouched), and a backing gets as few runs as the free blocks allow. Freeing a run splits it into aligned blocks, which merge with their neighbours.
4. *Blocks are aligned by absolute page number*, and a block lies inside one initial range. Two buddies merge only when the merged block still lies inside that range. Orders run from 0 to 40 (a block of 2^40 pages is the 52-bit physical limit), so the per-order state is 41 list heads and one 64-bit mask.

**Data** (in `physical.rs`).

```rust
/// Links of a free block's first page; positions are stored plus one, 0 = none.
#[derive(Clone, Copy)] #[repr(C)]
pub(super) struct FreeLink { next: u32, prev: u32 }

const BUDDY_ORDERS: usize = 41;

struct Buddy {
    /// Per allocator page (by page position): 0, or 1 + the order of the
    /// free block that starts at this page.
    tags: &'static mut [u8],
    /// Per allocator page; meaningful only where `tags` is non-zero.
    links: &'static mut [FreeLink],
    /// Per initial range: the allocator pages before it.
    bases: &'static mut [u64],
    /// Per order: position + 1 of the first free block, 0 = empty.
    heads: [u32; BUDDY_ORDERS],
    /// Bit k is set iff `heads[k] != 0`.
    nonempty: u64,
    free_pages: u64,
}
```

`PhysicalFrameAllocator` gains `buddy: Option<Buddy>`; its `free` becomes a plain inline array (it is never adopted any more), so `adopt_free_list`, `free_list_capacity_for` and the allocator's use of `GrowableStore` go. A *page position* is a page's index in the concatenation of the initial ranges, as the start index already defines it. New `page_position(physical_start) -> Option<usize>`: a linear scan before adoption (today's `start_position`), a binary search over the initial ranges plus `bases` after it. The manager's `start_position` calls it and loses its own scan.

**Invariants** (checked by a new `check_buddy`, called from `check_invariants`).
- I1. A non-zero tag `k + 1` sits only on a page whose page number is a multiple of 2^k, and the block of 2^k pages starting there lies inside one initial range.
- I2. Free blocks are pairwise disjoint, and no page inside a free block other than its first has a non-zero tag.
- I3. Every free block is on the list of its order exactly once; each list is a consistent doubly linked list; `nonempty` matches the heads.
- I4. No two free blocks are buddies of the same order whose union lies inside one initial range.
- I5. `free_pages` is the sum of the free blocks' pages.

**Operations after adoption.** `pfn` is a physical address divided by the page size. All arithmetic is checked and every refusal is decided before anything is changed, as today.

- `insert_range(start, pages)` (private; used by seeding, by the tail return and by `free_run`): find the initial range `r` holding `start` once. While pages remain: `k = min(trailing_zeros(pfn), ilog2(remaining))`; insert the block `(pfn, k)`; advance by 2^k. To insert a block: while `k < 40`, let `b = pfn ^ (1 << k)` and `m = min(pfn, b)`; stop unless the block `(m, k + 1)` lies inside `r` and `tags[pos(b)] == k + 1`; otherwise unlink `b` from list `k`, clear its tag, and continue with `(m, k + 1)`. Then set `tags[pos(pfn)] = k + 1` and push the block on the head of list `k`. Inside the loop a position is `bases[r] + (pfn - start_pfn(r))`, so each level is O(1).
- `allocate_run(n)`: `k = ceil(log2(n))`. If `nonempty >> k` is zero, `NoFramesAvailable`. Otherwise take the head of the lowest non-empty order `j >= k` (unlink, clear the tag), `insert_range` the tail `[start + n, start + 2^j)` if there is one, and subtract `n` from `free_pages`. Return `[start, start + n)`. `insert_range` itself never touches `free_pages`; its callers do.
- `allocate_run_below(n, ceiling)`: the same, for a block whose first `n` pages end at or below `min(ceiling, limit)`. First try the head of each non-empty order `j >= k`. If none qualifies, scan the tags upward from the lowest allocator page, one initial range after another: at a free block of order at least `k` that qualifies, take it; at a smaller free block skip its pages; otherwise step one page; stop at the ceiling. The scan is linear in the allocator pages below the ceiling. Its callers after growth are VT-d's pool at boot, whose ceiling is normally above all of RAM, so the first try answers.
- `free_run(range)`: `InvalidPageRange` and `OutsidePhysicalLimit` as today; `InvalidFrame` unless the range lies inside one initial range; `DoubleFree` if any page of it is free, which is decided exactly by two checks: no tag in `[pos(start), pos(end))` is non-zero (read eight tags at a time where aligned), and for each order `k` from 1 to 40 the aligned page `h = pfn & !((1 << k) - 1)`, when `h < pfn` and `h` is inside the range's initial range, does not carry tag `k + 1`. Then `insert_range`, and add the pages to `free_pages`.
- `largest_free_pages()`: 0 if `nonempty` is 0, else `1 << (63 - nonempty.leading_zeros())`. `available_frames()`: `free_pages`. `overlaps_free(range)`: the two `DoubleFree` checks.
- `adopt_buddy(tags, links, bases) -> Result<(), (…)>` replaces `adopt_free_list`: it refuses (returning the slices) unless this is the first adoption, `tags.len() == links.len() ==` the initial frames, that count is below `u32::MAX`, and `bases.len() ==` the number of initial ranges. It fills `bases`, then calls `insert_range` for each range of the boot free list in order, sets `free_pages` to their total, and sets `buddy`. Nothing in it can fail after the checks.

Before adoption every one of these runs today's code, unchanged.

**Costs.** Allocation and merging are bounded by the 41 orders, whatever the fragmentation: an allocation is one mask test, one unlink and at most 40 tail blocks; a free is at most 80 blocks of at most 40 merge steps, plus one byte read per page freed. `largest_free_pages` and `available_frames` are O(1). The M1 bound no longer limits anything after growth: a free cannot run out of list entries, because each page has its own.

**The manager** (`frame_roles.rs`, `manager.rs`).
- `RoleTableLayout` drops `ranges_offset` and `ranges` and gains `links_offset`, `bases_offset`, `bases` and `tags_offset` (tags last; every offset stays 8-aligned through the existing `after` helper). `grown_layout` sizes `links` and `tags` by `starts` and `bases` by the initial range count. The clause that kept the grown free list longer than the boot list (`.max(RANGE_CAPACITY + 1)`, §5.3a D1) and `adopt_storage`'s `layout.ranges > RANGE_CAPACITY` test go with the list.
- `adopt_storage` carves the three new parts inside its existing unsafe block and calls `adopt_buddy` where it called `adopt_free_list`. Its rule stands: every check before any change, and on an error the grant comes back with the manager as it was. So `adopt_buddy`'s refusals must all be reachable before `roles.adopt` runs: check them first.
- `take_run`, `free_chain`, the index's takes and frees, `allocate`, `allocate_below` and `cancel` do not change: they call the same allocator methods.
- `lib.rs` logs one more boot line next to `frame-role slots=`: the free pages and the largest free order at adoption.

**What changes in behaviour.** After growth, allocations no longer come lowest address first: they come from the most recently freed block of the smallest sufficient order. Nothing read for this design depends on low addresses without asking for a ceiling; the review checks that again (DMA without an IOMMU is the place to look). A backing in unfragmented memory may have a few runs where it had one, because a run cannot be longer than the largest aligned block; the count is bounded by about twice the number of orders involved, and step 3's index removes the cost. No ABI change.

**Tests (host).** All behaviour tests; none reads source.
1. *The buddy alone* (`physical/tests.rs`, storage from leaked vectors as `frame_roles/tests.rs` does). Seeding: a boot free list with unaligned ranges becomes the expected set of maximal aligned blocks (a test-only iterator over free blocks in address order). Allocation: exact length, tail returned, `available_frames` and `largest_free_pages` follow. Freeing everything in a different order restores the seeded block set. `DoubleFree` for the same block, for a page inside a free block and for a range containing a free block, each leaving the state unchanged; `InvalidFrame` for a range outside or across initial ranges. No merge across two initial ranges whose pages would pair as buddies. `allocate_run_below`: a block below the ceiling is found by the first try and by the scan; a refusal changes nothing. Exhaustion: single pages until `NoFramesAvailable`, the total equal to the initial frames. `page_position` gives the same answers before and after adoption.
2. *A model test.* A fixed-seed generator (a small LCG written in the test; no new crate) drives a few thousand allocations and frees of mixed sizes over three initial ranges of a few thousand pages against a `Vec<bool>` model. After every operation: `check_buddy` holds, `available_frames` matches the model, no returned run overlaps a live one, and `largest_free_pages` equals the largest aligned free block the model computes.
3. *The manager after growth* (`frame_roles/tests.rs`). The growth tests keep their meaning with the new layout. `the_grown_free_list_holds_more_holes_than_the_boot_list` becomes a test that a grown manager survives far more simultaneous holes than the inline list could hold (single pages allocated, every other one freed, `check_invariants` after each phase). `growth_succeeds_when_the_boot_free_list_is_longer_than_the_grown_bound` loses its premise and is replaced by growth on a small machine (the same sizes) simply succeeding.
4. *Chains and indexes on the buddy allocator.* The existing chain and index tests assert first-fit addresses and stay as boot-phase tests, untouched. New grown-phase tests with assertions that hold for either allocator: a backing of `n` pages resolves every page through a cursor to distinct frames inside allocator RAM, and cancelling it restores `available_frames` and the free block set; a request larger than the largest free block succeeds in several runs whose lengths never increase along the chain; running out of memory mid-backing frees every run taken; an on-demand backing commits scattered pages and a stepped release returns every page and node; `check_invariants` holds throughout.
5. Each new test is shown to fail against a real fault before it is trusted: at least the merge across initial ranges (drop the range check), the tail return (drop it), and the descendant double free (drop the tag scan).

**Checks.** The four kernel commands (`host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast`, `xtask clippy` with the ratchet unchanged, `xtask kernel-check`, `fmt --check`); then from Wyrmroot `dev-boot --check --smp --sdk` and one boot on one CPU against the lane. Production runs the buddy allocator for everything after growth, so those boots exercise it under every probe; the worker reports the new boot line and the step report's lines beside a boot of the base revision.

**Not in this step.** The shape and size of `RoleSlot`, and the grown table still being one contiguous run found first-fit by the boot allocator (step 2, which also decides how a table in several pieces is carved). The object index (step 3). Compaction (step 4). Any minimum or preferred run alignment for 2 MiB pages (later, §2).

**For the coordinator's review after the build.** Double and partial frees at the allocator; merges across ranges; position arithmetic and the `u32` position limit; `adopt_storage` staying all-or-nothing; the tail return; a consumer that assumed low addresses; and the cost of `free_run`'s tag scan for one very large run inside a stepped call.

**As built (2026-11-15).** Mike approved the design ("Approve, build it"). Deepwyrm `a7b15e3` (the allocator) and `1aeb551` (the range lookup, below), both signed, by an Opus worker from this design; reviewed by the coordinator, who also did the security review and re-ran the checks and boots. Main was fast-forwarded to `1aeb551` and the lane retired. Wyrmroot `1dc2efe` and the SDK `56eb579` are unchanged; `abi/` and `crates/` are untouched.

- *Built as designed*, with these additions. The buddy code is a module of its own, `memory/physical/buddy.rs`, with no `unsafe`. `accepts_buddy_storage` is the one statement of what adoption refuses, called by `adopt_buddy` and by `adopt_storage`'s checks, so `adopt_storage` stays all-or-nothing by construction. `overlaps_free` takes any range and clips it to each initial range. `check_buddy` reports any violation as `InvalidFrame`, which the manager maps to `InvariantViolation`. Seeding clears the tags and links again although `carve` has written them: a second linear write at boot.
- *One change to the design, found by measuring.* The design made the page-position lookup a binary search over the initial ranges. The page walk makes that lookup three or four times for every page a map or protect touches (`table_identity` through the start index), and the old scan had usually hit in the first or second range. In boots interleaved with the base, a map step was 25 to 35% slower and a protect step about 45% slower at `a7b15e3`. With the old linear scan put back (a scratch commit, since deleted) both fell below the base, which confirmed the cause. `1aeb551` replaces the search with *hints*: one entry for each granule of address space (1 GiB, or larger so that at most 4,096 granules reach the last range's last byte), naming the first initial range that can hold an address in that granule. A lookup starts there and walks upward over the ranges sharing the granule. The hints are a fourth part of the buddy storage and a sixth invariant (I6). §5.3a's R5 is closed by this, not by the binary search.
- *Review and security review* (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, 2026-11-15, of `b3ff9bf..1aeb551`; the author was the worker). No defect found. Read in full: `buddy.rs`, and the diffs of `physical.rs`, `frame_roles.rs` and `manager.rs`. Checked: a free of a page already free is refused in all three shapes (the same block, a page inside a free block, a range containing or ending inside one); no merge leaves an initial range, since the range test comes before any position is computed; positions fit `u32` because adoption refuses a page count at or above `u32::MAX`; the tail return cannot lose or double-count pages; every refusal precedes any change; `adopt_storage` checks the buddy storage before it moves anything. Nothing depends on low addresses after growth: device DMA goes through IOMMU domains with their own address ranges, and the callers that need a ceiling ask for one. `check_invariants` is not on a runtime path. Risks kept, all Low: `allocate_run_below`'s scan is linear in the pages below its ceiling (boot-only callers); seeding trusts that a boot free range lies inside one initial range (the boot list merges touching ranges, so initial ranges never touch; a violation would fail `check_buddy`, not corrupt memory).
- *Tests (host).* 1,418 kernel tests pass. New: 17 tests of the buddy allocator and its lookups, among them a 4,000-step model test that checks every result and every refusal against a reference model, and lookups compared with the plain linear definition over four layouts (one above 4 TiB); four tests of chains and indexes on a grown manager; the two growth tests adapted. The worker showed tests failing with the range test on merges removed (20 tests), the tail return removed (13), the tag scan removed (2), and the hints given a wrong definition (2 to 3).
- *Checks (coordinator, at `1aeb551`).* `host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` exit 0 with 1,418 passed; `xtask clippy` exit 0, ratchet unchanged (236 and 87); `xtask kernel-check` passed; `fmt --check` clean.
- *Boots (development tier, QEMU TCG, `dev-boot --check --smp --sdk`, Wyrmroot `1dc2efe`).* Every boot passed with no panic: seven at `a7b15e3` (six `--smp`, one on one CPU), two on the scratch commit with the linear scan, and at `1aeb551` three `--smp` and one on one CPU, among them the coordinator's `.tmp/dev-boot/1794779257426584088`. At takeover the kernel logs `frame-role slots=506570` and `buddy free-pages=490427 largest-pages=131072` (a largest free block of 512 MiB in the 2 GiB guest).
- *Step times, base `b3ff9bf` against `1aeb551`, three interleaved pairs* (mean microseconds per step, `call-total-us / call-steps`; base runs `…8535415570785`, `…8741337174605`, `…9160746312697`; buddy runs `…8634743713200`, `…8839580569639`, `…9257426584088`; host load 1 to 4):

| Call | Base | Buddy with hints | |
|---|---|---|---|
| eager create | 5,609 to 5,978 | 1,808 to 1,976 | about 3 times faster |
| map | 2,020 to 2,332 | 2,077 to 2,152 | the same |
| protect | 3,003 to 3,052 | 2,553 to 2,722 | 10 to 16% faster |
| unmap | 2,016 to 2,067 | 1,803 to 1,838 | about 10% faster |
| commit | 865 to 935 | 948 to 996 | 7 to 10% slower (§9) |

- *Corrected 2026-11-15 (§12.5).* The table above and the measurement behind the hints compare builds by QEMU wall time, which §12.5 shows cannot do that. Counted in instructions, the hints are worth about 1% on a map or protect step, not 25 to 45%; putting the linear scan back did not "confirm the cause", because that build differed in code placement too. The hints stay: they bound the lookup and cost nothing measurable. Eager create is not 3 times cheaper in instructions (it is 4% dearer); the wall-time gain is real on this host but its cause is not shown.
- *Not shown.* Hardware times: these are QEMU TCG. A machine with fragmented memory: the check boots never fragment the allocator much, so the bounded costs rest on the algorithm and the model test. The cost of `free_run`'s tag scan for one very large run, and of `allocate_run_below`'s scan, were not measured. A layout with many initial ranges in one granule exists only in host tests.

### 12.3 Step 2a: a smaller role slot: design (2026-11-15; approved by Mike the same day; built with §12.6's revision and merged: see "As built" in §12.6)

Read for §12.3 and §12.4, at Deepwyrm `fcec7d2`: `memory/frame_roles.rs` (every type), `frame_roles/manager.rs` (every access to a slot, a mark, a table count, a generation and the free-slot stack, found by grep and read in context: construction, `occupy`, `replace_record`, `vacate`, `next_slot`, `free_slot`, the kernel-image staging, the empty-hierarchy proof, `validate_staged_table`, `root_slot_of`, `identity`, `slot`, `validate_owner`, `grown_layout`, `adopt_storage`, `check_invariants`, `dynamic_slot_at`, `validate_external_pages`), the slot accesses in `chain.rs` and `index.rs`, the index's link types, `memory/growable.rs`, `physical/buddy.rs` (all, as merged), and `grow_frame_roles` in `arch/x86_64/mm/activation.rs`. Not read line by line: the rest of `manager.rs`. The design does not depend on it, because every write of a record goes through three functions (`occupy`, `replace_record`, `vacate`) and the design changes those.

**What the slot costs today.** `RoleSlot` is 80 bytes: the record 64 (a 16-byte range and a 48-byte role), the proof mark 8, the generation 4, the table count 4. The role is 48 bytes because of one variant, `PageTable`, which stores an owner key and an optional parent identity with a 64-bit manager domain in each, though every identity in the table belongs to this manager. The mark and the table count are used only on committed page tables. Beside the slots, the free-slot stack costs 4 bytes per slot. With step 1 the grown table is 97 bytes per allocator page (2.37% of RAM).

**Decisions.**

1. *A stored form and a logical form.* The code keeps working with today's `RoleRecord` and `FrameRole`. The table stores a compact `RoleSlot` of 48 bytes and converts at the accessors. So the logic of chains, indexes, tables and proofs does not change; only how a slot is read and written does.
2. *What the stored form drops.* The manager domain inside records (it is this manager's, checked where the value enters). The range's end (a start and a page count give it; a count fits 32 bits because the allocator has fewer than 2^32 pages, and an external role longer than that is refused with `Capacity`). The `Option` around the record (a free slot has a page count of zero). The mark and the table count as slot fields: they move into the stored page-table variants.
3. *No free-slot stack.* Free slots are linked through themselves: a free slot's `start` holds the next free slot plus one. The order of use is exactly today's (slot 0 first at boot; after growth the new slots lowest first, above the boot table's free ones), so issued slot numbers do not change.
4. *The duplicate-root check stops scanning the table.* Today every PML4 commit, which is every address space created, scans every slot for another PML4 of the same owner: 506,570 slots in the 2 GiB guest and one per page of RAM on a large machine. The slots of PML4 level (candidates and committed roots) are instead kept on a singly linked list threaded through their stored variants, and the check walks that list, which is as long as the number of address spaces.

**Data** (`frame_roles.rs`).

```rust
/// One slot as stored: 48 bytes (asserted at compile time).
#[derive(Clone, Copy)] #[repr(C)]
struct RoleSlot {
    generation: u32,
    /// The run's pages; 0 if the slot is free.
    pages: u32,
    /// The run's physical start; in a free slot, the next free slot plus one (0 = none).
    start: u64,
    role: StoredRole,
}

/// A role as stored: 32 bytes (asserted at compile time). Identities and
/// owners are raw values in this manager's domain.
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
enum StoredRole {
    Free,
    AllocatedUninitialized,
    Zeroed,
    BackingAllocated(ChainLink),
    ObjectBacking(ChainLink),
    ArchitectureBootstrap(ArchitectureBootstrapKind),
    /// A PML4 candidate; `next_root` is the next slot of the root list plus one.
    RootCandidate { owner: u64, next_root: u32 },
    TableCandidate { owner: u64, level: TableLevel },
    /// A committed PML4.
    Root { owner: u64, mark: u64, tables: u32, next_root: u32 },
    /// A committed table below a PML4; `parent` is its parent's raw identity.
    PageTable { owner: u64, parent: u64, mark: u64, tables: u32, level: TableLevel },
    External(ExternalFrameRole),
    ExternalImmutableModule { module_index: u32 },
    ExternalDeviceMmio,
    RoleTableStorage,
    IndexRoot(IndexRootLink),
    IndexNode(IndexEntryLink),
    ObjectPage(IndexEntryLink),
}
```

The manager loses `free_slots` and gains `first_free: u32` and `first_root: u32` (slot plus one, 0 = none); `free_slot_count` stays as a count. If the compiler does not lay `StoredRole` out in 32 bytes, the assertion fails the build and the worker stops and reports; the design does not fall back to a larger slot silently.

**Accessors** (`manager.rs`; the only code that touches a `RoleSlot`'s fields).

- `slot_view(slot) -> SlotView { generation, record: Option<RoleRecord>, mark, tables }` unpacks one slot: the range is `start .. start + pages × 4096`; `Root` unpacks to `PageTable { owner, level: Pml4, parent: None }`, `RootCandidate` to `TableCandidate { owner, level: Pml4 }`, and owners and parents get this manager's domain back. `mark` and `tables` are zero unless the slot is a committed table. Every `self.roles.as_slice()[slot]` read becomes this.
- `slot_views()` iterates all slots as `(slot, SlotView)`, for the scans that remain (`check_invariants`, the chain and index checks, `validate_external_pages`, `dynamic_slot_at` before growth, the start index's rebuild at adoption).
- `store(slot, generation, Option<RoleRecord>)` is the one writer of a record, called by `occupy`, `replace_record` and `vacate`. It packs the record and keeps three things right by comparing what the slot held with what it will hold:
  - *mark and table count:* kept when a committed table stays a committed table, zero otherwise (today `occupy` and `vacate` zero them and `replace_record` keeps them, which is the same thing);
  - *the root list:* a slot that becomes a PML4 candidate or root is pushed on the list; one that stops being either is unlinked by a walk from `first_root`; one that goes from candidate to root keeps its link;
  - *the free list:* `vacate` pushes the slot unless its generation is spent, and `occupy` pops it, still asserting that it is the first free slot.
  Packing refuses what cannot be stored, by a panic that names it, since each is a kernel bug and not an input: an owner or a parent of another domain, a committed table below PML4 with no parent, a PML4 with one, a page count above `u32::MAX` on a dynamic role. Before the build the worker lists every place a record with an owner or a parent is created and shows that each validates the domain first (`validate_owner`, `slot`); if one does not, it stops and reports.
- `set_mark(slot, epoch)`, `set_tables(slot, count)`: write the field of a committed table; on any other slot they panic (the callers have validated the table).
- `free_slot(depth)` walks `depth` links from `first_free` (depth is at most 2, in the kernel-image staging) and returns the slot and its next generation, as today.
- The duplicate-root check in `validate_staged_table` walks the root list and returns `DuplicateTableRoot` if a slot other than the staged one has the same owner.

**Growth.** `RoleTableLayout` loses the free-slot stack. `adopt_storage` links the new slots onto the free list in the order above instead of filling the stack. The root list needs no rebuilding: it lives in the slots, which are copied. The grown table becomes 48 + 4 (start index) + 8 + 1 (buddy) = 61 bytes per allocator page, 1.49% of RAM, from 97 (2.37%) now and 104 before M7.

**Invariants added to `check_invariants`.** The free list reaches exactly the free slots whose generation is not spent, each once, and `free_slot_count` is its length. The root list reaches exactly the slots of PML4 level, each once. No owner has two slots on the root list.

**Tests (host, behaviour).** Every existing test passes unchanged except those that construct a `RoleSlot` or read the free-slot stack directly, which move to the accessors. New: a record of every role kind survives a store and a read unchanged, with ranges at the limits (one page; exactly `u32::MAX` pages stored; one more refused on a dynamic role); a table's mark and count survive a role change from table to table and are zero after any other; slots are issued in the same order as before, at boot and after growth (compare slot numbers against a recorded sequence produced by the code before this change, kept as literal numbers in the test); a spent generation retires its slot and the free list skips it; the kernel-image staging still sees the three next slots; a second PML4 for an owner is refused whether the first is a candidate or committed, and accepted after the first is reclaimed; the root list survives growth; `check_invariants` fails on a corrupted free list, a corrupted root list and two roots of one owner. Each new check is shown to fail against a real fault (at least: the mark dropped on a table-to-table change, the root not unlinked at reclaim, the free list pushed in the wrong order at growth).

**Checks and measurement.** The four kernel commands; then three interleaved pairs of `dev-boot --check --smp --sdk`, base against this, and one boot on one CPU. Report the mean step time of map, protect, unmap, commit, create and teardown, the check's seconds, and the `[memory]` lines. Expected, to be confirmed or refuted by the numbers: commit no slower than the base of step 1 (a commit now pops a list and writes 48 bytes where it read a stack and wrote 80), and process creation faster (no table scan). If map, protect or commit is more than 5% slower in all three pairs, stop and report.

**Not in 2a.** Where the table lives (2b). The start index (it stays). External roles' own scan at boot.

### 12.4 Step 2b: the grown table in pieces: design (2026-11-15; approved with step 2; built after 2a with §12.8's notes, and not merged: it fails the stop rule, see §12.9)

**The problem (§9 R1).** The grown table is one contiguous run, found first-fit at boot: 1.49% of RAM after 2a, about 950 MiB at 64 GiB. A memory map without such a hole stops the boot.

**Decisions.**

1. *Fixed-size chunks, the same for every array.* The four arrays that grow with memory (slots, start index, buddy links, buddy tags) are each cut into chunks of 2^`shift` entries, and an entry is found by `index >> shift` and `index & mask`: one more load than a slice, no search. `shift = max(15, bits(slots - 1) - 6)`, so no array has more than 64 chunks. Considered and not taken: one piece per initial range (free for lookups by address, but the buddy lists link pages by position and the slots are not per page), and a virtually contiguous mapping (no cost in the accessors, but new kernel page tables and changes to the activation proofs).
2. *One run per chunk group.* Group `k` is one contiguous run holding chunk `k` of each array (61 bytes per entry): 2 MiB at the smallest shift, 32 MiB at 64 GiB. A small *directory* run holds the four arrays of chunk slices, the buddy's `bases` and its `hints`. So growth allocates at most 65 runs. A group is less than 1/32 of the table, 0.05% of RAM. Only a map whose ranges are nearly all smaller than that could fail to hold the groups, and the sanitizer's limit of 128 usable ranges rules it out: such ranges together hold under 7% of RAM, and the rest lies in ranges that each hold several groups.
3. *Staged, then adopted at once.* `grow_frame_roles` allocates and maps the parts one by one and hands each to the manager, which only records it; nothing is used until all are there. Then adoption checks everything and switches in one step that cannot fail. If any allocation fails first, the staged parts are freed and the manager is as it was. This keeps `adopt_storage`'s rule (every check before any change) without an array of grants on the boot stack.

**Data.**

- `memory/growable.rs` gains `ChunkedStore<T>`: `chunks: &'static mut [&'static mut [T]]` (every chunk full except possibly the last), `shift`, `len`; methods `len`, `get(index) -> T`, `get_mut(index) -> &mut T`, `set`, `iter`, and `segments(range) -> impl Iterator<Item = &[T]>` (the contiguous pieces of an index range, in order, for scans such as the buddy's tag scan). Out-of-range indexes panic, as a slice's do. `GrowableStore<T, N>` becomes the inline array or a `ChunkedStore`, with the same methods, so the manager's accessors from 2a call `get` and `set` and do not know which. Its module comment is rewritten (§9).
- `BuddyStorage` carries `tags` and `links` as `ChunkedStore`s; `bases` and `hints` stay slices. In `buddy.rs` every `self.tags[position]` and `self.links[position]` becomes `get`, `get_mut` or `set`; `any_tag` and the interior check of `check` take `segments`; the test-only block iterator uses `get`. Seeding no longer clears the tags and links: adoption has just written them (the double write noted in §12.2 goes).
- The start index becomes a `ChunkedStore<u32>`.
- The manager gains a fixed array of staged parts, `[Option<StagedPart>; 65]`, where a part is its run's identity and range, its base address and its length.

**Protocol** (`manager.rs`, `activation.rs`).

- `growth_plan() -> Result<GrowthPlan, FrameRoleError>` replaces `grown_layout`: the slot count (boot slots plus one per allocator page), the page count, the shift, the number of groups, and for each part its pages and the offsets of its arrays inside it (8-aligned, as today). Part 0 is the directory.
- `stage_growth_part(plan, index, grant, base) -> Result<(), GrantTransitionError<AllocationGrant>>` records one part. It refuses a part out of order, a run shorter than the part, a base that is not 8-aligned, and any part once the table is grown. The run keeps the role it was allocated with.
- `adopt_storage(plan)` requires every part staged and the buddy storage acceptable (2a's and step 1's checks, restated for the chunked arrays), then, with nothing left that can fail: carves the directory and each group, writes every element, copies the boot slots to the same indexes, links the new slots onto the free list (2a), gives every part's run the permanent `RoleTableStorage` role, adopts the buddy storage, and rebuilds the start index.
- `abandon_growth()` frees every staged run and clears the staging; the manager is as before the first part.
- `grow_frame_roles` loops over the plan's parts: allocate (`roles.allocate`), take the direct map's address, stage; on any failure abandon and return the error (the boot then panics as it does today). Then adopt.

Every `unsafe` block added carries its `SAFETY` comment; the count of undocumented blocks does not rise. The carving stays in one helper.

**Invariants added.** Every chunk but the last of an array has 2^`shift` entries, and the lengths sum to the array's length. The `RoleTableStorage` runs are exactly the staged parts. The buddy's and the manager's existing invariants are checked through the chunked accessors.

**Tests (host, behaviour).** `ChunkedStore`: `get`, `set`, `iter` and `segments` agree with a plain vector over lengths below, at and above chunk boundaries, with a last chunk that is short and one that is full. The buddy tests and the model test run on storage cut into several small chunks (a test-only shift well below 15), so every scan crosses chunk boundaries; they must pass unchanged in what they assert. Growth: a manager grown from a plan with several groups passes the grown-phase tests of §12.2; staging refuses each bad part; a failure at each position in the sequence, followed by `abandon_growth`, leaves `available_frames`, the free blocks and `check_invariants` as before the first part; growth succeeds on a boot map whose largest free range is smaller than the whole table but larger than one group, which fails on the code before this change. Each new check is shown to fail against a real fault (at least: `segments` dropping the piece after a boundary, the abandon leaving one run staged, a group one entry short).

**Checks and measurement.** As for 2a, base against this, with the same stop rule at 5% on map, protect and commit: the extra load on every slot and start-index access is the cost to watch. The `[memory]` lines gain the shift and the number of groups.

**Not in 2b.** A slot per page no longer indexed through the start index (a larger redesign, with the allocator's metadata folded into it; a candidate for a later stage, not scheduled). External roles' boot-time scans.

### 12.5 Measuring by instruction count (2026-11-15)

**Why.** Step 2a's worker stopped twice at the 5% rule: `b63aac0` and then `8379b6d` both showed map and protect steps tens of percent slower than the base in `dev-boot` boots. Three experiments then showed the instrument, not only the change, was moving. A copy of 2a padded so that every table had the base's size and layout was as slow as 2a. Every backing was a single run in both builds, so fragmentation was not the cause. And 2a with a 31-line diagnostic patch added ran *faster* than the base (map by 22 to 35%, protect by 16 to 20%). So the mean wall time of a step under QEMU TCG shifts by tens of percent between builds whose code merely sits at different addresses (presumably how TCG chains translated blocks across pages; not shown), on top of host load. It cannot compare two builds, and the 5% stop rule could not be applied with it. KVM is not available to this account (`/dev/kvm` is `root:kvm`), so hardware timing is not either.

**The instrument.** Mike, 2026-11-15: "Add icount mode". Wyrmroot `658a85f` (signed; by an Opus worker from the coordinator's design; the coordinator read the diff and re-ran `xtask test fmt`, `clippy`, `unit` and the boots below) adds `dev-boot --icount`: QEMU runs with `-icount shift=0`, so guest time is one nanosecond per guest instruction and the kernel's step report counts instructions. The check's host-side waits are four times as long in this mode, its pass line ends in `icount=1`, and in every mode it prints each call's mean step (`step means us …`, or `kinstr`, thousands of instructions, under icount; the line rounds down, so the figures below come from the raw totals).

- *What it measures.* Guest instructions executed in a step. Not time: caches, TLBs and branch prediction do not exist in it, so a change that trades instructions for a smaller footprint gets no credit, and nothing here is a hardware time.
- *Repeatability.* Two boots of the same build (main, below) agree to five digits on protect and finalize, within 0.2% on commit and 0.4% on unmap. Map differs by 3% and create by 1%: their step counts depend on where the probes' kills land, so they are a slightly different workload each boot.
- *One CPU only for now.* With four CPUs the kernel halts under icount (§9).

**Five builds, one CPU, `dev-boot --check --icount --sdk`, Wyrmroot `658a85f`** (thousands of guest instructions per step, `call-total-us / call-steps`; runs under `.tmp/dev-boot/`: `1794801079305142726`, `1794801356941370646`, `1794800522172216234` and `1794801935986692868`, `1794801635804416926`, `1794800792816505248`; a plain one-CPU check of main also passed, `1794800398601458793`):

| Call | Before M7 (`b3ff9bf`) | Buddy, binary search (`a7b15e3`) | Main: buddy with hints (`1aeb551`), two boots | 2a first (`b63aac0`) | 2a (`8379b6d`) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| map | 882 | 888 | 876, 903 | 1,442 | 1,048 |
| unmap | 2,371 | 2,223 | 2,200, 2,208 | 3,784 | 2,540 |
| protect | 2,992 | 3,046 | 3,007, 3,007 | 5,385 | 3,518 |
| fill-in | 4.68 | 4.75 | 4.66, 4.64 | 4.57 | 4.57 |
| commit | 1,123 | 1,200 | 1,190, 1,188 | 1,553 | 1,322 |
| eager create | 1,159 | 1,202 | 1,202, 1,212 | 1,199 | 1,170 |
| teardown | 110.7 | 110.2 | 111.0, 111.2 | 119.7 | 109.6 |
| finalize | 635 | 783 | 737, 737 | 1,260 | 953 |

**What this corrects.**

- *Step 1's hints (§12.2).* Between the binary search and the hints, map and protect change by about 1%, unmap by 1%, finalize by 6%. The 25 to 45% that §12.2 reports was the instrument.
- *Step 1's cost.* Against the kernel before M7, the buddy allocator with hints costs 6% more instructions on a commit step, 4% more on an eager create step and 16% more on a finalize step (cause not measured), and 7% fewer on an unmap step. Map and protect are unchanged. §12.2's "about 3 times faster" create and "10 to 16% faster" protect are wall-time figures and are withdrawn as comparisons. What step 1 was built for, allocation whose cost does not grow with the number of free ranges, is not exercised by these boots, which barely fragment memory.
- *The stop rule.* From here on, "5% slower" in this stage means guest instructions per step under `--icount`, one CPU, with a second boot of the base to show the spread.

**Step 2a by this measure.** It is built (lane `lane/m7s2a-slot`, `b63aac0` and `8379b6d`, by an Opus worker; 1,441 host tests pass; the coordinator read `slot.rs` and the whole diff of `manager.rs` against §12.3 and found no defect) and **not merged**. The first commit read every slot through a full unpack; the second added narrow readers (`live_generation`, `record_at`, `table_at`), which took map from 62% over the base to 18%, and protect from 79% to 17%. What is left at `8379b6d`, against main: map 16 to 20% more instructions per step, unmap 15%, protect 17%, commit 11%, finalize 29%; create 3% fewer. In return the grown table is half the size: the 2 GiB guest has 4,488 more free pages after growth (17.5 MiB, 0.9% of its RAM). That is well past the stop rule, so 2a waits. Next: count instructions per role-manager function on both builds (a throwaway diagnostic in lanes `lane/m7-diag-base` and `lane/m7-diag-2a`), then a revised design for the readers and the writer, or a decision by Mike that the footprint is worth the instructions.

### 12.6 Step 2a, revision 3: where the instructions go, and the readers and writers that remove them (2026-11-16)

**Counted per function.** An Opus worker put a throwaway probe around every role-manager function the stepped calls reach (lanes `lane/m7-diag-base` at `5417329` and `lane/m7-diag-2a` at `8379b6d`; never merged). Under `--icount` the guest's TSC advances one per instruction, so the probes count instructions. Four one-CPU boots, all passing: `.tmp/dev-boot/1794827525617251592` and `…7818864820641` (the build that leaves the smallest readers unprobed, used below), `…8128093426464` and `…8452999589495` (everything probed). The probes change inlining, so their per-step totals differ from §12.5's; they show where the cost is, not its exact size.

- *It reconciles.* The role functions' own instructions account for 99 to 109% of 2a's extra cost per step on map, unmap, protect, commit and finalize. Call counts per step agree between the builds within 2%: 2a calls nothing more often, each call costs more. The allocator is unchanged (284 per allocation, 682 to 686 per free).
- *Reading a whole record.* `record()` unpacks the stored role into a `FrameRole` (about 70 instructions) for callers that then look at one field. Per call, base against 2a: `index_root` 36 and 109, `index_entry` 58 and 136, `resolve_at` 104 and 176, `chain_run` 69 and 140, `free_index_slot` 66 and 123. These are 75 to 100% of the extra cost of a map, unmap or protect step and about two thirds of a commit or finalize step.
- *Writing a slot.* `occupy` 117 and 244, `vacate` 74 and 238: `store` is general (it compares the old and the new role to keep the counts and both lists right), and `vacate` first unpacks the record it is about to drop. This is the rest of commit (512 occupies a step) and finalize (about 500 vacates a step).
- *Already right.* The table lookups `8379b6d` gave narrow readers cost 5 to 9 instructions more than the base per call; `start_position` and `dynamic_slot_at` are slightly cheaper than the base.

**Design.** The slot layout, the lists and every result stay as §12.3 has them. Only how the hot paths read and write changes. `slot.rs` stays the only code that names `StoredRole` or touches a slot's fields.

1. *Readers that build no `FrameRole`* (in `slot.rs`, `#[inline]`, each matching on the stored role once and listing every variant):
   - `run_at(slot) -> Option<PageRange>`: the run of a live slot.
   - `head_kind_at(slot) -> HeadKind`, a fieldless enum `ObjectBacking | BackingAllocated | DeviceMmio | ImmutableModule | IndexRoot | Other` (`Other` for every other role and for a free slot).
   - `chain_at(slot) -> Option<ChainFacts { range, source }>` with `ChainSource::{Allocated(ChainLink), Backing(ChainLink), Module, Device}`; `None` for any other role.
   - `index_root_at(slot) -> Option<(PageRange, IndexRootLink)>` and `index_entry_at(slot) -> Option<IndexEntryFacts { range, node: bool, link: IndexEntryLink }>`.
2. *Their users.* `index_root` and `index_entry` return the range in place of the record (their callers read only `range`); `resolve_at`, `object_segments` and `release_chain` match on `head_kind_at`; `chain_run` on `chain_at`; `free_index_slot` uses `run_at`. Each keeps its checks and its errors exactly: the same error for the same input, in the same order. `record()` stays for the paths that are not hot (table staging, growth, the checks).
3. *Writers.* `occupy` of a role that is not of PML4 level writes the four fields in place after asserting the slot is free and at the head of the free list; `vacate` of a slot that is not of PML4 level reads its start, page count, generation and whether its role is dynamic (`StoredRole::is_dynamic`, the same truth table as `FrameRole::is_dynamic`), clears the start index, and writes the page count, the role tag and the free-list link in place, retiring the slot instead when its generation is spent. A slot of PML4 level, before or after, takes `store` as now, and so does `replace_record`. `store` remains the definition: the fast paths must leave exactly what it would.
4. *`index_memory` (§9).* `from_boot_map_in` writes the field, and ends with a destructuring of the manager that names every field, so a field added later without a write there does not compile. The constructor exists only for the kernel target, so there is no host test of it; the commit says so.

**Tests (host, behaviour).** Every reader against `record_at` for a slot of every role kind, a free slot and a retired one. The writers against `store`: two managers driven through the same long generated sequence of occupies, vacates and replacements over every role kind, PML4 candidates and roots and a spent generation included, one through `occupy`/`vacate` and one through `store` with the start index set by hand, compared slot for slot and list for list after every operation, and `check_invariants` on both. The index and chain tests that exist pass unchanged. Each new test is shown to fail against a real fault (at least: a fast vacate that does not push the free list, a fast occupy that keeps a stale table count, a reader that accepts the wrong role).

**Measured the same way as §12.5,** one CPU, main and the lane each booted, means from the raw totals. The stop rule is 5% on map, unmap, protect, commit and finalize. Expected from the counts: 1 to 3% left on each. If commit stays over, the lever held back is letting the three hot callers hand `occupy` a stored role directly, which skips the pack.

**As built (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `c19513d` merges `lane/m7s2a-slot`: `b63aac0` and `8379b6d` (§12.3 as first built), then `64abb16` (item 4), `6801ea6` (items 1 and 2) and `b9fa1fa` (item 3), all by Opus workers (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's designs. The last three and the merge are unsigned (the key was locked after a reboot) and are re-signed before any push. `abi/` and `crates/` are untouched; Wyrmroot stays at `658a85f`.

- *Built as designed*, with these differences. The test of PML4 level sits in two new functions of `slot.rs`, `occupy_slot` and `vacate_slot`, which `occupy` and `vacate` call, so that file stays the only writer of a slot's fields. The in-place occupy asserts the slot is free, which `store` never checked. The new error-path tests sit with the chain and index tests, and the writer test in its own file, `slot_writer_tests.rs`. A live slot holding the free role, which no writer produces, would now answer `WrongRole` where it answered `InvalidGrant`. No caller of `index_root` or `index_entry` read more than the range.
- *Review and security review* (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, 2026-11-15 and 2026-11-16, of `5417329..b9fa1fa`; the authors were the workers). No defect found. Read: `slot.rs` in full at `8379b6d` and its diff to `b9fa1fa`; the whole diff of `manager.rs`, `frame_roles.rs`, `chain.rs` and `index.rs`. Checked: every reader indexes with a slot number that `slot()` has bounds-checked, and an out-of-range index would panic, not read past the table; each narrow reader matches every stored variant by name, so a role added later does not fall into a default; the in-place writers leave the page count, the free-list link, the role tag and the count exactly as `store` does, the retired case included, and hand root-level slots to `store`; a vacated slot keeps its old payload bytes behind the free tag, which no reader looks at; errors and their order in the object and index paths are unchanged (the worker ran the new error tests against `8379b6d`'s code, where they pass). Page counts above `u32::MAX` are refused for external roles before anything is stored and cannot occur for allocator runs, since adoption refuses that much RAM. No `unsafe` was added outside the constructor's existing block. Risks kept, both Low: unlinking a root walks the root list, linear in the number of address spaces, at a root's retirement; `manager.rs` is 1,749 lines, past the 1,500-line guide, and is split when 2b reworks growth.
- *Tests (host).* 1,446 kernel tests pass (1,418 before 2a). For this revision: the five readers against `record_at` over every role kind, a free slot and a retired one; the object and index error paths; and the writers against `store` over 6,000 generated operations on a boot-sized and on a grown manager, compared after every operation. The worker showed each failing against a fault: a vacate that does not push the free list, an occupy that keeps a count, a reader that accepts the wrong role, an index entry of the wrong kind, a wrong dynamic flag.
- *Checks (coordinator, at `b9fa1fa`).* `host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` exit 0 with 1,446 passed; `xtask clippy` exit 0, ratchet 236 and 86 (87 before 2a); `xtask kernel-check` passed; `fmt --check` clean. The merge adds only this plan to that tree.
- *Boots (coordinator, development tier, Wyrmroot `658a85f`).* `dev-boot --check --smp --sdk` on four CPUs passed (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794831364380480664`); it logs `frame-role slots=506605` and `buddy free-pages=494914`, against 490,427 free pages before 2a: 17.5 MiB of the 2 GiB guest returned. Under `--icount` on one CPU, the lane (`…0839264752776`) against main before the merge (`…1099945121987`), thousands of guest instructions per step, with the worker's own pair beside it (`…0429261415463` against `…0158313865600`):

| Call | Main before 2a | 2a as merged | Difference | Worker's pair |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| map | 895.80 | 921.20 | +2.8% | −0.9% |
| unmap | 2,199.38 | 2,227.42 | +1.3% | +1.1% |
| protect | 3,007.06 | 3,125.05 | +3.9% | +3.9% |
| fill-in | 4.65 | 4.74 | +1.9% | +3.0% |
| commit | 1,189.51 | 1,137.89 | −4.3% | −4.7% |
| eager create | 1,201.61 | 1,188.05 | −1.1% | +0.6% |
| teardown | 111.11 | 110.96 | −0.1% | −0.1% |
| finalize | 737.01 | 709.70 | −3.7% | −3.7% |

- *Read with §12.5's limits.* Map and create move 1 to 3% between boots of one build, so only protect's 3.9% is a settled cost; nothing passes the 5% rule. These are instruction counts on one CPU, not times.
- *Not shown.* Hardware time, and with it whether the smaller table helps or hurts a real cache. A fragmented machine. The diagnostic lanes `lane/m7-diag-base` and `lane/m7-diag-2a` still exist with their unmerged commits; deleting them waits for Mike's word.

### 12.7 The BSP's timer re-arm must not stop the kernel: finding and design (2026-11-16; built and merged the same day: see "As built" at the end)

**What the diagnostic boot showed** (lane `lane/m7-diag-halt`, `f8b7c31`, cut from `c19513d`; `dev-boot --check --icount --smp`, run `.tmp/dev-boot/1794831654696256114`, `com1.log` lines 24 to 32 and `com1.cpu-state.txt`; the coordinator read both).

- The BSP halted in `time::live::halt_forever`, called from `service_current_rendezvous_latch` when `service_bsp_timer_request` returned an error. This is not the site of the two earlier boots (`arm_current_normal_quantum`). Both sites reach `reconcile_bsp_hardware_arm`.
- The error was `Faulted` from `reconcile_bsp_hardware_arm` after its eight tries. No clock sample, preparation or APIC write failed.
- In all eight tries the arm generation matched and the source revision had moved, by 1 or 2, between preparing the arm and rechecking it.
