- The earliest deadline was the same in all eight tries (`deadline_ns=43062480489`). So none of the twelve source changes that made the BSP lose had changed what the timer should be armed for.
- The clock moved 0.07 to 0.72 ms between tries, 2.5 ms over the eight.
- The same build booted with four CPUs and no icount passed (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794832454642438870`, 68 s): 84,714 calls of the loop, 7 retries, none exhausted.

**Reading** (the coordinator's, from the code; the parts not shown are marked).

- Each try samples the clock, prepares the arm under the time lock, writes the BSP's local APIC timer with the lock dropped, then takes the lock again and compares the source revision. Every accepted change to a deadline source moves the revision: a registered, cancelled or replaced wait deadline or Timer on any CPU, whether or not the earliest deadline changes.
- The recheck therefore asks "did anything change" where the arm only needs "is anything now due before what I armed".
- Under icount QEMU runs the four CPUs in turn on one thread. The clock deltas say the other CPUs ran 70 to 720 thousand instructions inside each try. That the APIC write is where the BSP's turn ends is inferred, not shown.
- The two earlier boots logged nothing, so their error is not shown to be this one.
- *Real hardware: not shown either way.* The window is one APIC write. Programs on the other CPUs that register and cancel waits or Timers in a loop move the revision without any privilege, and a hypervisor that pauses the BSP's virtual CPU inside the window widens it as icount does. This is treated as a fail-stop that unprivileged code may be able to cause, and is fixed rather than measured.

**Design.** Four changes to the loop and one to the halt, all in `kernel/src/time/`.

1. *The recheck asks whether the arm still covers the sources.* An arm is kept when its generation is still the desired one and the earliest deadline now, computed under the lock from the same clock sample the arm was prepared with, is not earlier than the deadline the arm was prepared for. A change that leaves the earliest deadline where it was, or moves it later, no longer costs a try. A timer armed earlier than it now needs to be fires once with nothing due and is re-armed by the interrupt path, as it already is after any cancel made on another CPU. `PhysicalArmSequence` gains this check as a pure function of the intent and the earliest deadline; `is_current` and the revision stay for the APs' private timers and the stop intents.
2. *Exhaustion defers; it is not a fault.* After eight tries that each lost to an earlier deadline, the loop prepares an arm for "now" under the lock (the one-shot's smallest count, the shot a deadline already in the past gets today), writes it, and returns success with the outcome "deferred". That shot cannot be later than any deadline, so it needs no recheck. It raises the BSP's timer interrupt as soon as interrupts are open; that path expires what is due and runs the loop again. The loop does at most nine APIC writes per call. It can be made to defer again only by other CPUs registering a still earlier deadline inside every one of eight consecutive windows, and each such round still expires due deadlines and runs their wakes. Real failures (clock, preparation, APIC access, the wrong CPU) are returned as before, and their callers still roll back or halt.
3. *The loop moves to host-compiled code.* Today it is in `time/live.rs`, which builds only for the kernel target, so the fault had no behaviour test. The loop becomes a function in `time/arbiter.rs` over a small trait with four operations (prepare an arm, write it, say whether it still covers the sources, prepare the immediate arm); `live.rs` implements the trait over the time state and the BSP's APIC, taking the lock inside each operation and never across the write. The tries stay at eight.
4. *Two counters.* How many tries were lost and how many calls deferred, counted only on those two paths, and printed with the step report as one line (`timer-arm retries=<n> deferred=<n>`), gated as the report's other lines are. They are how a boot shows the change: under four ordinary CPUs retries should fall from 7 and deferred should be 0.
5. *The halt says why.* `time::live::halt_forever` takes a short constant reason, distinct per call site, and writes one error record (`halt reason=<reason>`) through the early-record path, which only tries the output lock, before `cli; hlt`. The other `halt_forever`, in `arch/x86_64/syscall/live.rs`, is scheduled in §9.

*Not changed.* `reconcile_ap_scheduler_hardware` keeps its eight tries and its fault: an AP's scheduler source is changed only on its own CPU, by code that runs with interrupts off (read, not proven), so nothing can move it inside the window. The time from the clock sample to the APIC write still delays the shot by that much, as before.

**Tests** (host, in `time/arbiter.rs`, driving the real `PhysicalArmSequence` and deadline queues through a test implementation of the trait whose write step lets a "peer" change the sources).

- *The regression test for the fault.* A peer registers a later deadline and cancels another inside every window, so the revision moves every try and the earliest deadline never does: the loop commits on the first try with one write. Before the fix this is the eight losses and `Faulted`.
- A peer registers an earlier deadline once: one retry, and the shot left in the hardware is the one for the earlier deadline.
- A peer registers an earlier deadline in every window: the outcome is deferred, there are nine writes, the last is the smallest count, and no error.
- A peer cancels the earliest deadline: committed without a retry (the arm is early, not late).
- A failing write and a failing preparation are returned as errors, on the first try and on the immediate arm.
- The cover check alone: a superseded generation fails; an earlier earliest deadline fails; an equal or later one passes.

**Checks and boots.** The four canonical checks. Then `dev-boot --check`, `--check --smp`, `--check --icount` and `--check --icount --smp`, one at a time. The last is the boot that failed; it is run twice. If it fails for another reason, that failure is recorded with its evidence and filed by cause, and this fix is judged on the first three boots and the tests. The one-CPU icount boot is compared with main's step means under §12.5's 5% rule, since every arm now computes the earliest deadline once more.

**As built (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `7892e55` (one commit by the Opus worker in `lane/m7-timer-arm`, from this design), merged to main as `18fed40`. Both unsigned: the signing key was locked; to be re-signed before any push.

- *What it is.* `PhysicalArmSequence::still_covers`; `reconcile_hardware_arm` over the trait `HardwareArmPort` in `time/arbiter.rs` (eight tries, then the immediate arm and the outcome `Deferred`); `BspHardwareArm` in `time/live.rs`, which carries each arm's clock sample to its cover check and takes the time lock inside each operation; the counters and the report line `timer-arm retries=<n> deferred=<n>`; `halt_forever(reason)` with twenty distinct reasons. `reconcile_bsp_hardware_arm` kept its signature, so no caller changed.
- *Differences from the design.* The halt record is not written in guest-test builds: the early-record path does not exist there, as for the kernel's other early records. The sites number twenty, not "about twenty-one".
- *Review* (coordinator, against this design, at `7892e55`): nothing sent back. The loop, the cover check and the six tests are as designed. The worker put the revision compare back to see the regression test fail, and it did; the coordinator read the test and did not repeat that.
- *Security review* (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, 2026-11-16, at `7892e55`; Mike's standing instruction that the coordinator reviews). No finding to fix.
  - An arm kept after the revision moved cannot fire later than a deadline: it is kept only when no source holds a deadline earlier than the one it was prepared for. A deadline registered after the check is covered as before, by the registering CPU's request for timer service.
  - The immediate arm is the count-1 shot a past deadline already gets, so it adds no new hardware state.
  - *Residual, Low, by reading:* CPUs that register a still earlier deadline inside each of eight consecutive windows, again and again, keep the BSP in its timer interrupt path. Each round still expires due deadlines and runs their wakes, and it ends when they stop. This replaces a stop of the whole kernel. Scheduled in §9: M8 decides whether consecutive deferrals need a bound.
  - The halt record uses the output path that only tries its lock, at most 64 times, so a held lock cannot keep a CPU from halting; the record can then be missing.
  - No new `unsafe`; the lint ratchet is unchanged (236, 86).
- *Checks* (coordinator, at `7892e55`): `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy`, `xtask kernel-check` and `test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` exit 0; 1,452 tests passed, none failed.
- *Boots* (coordinator, lane at `7892e55`, Wyrmroot `658a85f`, SDK `56eb579`; all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`), with the main leg's counter line:

| Boot | Run under `.tmp/dev-boot/` | Seconds | retries | deferred |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `--check` | `1794833352292481809` | 95 | 0 | 0 |
| `--check --smp` | `1794833480142677080` | 65 | 0 | 0 |
| `--check --icount` | `1794833567082471914` | 238 | 0 | 0 |
| `--check --icount --smp` | `1794833827293590819` | 221 | 28 | 0 |
| `--check --icount --smp` | `1794834070244491538` | 194 | 17 | 0 |

- *Read these as follows.* The four-CPU icount boot failed three boots of three before the fix and passed two of two after it. The ordinary four-CPU boot lost 7 tries before (the diagnostic build's count) and none after. The 28 and 17 are tries lost to a deadline that really was earlier; no call lost eight in a row. The reboot leg of every boot printed 0 and 0.
- *Instructions per step* (one CPU, `--icount`; thousands; base is main at `1a42ab5`, run `1794832872071629475`, the same kernel code as `c19513d`):

| Step | Base | Fix | Change |
|---|---|---|---|
| map | 908.62 | 904.70 | −0.4% |
| unmap | 2,226.60 | 2,228.00 | +0.1% |
| protect | 3,125.05 | 3,137.48 | +0.4% |
| fill | 4.75 | 4.73 | −0.3% |
| commit | 1,135.75 | 1,136.99 | +0.1% |
| create | 1,209.13 | 1,188.04 | −1.7% |
| teardown | 110.89 | 109.10 | −1.6% |
| finalize | 710.20 | 710.18 | 0.0% |

  Nothing is near the 5% rule; create and teardown move this much between boots of one build (§12.5).
- *Not shown.* That the two halts at `arm_current_normal_quantum` were this fault: they logged nothing, and the only evidence is that the failing boot now passes. Whether the fault could be reached on real hardware. The deferred path and the halt record on a target: no boot took either; they rest on the host tests and on reading. Step instruction counts on four CPUs under icount are not compared with anything yet.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-timer-arm` is merged and retired. The diagnostic lanes `lane/m7-diag-base`, `lane/m7-diag-2a` and `lane/m7-diag-halt` still exist with their unmerged throwaway commits; deleting them waits for Mike's word.

### 12.8 Step 2b: what 2a as built changes for it (2026-11-16)

§12.4 is the design and stands. It was written before §12.6's revision of 2a, so the coordinator read it against the code at `18fed40`. These notes add to it; where they differ from §12.4 in a detail, they govern.

1. *The slot accessors.* Every slot read and write in `frame_roles/slot.rs` goes through `self.roles.as_slice()[slot]` or `as_mut_slice()` (27 places), §12.6's narrow readers and the in-place writers `occupy_slot` and `vacate_slot` among them. Those two calls go away. `ChunkedStore` and `GrowableStore` gain `entry(index) -> &T` beside §12.4's `get`, `get_mut` and `set`, so a reader that looks at two fields does not copy 48 bytes. Each accessor finds its slot once. The whole-table scans (`slot_views`, the list checks, the owner scans) walk the chunks with `iter`, not `get` per index.
2. *The start index.* `starts` is `Option<&'static mut [u32]>` today, used in three helpers; it becomes `Option<ChunkedStore<u32>>`.
3. *The test shift.* `growth_plan()` is `growth_plan_with(15)`; `growth_plan_with(min_shift)` computes `shift = max(min_shift, bits(slots - 1) - 6)`. Host tests pass a small minimum (3) so that tables of a few hundred slots are cut into many chunks. Production calls only `growth_plan()`.
4. *The staged runs' slots.* Each staged part is an ordinary allocation and takes one of the 4,096 boot slots, at most 65. A shortage is a staging failure like any other: the staged parts are freed and the boot stops as it does today when growth fails. No code assumes one `RoleTableStorage` record (read: the role appears only in the classification arms and in the one place that writes it).
5. *Where the pointer's contract lives.* `stage_growth_part` is the `unsafe fn`: its caller promises that `base` maps exactly that part's run, writable, 8-aligned and used by nothing else until the part is abandoned or for ever. Adoption relies on what staging was promised and says so in its `SAFETY` comments.
6. *`manager.rs` is split.* It is 1,749 lines. The growth code (the plan, staging, adoption, abandoning, the carving helper) and the start index helpers move to a new `frame_roles/storage.rs`. If `manager.rs` is still above 1,500 lines, `check_invariants` and its helpers move too. Nothing else moves.
7. *The slot-writer test runs on chunks.* §12.6's 6,000-operation differential test of the in-place writers against `store` also runs on a manager grown with the small shift, so occupied and vacated slots cross chunk boundaries.
8. *Measurement.* Base is main at `18fed40` under `--icount` on one CPU (two boots already taken: runs `1794832872071629475` and `1794833567082471914`, which differ only in the timer fix). The coordinator boots 2b the same way. If map, protect or commit is more than 5% above the base, the work stops there and the worker counts instructions per function as in §12.6, before anything is tuned. The four-CPU icount boot is run as well, now that it passes.
9. *The log lines.* `frame-role slots=<n>` gains `shift=<n>` and `groups=<n>`. Nothing in Wyrmroot reads these lines (checked: `dev-boot` does not match them).

### 12.9 Step 2b in chunks fails the stop rule: the counts, and a revised design (2026-11-16; the revision approved by Mike the same day, built and merged: see "As built" at the end)

**Built as designed.** An Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) built §12.4 with §12.8's notes in `lane/m7-step2b`: `e081e53` (the code) and `78914b5` (the tests; the worker's `c0a9efc` with one number in its message corrected by the coordinator, same tree). Not merged.

- *Checks (coordinator, at `78914b5`).* `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy`, `xtask kernel-check` and `host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` all exit 0: 1,464 tests pass (1,452 on main), ratchet 236 and 86.
- *Boots (coordinator, Wyrmroot `658a85f`, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`).* `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794836509102625794`), `--check --smp` (`…6632096712907`), `--check --icount` (`…6229407366838`), `--check --icount --smp` (`…6735837218226`, timer-arm retries 15, deferred 0). The table logs `frame-role slots=506628 shift=15 groups=16`.
- *Review (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, of `1a4dd09..78914b5`).* No defect found. The code does what §12.4 says: every check before any change in adoption, a refused part or plan changes nothing, the pointer contract sits in the one `unsafe fn`, and the tests are behaviour tests, each shown by the worker to fail against a fault.

**The cost (coordinator, one CPU under `--icount`, thousands of guest instructions per step).** Base is main: runs `…3567082471914` and `…2872071629475`, which agree within 0.4% on the first five rows.

| Call | Main | 2b in chunks | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| map | 904.70 | 1,075.03 | +18.8% |
| unmap | 2,228.00 | 2,643.32 | +18.6% |
| protect | 3,137.48 | 3,631.56 | +15.7% |
| commit | 1,136.99 | 1,321.25 | +16.2% |
| finalize | 710.18 | 1,005.26 | +41.5% |
| eager create | 1,188.04 | 1,239.70 | +4.3% |
| fill-in, teardown, release | | | within 2% |

The stop rule is 5%. The work stopped there, and the worker counted before anything was tuned (§12.8 note 8).

**Counted** (the worker, throwaway lanes `lane/m7-count-base` at `5740baa` and `lane/m7-count-2b` at `f4fa14a`, never merged; five one-CPU icount boots, `…7217257824925`, `…7584556030208`, `…7974678091775`, `…8296561786621`, `…8578889352454`; its full tables are kept in `.tmp/coord/2b-count-fine.md` and `2b-count-coarse.md`).

- *How many lookups a step makes in the chunked arrays* (plain counters, 2b): map 6,832, unmap 18,512, protect 23,556, commit 7,446, finalize 16,653. Slot reads are most of map, unmap and protect (two per slot looked at, one through `entry_checked` and one through `entry`); buddy tag reads are most of finalize (7,760 a step, about 16 per page freed).
- *What each lookup costs.* The step's extra divided by its lookups: 17.7 to 24.9 more instructions per lookup.
- *The lookup as compiled* (`llvm-objdump` of the two unprobed kernels). A slot read up to the load of its page count: 11 instructions on main, 21 in chunks (the shift is loaded and the mask rebuilt on every lookup, two bounds checks, two pointer loads). A tag read in the free path: 4 on main, 15 in chunks, four of them reloads from the stack.
- *The rest is how the callers are now built.* On main `validate_table_identity` has `table_facts`, `slot`, `live_generation` and `table_at` inlined, and its two reads of one slot share one bounds check and one address. In chunks `table_facts` is an out-of-line call returning through the stack that does two complete chunk lookups of the same slot. Per call: `validate_table_identity` 59 and 119, `is_on_demand` 34 and about 88, `table_identity` about 155 and 214. Why the compiler stopped inlining and merging there is the worker's inference (the larger accessors crossed its limits), not counted.
- *It reconciles.* With probes on the 18 public entry points only (inlining as in the unprobed kernel), the probed functions hold 98% of the extra on map, unmap, protect and finalize. On commit they hold 126%: not reconciled; the number of commit steps differs by up to 5% between those boots.
- *Calls per step are the same in both kernels.* Chunks call nothing more often; each lookup costs more.

**What the counts rule out.** At the 5% rule a step may spend this many more instructions per lookup: map 6.6, unmap 6.0, protect 6.7, commit 7.6, finalize 2.1. The chunk lookup alone, as compiled, adds 10 or 11. A tighter accessor (a constant shift, a directory held inline) would have to come in under those numbers after inlining, on every hot caller, and finalize leaves room for two instructions per tag read. That has not been built or measured, so it is not ruled out by a count; it is set aside because the next design needs no accessor at all.

**Revised design: the table in pieces of RAM, at one virtual range.** §12.4 decision 1 set this aside for its cost in page tables and proofs. The counts change that choice: with it the slot, start-index, tag and link arrays are plain slices again, and `slot.rs`, `buddy.rs` and the start-index helpers stay exactly the code on main, which is the code the base runs measured. The table's RAM is still taken in pieces, so §9's R1 is still closed. The work starts from main, not from the chunked lane; `ChunkedStore` is not taken. From that lane come the split of `manager.rs`, the staging of runs and its tests, reworked as below.

1. *The table map* (new `arch/x86_64/mm/table_map.rs`, host-compiled like `direct_map.rs`). A kernel range at `TABLE_MAP_BASE = 0xffff_c000_0000_0000` (PML4 slot 384, the first slot after the direct map's 128; the scratch window is slot 510 and the kernel image 511; the worker confirms 384 and 385 are unused), two PML4 slots long: 1 TiB, which holds the table of the 64 TiB the direct map can span (1.49% of it). `TableMap` records the pieces in order, each a physical start and a length in whole pages, at most `DIRECT_MAP_RANGE_CAPACITY` of them; piece `k` is mapped at the base plus the lengths of the pieces before it. Its leaves are read-write, no-execute and supervisor-only, the direct map's flags. Each leaf is the largest of 1 GiB (when the CPU has them), 2 MiB and 4 KiB for which the virtual address and the physical address are both aligned and the leaf ends inside its piece. `translate(virtual page)` gives the physical page and the bytes left in its piece. One `TABLE_MAP`, filled once while the deep root is built and read-only from then on.
2. *The pieces are taken while the root is built, before activation.* The layout needs only the allocator's initial frames, which are fixed at boot, and the runs need not be touched before activation. After `build_direct_map`, `build_table_map` asks the manager for `grown_layout()` (as on main: one layout, offsets from one base) and takes its pages in two passes. First, pieces aligned to 2 MiB whose lengths are multiples of 2 MiB, each the largest the allocator has (at most what is left), until less than 2 MiB is left or no aligned free run remains. Then the rest in pieces of any page alignment, largest first. On an ordinary map this is one large piece and a tail under 2 MiB. Each run is staged with the manager (item 4), then the map is filled from the staged runs and every leaf is written with `ensure_table`, as `build_direct_map` writes its own. A failure anywhere is a failed root build, which stops the boot as every other build failure does; so there is no abandoning of staged runs, and the chunked lane's `abandon_growth` is not taken.
3. *The allocator, boot phase only.* `allocate_largest_aligned(max_pages, align_pages)` on `PhysicalFrameAllocator` and the manager: among the free ranges, the one whose interior aligned to `align_pages` is longest; it takes `min(max_pages, that interior rounded down to a multiple of align_pages)` from the first aligned page, or reports that nothing fits. It may split a free range in two; the free list's bound holds (within one initial range the free ranges are at most its live runs plus one, wherever a run lies; the worker checks this against `free_range_bound`'s proof in `memory/physical.rs` and stops if it does not hold). Refused once the buddy allocator has taken over.
4. *The manager* (`frame_roles/storage.rs`, new, taking the growth code and the start-index helpers out of `manager.rs`, which falls under 1,500 lines).
   - `stage_growth_part(grant)` records a run as the next piece. Safe: it takes no pointer. It refuses, returning the grant and changing nothing, a full staging array (`MAX_GROWTH_PARTS`, equal to the table map's capacity), a grant that is not a live uninitialised allocation, and any part once the table is grown.
   - `staged_growth_parts()` yields each staged run's start and length, in order.
   - `unsafe fn adopt_storage(base)`. Its caller promises that `base` maps the staged runs back to back in staging order, writable, 8-aligned, used by nothing else, for the rest of the kernel's life. Every check comes before any change: not grown, the staged runs' pages equal to the layout's, every staged grant still valid, the base aligned, the buddy storage acceptable. Then it does what main's adoption does from one base, and every staged run takes the `RoleTableStorage` role.
   - `check_invariants` gains: the staged parts are a prefix of the array; before growth each is still its uninitialised allocation and no run holds the table's role; after it the runs holding the table's role are exactly the staged parts.
   - `GrowableStore`, the start index and `BuddyStorage` keep main's types. `growable.rs`'s module comment is corrected (§9).
5. *The graph check* (`activation/graph.rs`). `validate_table_map_entry`, beside `validate_direct_entry`: a leaf in the table map's slots must have exactly the leaf flags for its size (1 GiB only when the CPU has them), map exactly `translate` of its address, and end inside its piece; a table there must cover some mapped address. The walk takes that branch for addresses in the table map's slots when a table map is bound; with none bound, an entry there fails as an extra leaf does today. After the walk the leaves' bytes must equal the map's (a new `TableMapCoverage` error). Before it: no kernel segment and no scratch page may lie in the table map's slots, and the map's pieces must be exactly the manager's staged runs, in order. The scratch bindings carry the map as they carry the direct map.
6. *Adoption* (`grow_frame_roles`, after activation as now). It calls `adopt_storage(TABLE_MAP_BASE)`. Its `SAFETY` comment rests on item 5: the active root passed the check that the table map's leaves cover the staged runs exactly and in order. Other roots copy the kernel half's 256 PML4 entries from the primordial root (`KernelHalfBinding`), so they have the map without further work. No TLB entry needs flushing: the mapping exists before the root is ever loaded. The table's frames also lie in the direct map, as every allocator frame does and as the whole table does on main; nothing hands out a direct-map address for a run holding the table's role.
7. *The log line.* `frame-role slots=<n> pieces=<n>`.

**Tests (host, behaviour).**

- `table_map.rs`: over generated piece lists, the leaves cover every piece exactly once, each aligned in both spaces, inside its piece and the largest that fits; `translate` agrees with them; `push` refuses an empty or unaligned piece, one overlapping an earlier piece, one past the capacity and one past the span.
- Graph: every leaf the map emits is accepted; refused are a user or executable leaf, a wrong frame, a leaf running past its piece, a 1 GiB leaf without the CPU feature, a table over nothing mapped, a present entry in slot 384 with no map bound, leaves short of the map, and pieces that are not the manager's staged runs. These run in the model the direct map's graph tests use.
- Allocator: the aligned allocation starts aligned, is a multiple of the alignment, is never more than asked, conserves free pages, keeps the invariants on generated maps, and is refused after growth.
- Manager: growth from several staged runs laid back to back in one host buffer keeps identities, indexes every start and gives every run the table's role; main's grown-phase tests and §12.6's 6,000-operation writer test pass on it unchanged; each staging and adoption refusal changes nothing; growth succeeds on a map whose largest free range is smaller than the table.
- Each new check is shown to fail against a real fault, at least: a leaf mapped at the wrong offset of its piece, adoption accepting fewer staged pages than the layout, a staged run left without the table's role, an aligned allocation that starts unaligned.
- `build_table_map` and `grow_frame_roles` are target-only and have no host test. Every boot runs the graph check over the map they build, and the dev-boot guest's table should come out as two pieces (the tail), which the log line shows; if so the join between pieces is used on every boot.

**Checks and measurement.** The four commands and the four boots, as for the chunked build. The stop rule is 5% on map, unmap, protect, commit and finalize against main's runs above. The hot paths are main's source unchanged; whether the counts come out the same is measured, not assumed. If a row is over, the work stops and the cause is counted first.

**Review.** The coordinator reviews the diff and does the security review: this adds a kernel mapping, a region to the activation proof and an `unsafe` contract at adoption.

**Not shown by this design, and where it goes.** On main the table is reached through the direct map, in 1 GiB pages where the CPU has them; here it is reached in 2 MiB pages unless a piece happens to be aligned for 1 GiB. Instruction counts cannot show what that costs a real TLB. It is in §9 for M8, with the other items that need a time on hardware. A map with almost no free run aligned to 2 MiB falls to 4 KiB leaves and can run out of table frames (2,048) or boot slots; the boot then stops with a capacity error, as it stops on main today on any map without one run for the whole table.

**Lanes kept at the time of the design** (all unmerged): `lane/m7-step2b` at `78914b5` (the chunked build; its staging code and tests were the starting material for item 4), `lane/m7-count-base` at `5740baa` and `lane/m7-count-2b` at `f4fa14a` (the counts), and the three diagnostic lanes of §12.6 and §12.7. "As built" below says where they stand.

**As built (2026-11-16).** Mike approved the revision the same day ("Approve 12.9"). Deepwyrm `9d62d5c` merges `lane/m7-table-map`: `61fdfbc` (the aligned boot-phase allocation, item 3), `9944ead` (the table map, item 1), `6fd9700` (items 2 and 4 to 7 and their tests) and `843fd24` (one more graph test), by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's design. All five commits are unsigned (the key is locked) and are re-signed before any push. `abi/` and `crates/` are untouched; Wyrmroot stays at `658a85f`.

- *Built as designed*, with these differences. The graph check also requires the frame a large leaf maps to be aligned to the leaf's size; without that a 2 MiB leaf over unaligned RAM passed the "maps exactly `translate`" check (the worker's test shows it). `TableMapCoverage` reports both leaves short of the map and pieces that are not the staged runs. `grow_frame_roles` compares the map's pieces with the staged runs again before adopting. `adopt_storage` returns a plain error and leaves the staged runs where they are (a refusal stops the boot). The three start-index helpers moved to `storage.rs` with their bodies unchanged and gained `pub(super)`. `frame_roles/slot_tests.rs` was touched, to call the new test helper. `manager.rs` is 1,504 lines (1,749 before).
- *What the design asked to be verified* (the worker, by reading). PML4 slots 384 and 385 were unused: the direct map is 256 to 383, the scratch window is fixed at slot 510 by the handoff contract and the image links in slot 511; a compile-time assertion now holds the table map between them. An allocation from the middle of a free range keeps the free list within `free_range_bound`, whose proof text now says so; the code refuses the split, before any change, when the list is full. A failed root build and a failed growth both panic in `lib.rs`; no caller survives either.
- *Review and security review* (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, 2026-11-16, of `6459f76..843fd24`; the author was the worker). No defect found. Read: `table_map.rs`, `storage.rs`, and the whole diff of `build.rs`, `graph.rs`, `activation.rs`, `physical.rs`, `frame_roles.rs` and `manager.rs`. Checked:
  - `slot.rs` and `buddy.rs` have no diff against main, and `GrowableStore`, the start index and `BuddyStorage` keep their types, so the hot paths are main's.
  - Adoption makes every check before any change: not grown, the staged runs' pages exactly the layout's, every staged grant still its live allocation, the base aligned, the buddy storage acceptable. From there it is main's adoption, with every staged run given the table's role.
  - The `unsafe` contract at adoption rests on the graph check, which the root passes before it is ever loaded: each leaf in slots 384 and 385 has exactly the direct map's flags for its size (so supervisor-only, writable, never executable), maps exactly the frame the map gives for its address, is aligned for its size and ends inside its piece; the leaves' bytes equal the map's; the pieces are the manager's staged runs in order; no kernel segment or scratch page lies in those slots; and with no map bound any entry there fails. Tables there are checked as owned tables of the primordial owner, as the direct map's are.
  - Other roots copy all 256 kernel PML4 entries from the primordial root, so they hold the same map; nothing changes it after activation, so no TLB entry is ever stale.
  - The piece loop ends: every allocation takes at least one page and the staging array is bounded. Sizes and addresses use checked arithmetic, and the map refuses a piece that overflows, overlaps an earlier piece or passes 1 TiB in total.
  - Risks kept, all Low. The table's frames also lie in the direct map, as on main; no API hands out a direct-map address for a run holding the table's role. `activation/tests.rs` is 3,792 lines; it was 3,408 before this change, already past the 1,500-line guide (not split here). Commits `61fdfbc` and `9944ead` were not checked on their own; only the lane's tip was.
- *Tests (host).* 1,477 kernel tests pass (1,452 before). New: the aligned allocation on generated maps; the table map's leaves and `translate` on generated pieces and `push`'s refusals; eleven graph tests (the builder's shape accepted with and without 1 GiB pages, and each refusal of item 5, the segment and scratch-page layout check included); growth from seven staged runs, the staging and adoption refusals, growth on a map whose largest free range is smaller than the table, and the storage invariant. Main's grown-phase tests and §12.6's writer test now grow from three staged runs and assert what they asserted. The worker showed the new checks failing against fourteen injected faults, the four this design named among them.
- *Checks (coordinator).* At `6fd9700`: `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86), `xtask kernel-check` and `host test -p deepwyrm-kernel --no-fail-fast` (1,476 passed) all exit 0. At `843fd24`, which adds one test: fmt, clippy and the tests again, 1,477 passed. The merge's tree is `843fd24`'s.
- *Boots (coordinator, development tier, Wyrmroot `658a85f`, at `6fd9700`, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`).* `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794847774365771863`), `--check --smp` (`…7894240623178`), `--check --icount` (`…7514915420574`) and `--check --icount --smp` (`…7985571220484`; timer-arm retries 18, deferred 0). Each logs `frame-role slots=506623 pieces=2` (506,599 on four CPUs): one aligned piece and the tail, so the join between pieces is used on every boot. Free pages after growth: 494,929, against 494,941 on main.
- *Instruction counts* (one CPU under `--icount`, thousands of guest instructions per step; main is run `…3567082471914`, with main's second boot `…2872071629475` beside it):

| Call | Main | Main, second boot | Table map | Difference from main |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| map | 904.70 | 908.62 | 902.48 | −0.2% |
| unmap | 2,228.00 | 2,226.60 | 2,228.33 | 0.0% |
| protect | 3,137.48 | 3,125.05 | 3,138.38 | 0.0% |
| commit | 1,136.99 | 1,135.75 | 1,139.54 | +0.2% |
| finalize | 710.18 | 710.20 | 714.10 | +0.6% |
| eager create | 1,188.04 | 1,209.13 | 1,219.43 | +2.6% |

- *Read with §12.5's limits.* Nothing passes the 5% rule. Eager create and teardown move 1.6 to 1.8% between two boots of main, so their rows show no settled cost. These are instruction counts on one CPU, not times.
- *Not shown.* Hardware time, and with it the TLB cost of reaching the table in 2 MiB pages (§9, M8). A boot whose table has more than two pieces, or 4 KiB leaves beyond the tail: host tests cover the map, the check and the manager on such shapes, but `build_table_map` and `grow_frame_roles` are target-only and ran only on the dev-boot guest's map. A machine with 1 GiB of table or more.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-table-map` is merged and retired. The six throwaway lanes were deleted the same day on Mike's word, each checked clean and at its recorded commit first: `lane/m7-step2b` (`78914b5`), `lane/m7-count-base` (`5740baa`), `lane/m7-count-2b` (`f4fa14a`), `lane/m7-diag-base` (`6ab6259`), `lane/m7-diag-2a` (`54ed5b8`) and `lane/m7-diag-halt` (`f8b7c31`). Their commits are no longer on any branch; what they found is in §12.6, §12.7 and this section. No lane remains.

### 12.10 Step 3: an index for fragmented eager backings: design (2026-11-16; approved by Mike the same day; both parts built and merged: see "Part 1 as built" and "Part 2 as built" at the end)

Read for this design: Deepwyrm `0696682` (`memory/frame_roles/chain.rs` and `index.rs` in full; `frame_roles.rs`'s grant, allocation and cursor types; `memory/object.rs`'s finalization and owed queue; `arch/x86_64/mm/journal/publisher.rs`'s batch validation and page descriptor; the callers of every chain and index entry point), §6.2 to §6.8, §7.3, §8.3, §8.4 and §10.12 of this plan, and, through a read-only survey subagent, Wyrmroot `658a85f` (`userspace/hello/src/stream_main.rs`, `tools/xtask/src/dev_boot/memory.rs`, `userspace/system-init/src/job_memory.rs` and `jobs.rs`) and Deepwyrm's pool sizes (`kernel/arch/x86_64/pools.toml`).

**What the code does today, by reading.** An eager backing is a chain of runs, and the buddy allocator gives each `allocate_backing` the largest free block first (`take_run`), so a backing has many runs only when memory is fragmented. For a backing of N runs:

- Every map, unmap and protect step checks its mappings with `validate_object_range`, which for a chain calls `object_segments`, which calls `chain_totals`: a walk of all N runs, per mapping, per step.
- Every step starts its page lookups with a cursor at the chain's head (`BackingCursor::for_backing`), so the step's first lookup walks every run before its offset: up to N.
- A creation's zeroing step (`zero_backing_pages`) calls `chain_totals` and then walks from the head to its first page.
- So a call over the whole object in S steps reads about S × N runs where an unfragmented one reads S. This is §9's R2.
- Two holds are not steps at all: `allocate_backing` takes all N runs in one hold, and finalization frees all N in one hold (`cancel_object_backing` → `release_chain`), where an on-demand backing is freed in owed steps (§10.12).

None of this has been measured: no check boot has ever had a fragmented eager object, and every "as built" in this plan says so. How much a step costs at a given N is therefore not known, and part 1 finds out before part 2 is built.

**Part 1: a fragmented guest (Wyrmroot), built first.**

Why it takes a recipe: an eager object lands in fragments only once every larger free block is gone, a job's budget is capped at 512 MiB in the dev-boot config, the jobs together are limited to free memory less a 256 MiB reserve, a region has 64 mapping slots and a job 64 memory objects. So fragments are made without mappings, and other jobs hold the large blocks.

1. *Fragment.* A new `bin/hello fragmented` mode creates two on-demand objects, P (pins) and H (holes), and commits their pages alternately with `memory_object_commit`: one page of P, then seven of H, about 16,000 times. Single-page allocations from the buddy allocator come out ascending and adjacent (`Buddy::take` returns a block's lowest pages), so the frames interleave: one pinned, seven hole. P ends at about 64 MiB and H at about 448 MiB, together the job's 512 MiB. If a commit needs a mapping, each object is mapped once; the worker confirms which.
2. *Free the holes.* It closes H. Finalization frees H's pages in owed steps; the mode waits until its usage has fallen. Each hole of seven pages is now free blocks of 1, 2 and 4 pages that cannot merge past the pinned page.
3. *Soak the large blocks.* Before step 1 the check starts other jobs in the existing `hold` mode, sized from the free count the check already reads, so that the free memory outside the holes is about 96 MiB less than the object of step 4. The window for that size is about 190 MiB wide (from the budget cap, the reserve and P), so it need not be exact.
4. *Measure.* The mode creates one eager object C of about 448 MiB, which takes the remaining large blocks and then about 96 MiB of holes, in runs of at most four pages: on the order of ten thousand runs. It maps it, touches every page (checking each reads zero), protects it read-only, reads back, unmaps and closes it, and prints one line with the time of each call and the object's run count. The run count comes from the kernel's step report or a new field of an existing status reply; the worker proposes which, and a new field is an ABI question that comes back to the coordinator before it is built.
5. *In the check.* `dev-boot --check` runs it after the existing eager probe, requires `fragmented ok` and that usage returns to the base afterwards, and echoes the timing line. Under `--icount` those times are guest instructions. It is also the first guest run of a fragmented eager object at all.
6. *Measured on main first.* The coordinator boots main with the probe, one CPU under `--icount`, twice. If the fragmented object's map, protect, unmap and zeroing cost no more than 10% above the same calls on the unfragmented 256 MiB object, per page, part 2 is not built as designed and this section is revised with the numbers. Otherwise the numbers are the base for part 2.

Not known until it is built: how long the probe adds to a check boot (about 32,000 commit calls, the soak jobs' zeroing, and on main the cost being measured). If it adds more than about a minute to the plain check, the sizes are cut or it runs only under `--icount`, and the coordinator says which.

**Part 2: the indexed backing (Deepwyrm).** A backing whose allocation took more than 16 runs (`INDEX_RUN_THRESHOLD`, §6.8's figure) is held as an index, not a chain: M6's 512-way radix tree keyed by object page (§8.3), with leaves that name runs. A backing of 16 runs or fewer stays a chain and runs today's code unchanged. On the dev-boot guest the largest free block is 512 MiB, so every backing the check makes today is one run or a few.

1. *Shape.* The backing's identity is its index root, as an on-demand backing's is. `IndexRootLink` gains a state: `OnDemand`, `EagerAllocated` (runs taken, not yet zeroed) or `Eager`. Every object page's leaf entry holds the raw identity of the run containing that page, so a run of n pages is named by n entries. A run under an eager root holds the `ObjectPage` role with its first object page as its place and any length; under an on-demand root an `ObjectPage` is still exactly one page. No role variant is added, so the stored-role matches in `slot.rs` keep their arms; only the packing of the root's link changes there.
2. *Lookup.* `resolve_at` already sends an index root to `index_resolve`. The entry check becomes: the entry's owner is this root, and either the root is on demand and the entry is one page at exactly this place (today's rule), or the root is `Eager` and the run's place is at or below the page and the page lies inside the run. The physical address is the run's start plus the page's offset in it. A lookup reads at most four nodes whatever N is. An `EagerAllocated` root resolves for nobody but the zeroing below.
3. *Validation.* `validate_object_range` on an index root is the bounds check against the root's page count that on-demand backings already use: no walk.
4. *Creation.* `allocate_backing` builds the chain as now. If it took more than 16 runs and the manager has its index memory, it converts in the same hold, in two passes. First it takes and zeroes the root and every node the object's size needs and links them, which is the only part that can fail; on failure the nodes and the still-whole chain are freed and the allocation fails as it does today. Then, with nothing left that can fail, it walks the chain once, writes each run's entries and gives each run its `ObjectPage` role. With no index memory (before growth) the backing stays a chain. `BackingAllocation` and `ObjectBackingGrant` carry the root's identity, and the grant's kind is a new `ObjectBackingKind::AllocatorIndexed`.
5. *Zeroing.* `zero_backing_pages` on an indexed allocation resolves its first page through the index and goes run by run: the pages of its range plus the runs it crosses. `assume_backing_zeroed` changes the root's state from `EagerAllocated` to `Eager`, one write where a chain re-roles every run.
6. *Freeing in steps.* `defer_finalization` also queues a page-backed object whose backing is indexed, and `release_index_step` frees an eager root's runs as it frees pages: at the cursor's entry it frees the whole run, counts its pages as freed and moves the cursor to the run's end, and frees each node when the cursor passes its end, as now. `cancel_backing_allocation` (a failed creation) and the final `cancel_object_backing` free through `release_index`.
7. *What must refuse an eager root.* `is_on_demand`, `commit_on_demand_page`, both commit-cost functions, `decommit_on_demand_range` and `on_demand_frames` take an on-demand root only; an eager root is `WrongRole` to them (and `false` to `is_on_demand`), so the fault path, release on unmap and commit never treat an eager object as on demand. `FrameRole::kind` reports the root by its state.
8. *Charging (a default, Mike may change it).* An eager object is charged its pages at creation, before allocation, as now. The index's nodes, at most one page per 2 MiB of object plus three, are not charged: they are not known until the allocation has found more than 16 runs. The owed step lowers the object's charge by the run pages it frees, not by nodes.
9. *Invariants.* `check_indexes` gains: under an `Eager` or `EagerAllocated` root every page below the root's count has an entry, each run is reached at its first page and counted once, the runs' pages sum to the root's count, and each entry inside a run names that run. Under an on-demand root an `ObjectPage` is one page, as now.

**Left as it is, and where it goes.** `allocate_backing` still takes all N runs in one hold, and the conversion adds one entry write per page to that hold. That is step 3b: allocation in steps, which changes the creation call's phases; it is designed after part 1 has measured how long that hold is on a fragmented guest. A walk over an indexed backing pays a full index lookup per page, where a chain's cursor pays one step; part 1's probe measures it, and a cursor that remembers its leaf is the lever if a step is slow.

**Tests (host, behaviour).**

- An indexed backing made on a fragmented model allocator: every page resolves to the frame its chain would have given (compared with the chain of the same allocation before conversion); a lookup reads at most four nodes, counted through the test's `IndexMemory`, for 17 runs and for thousands; pages past the end and a stale or foreign root are refused.
- Conversion: 16 runs stay a chain and 17 become an index; a node allocation failing at each position frees everything and leaves the allocator and the invariants as before; with no index memory the backing stays a chain.
- Zeroing by ranges that start and end inside runs zeroes each page once; the state change makes the backing resolvable and not before.
- Finalization in steps frees every run and node across step boundaries that fall inside a run and inside a leaf, with the pages freed summing to the object's.
- Every on-demand entry point refuses an eager root, and an on-demand root still refuses a multi-page entry.
- M3's chain tests, M6's index tests and the object tests pass unchanged in what they assert.
- Each new check is shown to fail against a real fault, at least: a lookup that ignores the page's offset in its run, a conversion that leaves one run on the chain, a step that frees a run twice, an on-demand entry point that accepts an eager root.

**Checks and measurement.** The four commands and the four boots. Unfragmented, against main: the 5% stop rule on every row of §12.9's table, on-demand rows included, since `index.rs` and the root's packing change. Fragmented, against part 1's numbers for main: the probe's map, protect, unmap, zeroing and close. What part 2 must show is that those no longer grow with the run count; the figure it is held to is set when part 1's numbers exist.

**Review.** The coordinator reviews both parts and does the security review of part 2: it changes which frames a mapping, a DMA mapping and a boot write can reach, and how an object's memory is freed.

**Decisions this asks of Mike.** Approve the two parts in this order (the probe, measure, then the index). The defaults taken: the threshold of 16 runs, uncharged index nodes, and the probe as part of every `dev-boot --check`.

**Part 1 as built and measured (2026-11-16).** Mike approved the design ("Approve and build part 1"). Deepwyrm `269843f` merges `b1e7ca2` (the step report's `backing-runs longest=<runs> pages=<pages>` line: the eager backing with the most runs since boot) and Wyrmroot `3ffdee3` merges `fa8fc1a`, `21ae618`, `a3099dc` and `60fbdfe` (the probe), all by Opus workers (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's briefs. Every commit is unsigned (the key is locked) and is re-signed before any push. No ABI changed; Wyrmroot's check now needs a Deepwyrm at `269843f` or later, which `dev-boot` builds from the local checkout.

- *How the probe differs from the recipe above, and why.* A shell runs at most three jobs at once (`MAX_JOBS_PER_SHELL`, `run` jobs included), not four, so soak jobs could not hold the large blocks. Two jobs fragment instead, one after the other, and the probe runs after the CPU hogs end, when all three slots are free. Each `fragment` job commits one pinned page then three hole pages up to its budget, closes the holes, and then takes an eager object that brings it to its budget, which is the sign the harness waits for. The second job first commits a 382 MiB filler that takes the first job's holes, so its own pins land in large blocks, and gives the filler back with its holes. The measuring job takes a 60 MiB eager top-up and then the 448 MiB object. Holes total 475.5 MiB; about 79 MiB of the measured object comes from them.
- *The baseline is the same object in the same place.* The first build timed the unfragmented object inside the eager probe, while the hogs ran; the probe's clock keeps running while a hog has the CPU, and that line came out slower than the fragmented one. `hello unfragmented` now runs the same code as `hello fragmented` right before the first fragmenter, with the hogs ended and usage back at the base.
- *What the check requires.* `unfragmented ok` and `fragmented ok` with a normal exit and the planned budget, usage back to the base afterwards, and after the boot a `backing-runs longest` of at least 1,000. It echoes both timing lines and the sizes it computed.
- *Review (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2).* Read: both diffs whole. No defect found. The kernel change is one relaxed atomic and a report line; the probe is guest and harness code and loosens no existing check. `hello eager` keeps its three sizes and gains `close-us=`.
- *Checks (coordinator).* Deepwyrm at `b1e7ca2`: `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86), `xtask kernel-check` and 1,478 kernel tests pass; one check boot passes (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794850961897613165`) and logs `backing-runs longest=1 pages=13`, so no eager object in the check before the probe had more than one run. Wyrmroot at `60fbdfe`: `xtask test fmt`, `clippy` and `unit` pass. Boots with both, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`: `--check --icount` twice (`…3685325317555`, `…3983611981301`), `--check` (`…4275357835265`, 107 s against 97 s before the probe, one sample each) and `--check --smp` (`…4404778267192`).
- *The measurement* (main, one CPU under `--icount`; thousands of guest instructions for the whole call on one 448 MiB eager object; the fragmented object has 10,171 runs of at most two pages over its last 79 MiB):

| Call | Unfragmented, boot 1 | Fragmented, boot 1 | Fragmented, boot 2 | Change (boot 1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| create | 81,716 | 118,456 | 165,486 | +45% |
| map | 814,140 | 1,295,288 | 1,249,692 | +59% |
| protect | 765,132 | 1,709,942 | 1,709,943 | +123% |
| unmap | 721,024 | 1,241,618 | 1,241,619 | +72% |
| close | 138 | 9,508 | 9,508 | 69 times |
| touch every page | 716 | 722 | 722 | +1% |

- *Read.* The unfragmented column and the worker's own boot agree with boot 1 within 0.02% on every row. Protect, unmap and close repeat exactly between boots; the fragmented create and map do not (create 118,456 and 165,486, map 1,295,288 and 1,249,692), and why is not known. The gate of part 1 item 6 was 10%: it is passed several times over, so part 2 is built. A map is 224 steps of 2 MiB, so a fragmented step pays about 2.1 million more instructions on map, 2.3 on unmap and 4.2 on protect, which is 210 to 415 per run of the backing per step. The close frees all 10,171 runs in one hold of 9.5 million instructions, about 930 a run. These are instruction counts on one CPU, not times, and one fragmentation level: that the cost grows with the run count is from reading the code, not from a second measurement.
- *The step report's means have a new base.* The probe's calls are in the whole-boot means: against the boots before it, map is 76% up, protect 57%, unmap 35%, create 37%, and commit 95% down (about 81,000 one-page commit steps). Boots before and after the probe do not compare. Part 2's unfragmented check compares the probe's `unfragmented` line and the on-demand rows of probe-carrying boots of main (`…3685325317555`, `…3983611981301`) with the same from its lane.
- *The figures part 2 is held to* (set now, as the design said): on the fragmented object, map, protect and unmap within 15% of the unfragmented object in the same boot; create no higher than main's fragmented create (118,456, the lower of the two boots); the close call itself within 15% of the unfragmented close plus what queueing costs, with the freeing in owed steps none longer than the longest finalize step main shows; and the unfragmented line within 5% of main's. If one is missed the work stops and the cause is counted before anything is tuned.
- *Not shown.* Hardware. A second run count. Whether an index node or another kernel page lands among the pins (it would act as one more pin). The check's times on a loaded host: the 10 seconds the probe adds is from single boots.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-run-count` (Deepwyrm) and `lane/m7-frag-probe` (Wyrmroot) are merged and retired; no lane remains.

**Part 2 as built and measured (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `9114920` merges `lane/m7-index` (`baf6402`, `016bb0e`, `badf93b`, then the review round `c339b92`, `f9a186b`, `d7eae39`), built by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's brief. Every commit is unsigned (the key is locked) and is re-signed before any push. No ABI changed and Wyrmroot is unchanged.

- *What it is.* `allocate_backing` converts a backing of more than 16 runs (`INDEX_RUN_THRESHOLD`) to an index: the root and every node are taken, zeroed and linked first, the only part that can fail, and then one walk of the chain writes each run's entries and gives the run the `ObjectPage` role with its first object page as its place. The root carries a state: `OnDemand`, `EagerAllocated` (runs taken, not zeroed: it resolves for its zeroing only), `Eager`, and `EagerFreeing`. The backing's kind is `ObjectBackingKind::AllocatorIndexed`. A lookup under an eager root takes the run whose place is at or below the page and holds it; under an on-demand root a page is still exactly one page at exactly its place. The object's finalization is queued and done in owed steps, which lower its charge by the run pages freed; index nodes are not charged. Every on-demand entry point refuses an eager root.
- *Where it differs from the design above, and why.*
  1. A stepped release frees a run at its last leaf, not at the cursor's first entry: a run that goes past the cursor's leaf moves the cursor to the leaf's end, and the leaf is freed; a run that ends with a leaf is freed whole; a run that ends inside a leaf has its entries cleared, one visit each, and is then freed. Freeing at the first entry would leave later leaves naming a freed slot. A host test pins the visits: one per leaf passed, one per node freed, one per entry cleared.
  2. The fourth state, `EagerFreeing`, is the coordinator's addition after review. The first release step writes it to the root. From then on no lookup resolves the backing, and the invariant check can tell a half-freed index (entries may start past page 0) from a live one, which must be whole from page 0. As first built the check accepted a live eager index missing its first pages.
  3. Node creation is a loop, level by level, since `kernel-check` refuses new recursion a syscall reaches.
  4. With no index memory set, or more than 2^36 pages, a backing stays a chain. If the nodes cannot be taken the allocation fails and frees all it took; it does not fall back to a chain. `grown_tests::running_out_of_memory_mid_backing_frees_every_run_taken`, which takes every free page in one-page runs, now asserts that failure and keeps its old assertions with index memory unset.
- *Review, security review included (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2).* Read: both rounds' diffs whole, and every caller of `resolve_at`, `validate_object_range`, `object_segments`, `cancel_object_backing`, `defer_finalization` and of a grant's kind or first-run address. No defect found that reaches wrong memory. Every mapping, DMA mapping and boot write reaches an indexed backing's frames only through `index_walk`, which checks each entry's owner against the root, its role, and that the page lies inside the run; a stale or foreign entry fails as before. An unzeroed backing (`EagerAllocated`) and one being freed (`EagerFreeing`) resolve for nobody. No production code uses an object backing's first-run address, which for an index is its root node. Sent back and fixed: the weakened invariant check (item 2 above), a test name and a comment. Scheduled in §9: the whole-backing cancel in one hold, and `manager.rs` at 1,507 lines.
- *Tests.* 1,495 kernel tests (1,478 before): the indexed backing against the same allocation's chain page by page, with exactly one, two and three node reads per lookup; 16 runs stay a chain and 17 become an index; a node allocation failing at each position frees everything; zeroing inside runs; stepped release at several budgets, on the range and the buddy allocator; each on-demand entry point refusing eager roots; a backing being freed resolving for nobody; the visit accounting; and an indexed page-backed object freed in steps with its charge falling by run pages. The worker injected a fault for each and saw the named test fail.
- *Checks (coordinator, on `d7eae39`, whose tree is the merge's).* `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86, unchanged), `xtask kernel-check` and the kernel tests pass. Boots, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`: `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794859649196410706`), `--check --smp` (`…9778226308360`), `--check --icount` (`…9876525465421`). On `badf93b`, before the review round: `--check`, `--check --smp`, `--check --icount` twice and `--check --icount --smp` (`…7250351648232`, `…7966461085868`, `…7380651316645`, `…7673138122749`, `…8066186750779`).
- *The measurement* (one CPU under `--icount`; thousands of guest instructions for the whole call on the 448 MiB eager object; the fragmented object has 10,171 runs and a two-level index; five boots of the lane):

| Call | Unfragmented | Fragmented | Against unfragmented | Main, fragmented (part 1) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| create | 81,642 | 104,969 to 152,428 | +29 to +87% | 118,456 and 165,486 |
| map | 814,058 | 881,765 to 928,412 | +8 to +14% | 1,295,288 (+59%) |
| protect | 765,090 | 918,755 | +20% | 1,709,942 (+123%) |
| unmap | 720,978 | 834,818 | +16% | 1,241,618 (+72%) |
| close, as the guest sees it | 138 | 5,046 | | 9,508 |
| touch every page | 716 | 721 | +1% | 722 |

- *Against the figures part 1 set.* Met: map; the unfragmented line (within 0.1% of main's on every row); create in two boots of five. Missed: protect by 5 points of the 15% line (about 340 instructions a page) and unmap by 0.8 (about 50 a page); create in three boots of five (about 152,000, the higher of two values it takes, as main's takes 118,456 or 165,486; each is about 13,000 below main's, and why there are two is not known); the longest finalize step, 867 against 836; the close as the guest sees it. Mike, 2026-11-16, when the coordinator had stopped the merge for these and started a per-function count: "Make sure we aren't getting to caught up on instruction counts if it's just worth nanoseconds of movement". The 15% line was the coordinator's own. Sized: the protect miss is about 39 million instructions on a call of 919 million, the finalize step's about 31,000 a step. As time on hardware these are tens of nanoseconds a page and microseconds a step, by estimate only: nothing here is measured on hardware. The count was cut to the close alone, because a hold that grows with the object is a bound and not a margin. The misses stand as measured; nothing was tuned.
- *The close, counted* (an Opus worker, throwaway lane `lane/m7-count-index` at `8f07f9e` on `badf93b`, never merged; one probed boot, `.tmp/dev-boot/1794858355142034132`, 10,263 runs; tables in `.tmp/coord/ix-count.md`). The close syscall is 1.82 million instructions from entry to return, 97.7% of it five finalize steps run by its own drains (the three full ones about 411,000 each, 512 entries); taking and queueing the finalization is about 1,000. The longest hold of the runtime lock in the call is 826,000, two steps; on main the close frees every run in one hold of 9.5 million. Four more steps run at a timer gate right after the return, which is most of the rest of what the guest sees; the last third of the guest's figure fell outside the probe's window. Freeing the whole object takes 42 steps and 16.7 million instructions, 1.76 times main's inline free. An eager step costs about 783 instructions per entry visited and an on-demand step about 1,459.
- *The step report* (means per step against main's probe-carrying boots; `…9876525465421` against `…3685325317555`): map −9.0%, unmap −10.6%, protect −27.0%, create −3.7%, commit +2.9% (+3.6 to +3.7% in the three boots at `badf93b`), fill −0.1%. The finalize row now mixes eager steps with on-demand ones and does not compare. The longest step of the boot fell from 12,096 (a protect) to 4,808 (an unmap).
- *Not shown.* Hardware. A second run count or a deeper index in a guest (three levels is host-tested only). What a cursor that remembers its leaf or run would save: a walk still pays a full lookup per page, which is the 8 to 20% left. Why a commit step costs 3 to 4% more (§9).
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-index` is merged and retired. `lane/m7-count-index` held only the diagnostic probe commit; Mike had it deleted the same day ("Delete the branch"). No lane remains.

### 12.11 Step 3b: a fragmented backing allocated and cancelled in steps: design (2026-11-16; approved by Mike the same day; part 1 and part 2's items 1 to 10 built and merged: see "Part 1 as built", "Part 2 as built" and "Item 11 as built" at the end)

**What is left after step 3, read from the code at `9114920`.**

- *The allocation is one hold.* `handle_deferred_output` (`primordial.rs`, near line 1139) calls `allocate_backing(page_count)` once, holding P alone. For a backing of N runs that is N `take_run` calls; past 16 runs it is also the root, every node (one per 512 pages), one entry write per page and N role rewrites. The create's step timing begins after it, in `zero_backing_in_steps`, so no row of the step report includes it.
- *Three cancels are one hold each.* A create stopped or left parked by an ended Thread (`settle_abandoned_call`, under T with P beneath), a zeroing that fails (`cancel_backing`, under P), and a create refused in its last hold after the backing exists (`rollback_object_backing`, under T). On an index each checks and frees every entry.
- *How long the allocation hold is has not been measured.* What bounds it from above: the fragmented create costs 23.3 million instructions more than the unfragmented one in the lower of its two values, for 10,171 runs and 114,688 pages (§12.10, part 2); that difference also holds the zeroing's and the finish's share. The longest step the check boot takes anywhere is 4.8 million. One probed boot logged a longest P hold of 21.7 million without recording where.
- *Why it matters though the check's object is small.* The hold grows with the runs, and a job chooses its own fragmentation: the probe reaches 10,171 runs with 512 MiB. Every first touch, map step and finalize step on every CPU waits for P. This is a bound, not a margin (Mike, 2026-11-16, §12.10).

**Part 1: measure the hold.** One small Deepwyrm change, kept afterwards as the guard.

1. The step report gains `backing-alloc-longest-us=<us> runs=<n> pages=<n>`: the longest single stretch spent in `allocate_backing` for a create, with that backing's runs and pages, timed with the report's clock around the call near line 1139.
2. Wyrmroot's check echoes the line; it requires nothing of it yet.
3. *The gate, in absolute terms.* Part 2 is built if the hold for the probe's fragmented object is longer than the boot's longest step (`longest-step-us`, 4.8 million instructions today). If it is shorter, the hold is no worse than a step the kernel already takes; part 2 is then not built, and Mike is asked whether the three cancels alone are worth it.

**Part 2: allocation and cancellation in steps.**

1. *A fifth root state, `EagerBuilding`.* Its entries name whole runs, gapless from page 0 to `linked`, which may be less than the root's `pages`. Nothing resolves it. The invariant check takes it as it takes `Eager`, except that the entries may end before `pages`.
2. *The first hold takes at most 16 runs, as a chain* (`begin_backing(page_count)`: today's loop, stopped after `INDEX_RUN_THRESHOLD` runs). If they cover the request, the allocation is today's chain, complete, and nothing else in this design runs: an object that fits 16 runs pays nothing new. If they do not, the allocation is *building*: the root is taken, zeroed and given the `EagerBuilding` state, and the 16 runs stay a valid chain, the *remainder*, owned by the allocation. Without index memory, or past 2^36 pages, the chain is completed in that hold as today (boot only).
3. *One step* (`extend_backing(&mut allocation)`) links runs into the index until it has linked 512 runs or 65,536 pages, or the request is covered. Each run is at most `RUN_CAP = 4,096` pages, the size of a zeroing step:
   - while the remainder holds runs, the next run comes from its tail: the tail run whole if it is at most `RUN_CAP` pages (its predecessor's link is cleared, a walk of at most 16), else its last `RUN_CAP` pages, split into a record of their own while the tail run keeps its start and shrinks;
   - then runs come from the allocator, `take_run(min(left, RUN_CAP))`.
   Linking a run is two passes, as a commit's is: the nodes its leaves need and lack are taken and zeroed first (at most 9 leaves and 3 above them, held in a local array) and given back if one fails; then the nodes are linked, the entries written, and the run given the `ObjectPage` role with `linked` as its place. A run is so either wholly in the index or not in it, and no run is ever half linked. Object order no longer follows size; nothing depends on it.
4. *Completion.* When `linked` reaches `pages` the root becomes `EagerAllocated`, one write, and the allocation is what part 2 of step 3 makes today. Zeroing and finishing are unchanged.
5. *The create call.* `zero_backing_in_steps` gains the building steps before the zeroing steps, in the same loop shape: a safe point, then P for one `extend_backing`. They are timed as create steps, so the report covers them. A yield parks the call with its allocation as now; a Stop abandons it. A step that fails (no frames for a run or a node) ends the create with `NO_RESOURCES` through item 6.
6. *Nothing cancels an index whole.* An allocation or backing that will not be used is *abandoned*: its remainder chain is freed (at most 16 runs); a complete chain is freed as today; an index, building or complete, has its root set to `EagerFreeing` with `pages` set to `linked`, one write, and is queued as an *owed backing*. A root with nothing linked is freed at once. The three cancels above and the failed step all end here.
7. *Owed backings are freed by the finalize step.* `MemoryObjectAuthority` keeps them in a fixed array beside the owed objects; `finalize_owed_step` serves them after the objects, from the same budget, with `release_index_step`, and frees the root when the cursor reaches the end. `finalization_owed` counts them, so the idle step and §10.19's wake cover them.
8. *An owed backing stays charged.* Today an abandoned create's reservation ends when its frames are freed, in the same hold. Freed in steps, the frames would be held uncharged, and a job could abandon creates faster than they drain. So the Thread's reservation is re-keyed to the owed backing in the hold that abandons it, with the pages linked, lowered by each step and ended with the root. The registry's `reserved` slots take a key that is a Thread or an owed backing. The owed array has as many entries as there are reservation slots and each owed backing holds one, so the array cannot overflow. A create that finds every slot held is refused with `NO_RESOURCES` (today the code documents that as impossible, since only Threads hold slots).
9. *Removed.* The conversion (`index_allocation`), `take_eager_index` and `take_index_nodes`: nodes are now made as runs are linked. `allocate_backing` stays as begin plus steps to completion in one call, for the boot allocator and host tests; no syscall reaches it.
10. *`manager.rs`* is brought under 1,500 lines by moving the object-backing grant functions it holds to a file of their own (§9).
11. *One finalize step in one hold of the runtime lock* (added 2026-11-16: Mike had the item scheduled, decision 3 below). The count of §12.10 saw two finalize steps inside one hold taken at `with_synchronized_runtime_at_safe_point`, and five drains in one close. The worker first finds, by reading, which callers drain twice in one hold, and says so before changing anything. The change: a hold of the runtime lock runs at most one finalize step, however many drains it makes; a drain after the first skips the step and leaves the work owed. The step report gains `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest=<n>`, which the check then requires to be 1.

**Tests (host).** A backing of 16 runs is a chain after the first hold and one of 17 is building; a building index is refused by every lookup and every on-demand entry point; steps at the budgets link exactly the runs the rule gives, and the result resolves every page to a distinct frame, zeroes and frees like part 2's; a tail run over the cap is split and the chain stays whole after each split; a node or a run failing at each position leaves the index as it was before that run; an abandoned building index, a complete unzeroed one and a rolled-back backing are each freed by owed steps, their reservation falling by the pages freed and ending with the root; with every reservation slot held a create is refused; the invariants hold after every step. Each test is seen to fail on an injected fault.

**What it is held to** (one CPU under `--icount`, the check's fragmented object):
- no single hold in its create, the building steps included, longer than the boot's longest region step, with `backing-alloc-longest-us` showing the first hold at 16 runs;
- the unfragmented object's lines as they are now, within 1%: it takes the same code as today;
- the fragmented create's total is reported, not gated: it gains about 23 safe points, and its node allocations move from the first hold into the steps.
A miss of the first is a bound and stops the merge. The others are sized in instructions before anyone acts on them.

**Not in this step.** The on-demand index. Compaction (step 4). Making a run of several pages cost one entry.

**Decisions this asked of Mike, and his answers (2026-11-16: "1. Approved 2. Approved 3. Schedule it").**
1. Approve the two parts in this order: the measurement, then the build if the gate says so. *Approved.*
2. The defaults taken: runs capped at 4,096 pages, a step of 512 runs or 65,536 pages, and a create refused when every reservation slot is held. *Approved.* As built, a step's page limit is 32,768: at 65,536 a step missed the bound this design holds it to (part 2 as built).
3. Accept, or schedule otherwise: two finalize steps can share one hold of the runtime lock (826,000 instructions for two eager steps, counted once; an on-demand step is about twice as long, so about 1.7 million there, which is not counted; §9). It is bounded and below a region step, so this design as first written did not change it. *Scheduled: it is item 11 of part 2.*

**Part 1 as built and measured (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `1f01518` merges `b2d0bd3`, built by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's brief; unsigned, to be re-signed before any push. The step report prints `backing-alloc-longest-us=<us> runs=<n> pages=<n>` after `backing-runs`: the longest `allocate_backing` call a create made, timed alone, with the runs and pages of the allocation it returned. The three are one packed atomic word (24 bits of microseconds, 20 of runs, 20 of pages, each clamped: an object past 4 GiB or a million runs reads as the maximum). Report only.

- *Differs from the design.* Wyrmroot's check does not echo the line yet. The coordinator reads it from the serial log; the echo comes with part 2, where the check gains a requirement on it.
- *Review (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2).* Read whole. No defect: two clock reads around the one call, a failed call and a missing clock not recorded.
- *Checks (coordinator, on `b2d0bd3`).* `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86), `xtask kernel-check` and 1,497 kernel tests pass. Boots, both `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`: `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794865046045511773`) and `--check --icount` (`…5174316806370`); the worker's own icount boot is `…4645039990453`.
- *The measurement* (one CPU under `--icount`): the fragmented object's allocation is one hold of **21.6 million instructions** (`backing-alloc-longest-us=21623 runs=10171 pages=114688`, the same in both icount boots). The longest step of that boot is 4.8 million (`longest-step-us=4808 call=unmap`). The fragmented create costs 23.2 million more than the unfragmented one in that boot, so the allocation hold is nearly all of the difference. In the plain boot the hold is 11.3 ms of QEMU wall time.
- *The gate.* The hold is 4.5 times the longest step: part 2 is built.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-alloc-hold` is merged and retired.

**Part 2 as built and measured, items 1 to 10 (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `42159ae` merges `234f1ca`, `c807207`, `123243e`, `e99b716`, `d41f058` and `3dd19cc`, built by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's brief; unsigned, to be re-signed before any push. No ABI changed. Item 11 is not in it.

- *What it is.* A create's first hold (`begin_backing`) takes at most 16 runs. If they do not cover the request, a root is taken in the `EagerBuilding` state and the runs are the allocation's remainder. Each building step (`extend_backing`, in the new `frame_roles/build.rs`) links runs at the object page reached so far, the remainder's tail first and then runs from the allocator, until it has linked 512 runs or 32,768 pages. The create call runs the building steps before the zeroing steps in the same loop, each a create step with a safe point before it. An allocation or backing that will not be used is abandoned under the runtime lock with the paging lock beneath: an index with runs linked becomes an `OwedBacking`, queued in `MemoryObjectAuthority` and freed by the finalize step after the owed objects. Its create's reservation passes to it in the same hold and falls by the run pages each step frees.
- *Differs from the design.*
  - A step's page limit is 32,768, not 65,536 (see the measurement).
  - Item 10 moved the page-table functions out of `manager.rs` (to `frame_roles/tables.rs`, a pure move), not the object-backing grant functions the design names: the coordinator's brief chose them as the larger and more separate group. `manager.rs` is 875 lines.
  - A run from the allocator holds the `AllocatedUninitialized` role while its nodes are taken, since they would otherwise take the same free slot.
  - `RootUse` gains `Building` and `Unfinished`; `EagerIndex`, `write_run_entries` and `node_at` stay, since linking uses them.
  - A create the adapter rolls back is now abandoned inside its last hold of the runtime lock for every kind of backing. A chain or an on-demand root is still freed whole there (at most 16 runs, or one page); before, that was done under the paging lock alone after the hold.
  - A rolled-back backing's reservation is made anew in that hold, with the creating process's charge tag, because the Thread's own reservation has to end before the adapter admits. The Thread's slot was freed in the same hold, so a slot is free.
  - The live code that allocated or cancelled a backing whole is removed (`user_access::allocate_backing`, `allocate_zeroed_backing`, `cancel_backing`, and the `MemoryObjectBackingAccess` impl for the live address space). `cancel_backing_allocation` stays for `allocate_backing`'s own failure path and the boot allocator.
- *Review and security review* (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2, of `200e86c..3dd19cc`; the author was the worker). `build.rs` read whole; the diffs of `chain.rs`, `index.rs`, `frame_roles.rs`, `object.rs`, `object/mod.rs`, `primordial.rs` and `user_access.rs` read whole; the tests read in part. No defect found. Checked: a run is linked wholly or not at all (every failure comes before the first write of that run, and nodes taken are given back); the node array cannot overflow (at most 8 leaves and 2 upper nodes for a run of 4,096 pages, against 12 places); a building root resolves for nobody; splitting a run rewrites one record whose start does not change, and the invariant check compares every pair of records for overlap and the allocator's totals; the levels of a root are always read from its record, so a root abandoned with fewer pages than its levels cover frees correctly (the coordinator asked for the test of this, `3dd19cc`); an owed backing is charged from the hold that abandons it until its root is freed, and the reservation cannot be lowered below zero without a panic; the owed array has one entry for each reservation slot; the Thread of an abandoned create still holds its reservation in every caller of `settle_abandoned_call` (nothing else ends one); the lock order, runtime lock then paging lock, is the existing one. Not shown by any test: the panics on a full owed array or a missing reservation, which are argued from the code. One risk kept and scheduled (§9): owed backings hold reservation slots, so a job can delay other processes' creates.
- *Tests.* 1,508 kernel tests (1,497 before): seven of the role manager's in `build_tests.rs`, one of the registry, two of the authority, each seen by the worker to fail on an injected fault, and the coordinator's added case. Seven tests of `indexed_tests.rs` changed because object order no longer follows chain order.
- *Checks (coordinator).* On `d41f058`: `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86), `xtask kernel-check`, 1,507 kernel tests; boots, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS`: `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794868757314560575`), `--check --smp` (`…8887309629927`), `--check --icount` (`…8984570802532`). On `3dd19cc`, which adds one test and no kernel code: `fmt --check` and 1,508 kernel tests. The merge's tree is `3dd19cc`'s.
- *The measurement* (one CPU under `--icount`, thousands of instructions; the coordinator's boot at `d41f058`):

  | | before (`1f01518`) | now |
  |---|---|---|
