  | first allocation hold, fragmented | 21,623 for 10,171 runs | 13 for 16 runs |
  | longest create step | 3,580 | 3,597 |
  | longest step of the boot | 4,808 (unmap) | 4,795 (unmap) |
  | fragmented create, whole | 118,456 and 165,486 | 107,236 |
  | unfragmented create, map, protect, unmap, close | 81,751, 813,443, 764,465, 720,358, 138 | 81,931, 817,597, 768,749, 724,600, 138 |

  The unfragmented lines are within 0.6% of the plan's. The fragmented object's map, protect and unmap are as in §12.10 (928,098, 918,476, 834,539). Its close reads 5,031 here and 3,350 in the worker's boot at `e99b716`: the close path is not changed by this merge, and the figure follows how many finalize steps land in the call, which item 11 is about.
- *The bound was missed once.* At `e99b716`, with a step of 65,536 pages, the longest create step was 6,065 thousand instructions against an unmap step of 4,797 (the worker's boot, `…7656868894580`). With the limit at 32,768 it is 3,597, the length of a zeroing step, so a building step is no longer the longest create step; how long it is by itself is not reported. The cause is inferred, not counted: each entry written looks its node's direct-map address up again, about 92 instructions an entry, and 65,536 of them is 6.03 million (§9 schedules the lookup with step 4).
- *Not shown.* Abandonment in a guest: no boot stops or fails a fragmented create, so the owed-backing path runs only in host tests. Two or more CPUs building at once (the SMP boot passes; it does not time steps under `--icount`). Hardware.
- *What is left of step 3b.* Item 11, with `memory/object.rs`'s split (§9), and Wyrmroot's check echoing `backing-alloc-longest-us` and requiring `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest=1`.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-stepped-alloc` is merged and retired.

**Item 11 specified from the code (2026-11-16, read at `42159ae`).** The design left the reading to the worker; the coordinator did it, so the worker gets a finished change.

- *Which callers drain twice in one hold.* `drain_finalizers` (`primordial.rs`) runs one finalize step each time it is called, and one hold of the runtime lock calls it from up to four places: (a) `service_q35_retirement_at_bsp_safe_point`, at the start of every hold `with_synchronized_runtime_at_safe_point` takes on the BSP (the report's `gate` source); (b) `service_pending_timer_expiries_on_bootstrap`, which most call paths run next on the BSP and which drains whether or not a timer expired; (c) a call's own drains inside the hold (`drain_step_finalizers` for a call in steps, the page fault's drain when a touch is refused, the rendezvous and retirement paths); (d) `end_hold(Return)` when the hold pushed releases. On one CPU, which is the BSP, (a) and (b) alone are two steps in every such hold. This is read, not counted; the new report line counts it.
- *The change.*
  1. A small type, `HoldStepBudget`, in `syscall/hold_end.rs`: `take()` is true once per hold, `end()` returns how many steps the hold took and clears it. The runtime core holds one for finalize steps.
  2. The carrier's `finalize_owed_step` runs a step only if finalization is owed and `take()` allows it. Skipped, it still reports finalization as owed and returns `true`, so the work stays owed for the next hold.
  3. `check_hold_end`, which the guard runs as it releases the lock, calls `end()` and gives the count to the step report.
  4. The loop that completes every owed finalization as the system ends (`while self.finalize_owed_step() {}`) keeps running many steps in its one hold: it calls a variant that does not ask the budget and is not counted.
  5. The step report gains `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest=<n>` after `finalize-steps`: the most steps one hold ran since boot.
- *What does not change.* The release step and the teardown step still run in every drain; they are not counted here. A touch refused while finalization is owed still returns `Released` and comes again: if its hold's safe point already ran the step, the drain for the touch skips it, and the next hold's step frees more. Each such hold frees one step's worth, so the touch still ends.
- *`memory/object.rs`* (§9) is brought under 1,500 lines first, as a pure move: the owed-finalization queue and steps to `memory/object/owed.rs`, and the release-on-unmap step with its two release-list types to `memory/object/release.rs`.
- *Tests.* `HoldStepBudget` on the host; the report line's tally on the host. The carrier is not host-testable, so the behaviour is checked in the guest: Wyrmroot's check requires `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest=1`, and requires `backing-alloc-longest-us` to show at most 16 runs.
- *What it is held to.* The line reads 1 in the plain, SMP and icount boots. The check's lines move by less than 1% where finalization is not involved. The fragmented close, which carried up to five steps, becomes shorter and its object takes more holds to free: the longest wait for a finalization to complete (`finalize-wait-longest-us`) is reported before and after, and a wait that grows by more than the number of steps moved out of the close is stopped and looked at.

**Item 11 as built and measured (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `2ebf6ba` merges `0663c9f`, `2f9cfa1` and `57813b4`; Wyrmroot `b53b404` merges `6e38bd9`. Each by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the coordinator's brief; unsigned, to be re-signed before any push. No ABI changed. Wyrmroot's check now needs a Deepwyrm at `2ebf6ba` or later, which `dev-boot` builds from the local checkout. The pair tested is Deepwyrm `57813b4` with Wyrmroot `6e38bd9`, whose trees the two merges have.

- *What it is.* `HoldStepBudget` (`syscall/hold_end.rs`) counts the finalize steps a hold has run. The carrier's `finalize_owed_step` runs a step only while the hold has run none; otherwise it leaves finalization owed. The count is kept where a step actually runs, so a step that did not ask would show. `check_hold_end` gives the count to the step report and clears it. The report prints `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest=<n>` after `finalize-steps`. The system-end loop runs its steps without asking and discards its count before its hold ends; the report is printed after that hold (`power::soft_off_then_halt` needs the lock released), so without the discard the line would have held the loop's count.
- *The coordinator's reading was confirmed by the worker,* and every acquisition of the runtime lock ends through `check_hold_end` (the guard's `Drop`), so no other reset was needed.
- *Differs from the specification.* The budget sits in a `Cell`, because `check_hold_end` has only `&self` and changing the trait would have touched a UI stub and test impls. As first built the line counted the budget's own flag and could only read 0 or 1; the coordinator had it changed to count steps run (`57813b4`). `take_finalization` stays in `object.rs`: it takes every kind of finalization. `0663c9f`'s message gives `object.rs` as 1,392 lines; it is 1,399.
- *Wyrmroot's check* (`tools/xtask/src/dev_boot/memory.rs`): `backing_alloc` requires the `backing-alloc-longest-us` line to parse and show at most 16 runs; `finalize_hold` requires `finalize-steps-in-one-hold longest` to be exactly 1, and fails on 0 too. Every step line was already echoed, so the echo the design asked for needed no change.
- *Review (coordinator, Claude Mythos 5.2).* Both diffs read whole. No defect. The split is a pure move (the old file's lines against the three new files). A touch refused while finalization is owed still ends: each hold that serves it frees one step's worth before or after the refusal.
- *Tests.* 1,510 kernel tests (1,508 before): the budget, and the report's tally and line, each seen by the worker to fail on injected faults. Wyrmroot: two tests of the two rules, seen to fail on four faults; 86 and 5 xtask tests pass for the coordinator.
- *Checks (coordinator, on the pair).* Deepwyrm `57813b4`: `fmt --check`, `xtask clippy` (ratchet 236 and 86), `xtask kernel-check`, 1,510 kernel tests. Boots run from Wyrmroot `6e38bd9`, all `DEV_BOOT_CHECK_PASS` with both new rules met: `--check` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794872130581298910`), `--check --smp` (`…2268153508492`), `--check --icount` (`…2372919627762`). Deepwyrm `2f9cfa1` was also checked and booted three times with Wyrmroot `3ffdee3` before the counting change.
- *The measurement* (one CPU under `--icount`, thousands of instructions; before is the coordinator's boot at `d41f058`):

  | | before | now |
  |---|---|---|
  | most finalize steps in one hold | not reported (two read from the code on the BSP, two counted once in §12.10) | 1 |
  | fragmented close | 5,031 | 3,380 |
  | longest fill step | 1,736 | 867 |
  | longest commit step | 2,746 | 1,949 |
  | longest wait for a finalization to complete | 355,029 | 305,834 |
  | finalize steps in the boot | 3,096 | 3,164 |
  | unfragmented create, map, protect, unmap, close | 81,931, 817,597, 768,749, 724,600, 138 | 81,960, 816,143, 767,290, 723,181, 138 |

  The fragmented object's create, map, protect and unmap are within 0.4% (106,832, 928,103, 918,495, 834,558). The wait did not grow, which was what this item was held to. Why fill and commit steps are shorter is inferred (their holds no longer carry a second finalize step), not counted. The unfragmented create read 84,013 in one of four icount boots of this code and 81,886 to 81,960 in the others; the cause is not measured.
- *Not shown.* How many holds a close takes, and why the close is 3,380 and not shorter. Two CPUs under `--icount`. Hardware.
- *Lanes.* `lane/m7-finalize-hold` and `lane/m7-check-holds` are merged and retired. Step 3b is complete.

### 12.12 Step 4: cheaper paths, and whether to compact: design (2026-11-16; approved by Mike the same day)

Read for this design, at Deepwyrm `14b0c34` (the re-signed `290c108`): `memory/physical/buddy.rs` in full; every caller of the role manager's and the allocator's allocation entry points (`allocate`, `allocate_below`, `allocate_largest_aligned`, `allocate_run`, `begin_backing`, `allocate_on_demand_backing`); `arch/x86_64/mm/direct_map.rs`'s lookup; `DirectMapIndexMemory` in `arch/x86_64/mm/activation.rs`; `frame_roles/index.rs`'s entry writer and release step; `memory/object.rs`'s `dma_physical_range`; `kernel/build.rs`'s assembler invocation; §12.5 and §12.6's counts.

**What the code says.**

- *Nothing a running system asks for needs more than one contiguous page.* Page tables, roots, journal pages, and index roots and nodes are each `allocate(1)`; the IOMMU's tables come from a pool of single pages. An eager backing takes whatever runs exist (steps 3 and 3b). An on-demand backing commits single pages. A DMA mapping resolves its pages through the role manager and "may lie in several runs" (`dma_physical_range`). Kernel stacks, thread contexts and IPC buffers come from pools sized at boot. The only requests for an aligned or large run (`allocate_largest_aligned`, the AP trampoline's `allocate_below`) are made at boot, before any job runs.
- *The check already shows this in a guest.* Its fragmented probe leaves free memory in holes of one and two pages, then starts a process and creates, maps, touches, protects, unmaps and closes a 448 MiB object there, in 10,191 runs (§12.10, §12.11).
- *User mappings use no large pages* (nothing in the journal, the publisher or the regions maps a user range with a 2 MiB entry). Contiguous memory therefore buys a mapping nothing in the TLB today.
- *So §6.8's trigger for compaction has no instance.* It said compaction "runs when a large request fails, before the OOM path". No request can fail for fragmentation alone.
- *What fragmentation still costs is per run, and it is measured* (one CPU under `--icount`, the coordinator's boot `.tmp/dev-boot/1794872372919627762`; the same 448 MiB object in 10,191 runs against one run):

  | call | one run | 10,191 runs | extra per page |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | create | 81,960 | 106,832 | 217 instructions |
  | map | 816,143 | 928,103 | 976 |
  | protect | 767,290 | 918,495 | 1,318 |
  | unmap | 723,181 | 834,558 | 971 |

  The first three columns are thousands of instructions. An estimate, not a measurement: at one to three instructions a nanosecond the worst of these is about half a microsecond a page.
- *The free path, as read and as counted so far.* Freeing one page through the role manager is `free_index_slot` (123 instructions), `vacate` (238) and the allocator's `free_run` (682 to 686), by §12.6's count. Inside `free_run` nothing is counted yet. Read, it does three things: finds the initial range (`range_index`), checks for a double free (`overlaps_free_in`: the tags of the freed pages, then one probe for each order upward, up to 40, each computing a position), and merges (`insert_block`: a position, a tag compare and an `unlink` for each merge). Which of the three holds the instructions is not known and this design does not guess.
- *Reading or writing an index entry looks its node's address up again every time.* `DirectMapIndexMemory::word` calls `DirectMap::virtual_address`, a binary search over the direct map's ranges, for every `read` and `write`. A walk reads one entry at each level, so every page a map, protect or unmap step resolves through an index pays it two or three times; a building step pays it once for each entry written (about 92 instructions an entry, inferred in §12.11).
- *The kernel build already runs the pinned clang* (`DEEPWYRM_CLANG`, for the assembly sources), so compiling a C file for the kernel needs flags, not a new tool. The host test build would need the host clang that `tooling/host-rust-toolchain.toml` pins.

**Part 1: count.** Throwaway lanes, never merged, as §12.6's were. One Opus worker.

1. *Probes* around: `Buddy::free_run` and, inside it, `range_index`, `overlaps_free_in` and `insert_range`; `Buddy::allocate_run`; `vacate`, `occupy`, `free_index_slot`, `take_index_frame`; `DirectMapIndexMemory::word` (calls and instructions); `index_walk` and `index_entry`. Reported per kind of step: an eager finalize step, an on-demand finalize step, a commit step, a map step on the fragmented object, and a building step. Two one-CPU `--icount` boots, to see what moves between boots.
2. *The buddy's free and allocate paths three ways,* on the same boot's workload, each behind the same Rust interface so the same host tests drive all three:
   - as they are;
   - in Rust with the bounds checks of the merge loop and the list links removed (`get_unchecked` under the invariants I1 to I6, each use documented), to see what the checks cost;
   - in C: one file, `memory/physical/buddy_free.c`, the same algorithm over the same arrays (`tags`, `links`, `bases`, `heads`, `nonempty`), called through `extern "C"`, compiled by the build's clang with the kernel target's code model and feature flags (no red zone, no SSE, kernel code model; the worker reads them from the target specification rather than guessing).
   The result is a table: instructions per free of one page and per allocation of one page, for each of the three.
3. *Nothing is tuned in this part.* It ends with the tables and the worker's note of anything the probes changed (inlining moves, as in §12.6).

**Part 2: the cheaper paths.** Built from the counts. The candidates are specified now so that the worker gets a finished change; each has the count that selects it.

1. *A free bitmap for the double-free check* (built if the check is 100 instructions or more of a one-page free). One bit per allocator page, by position, set exactly while the page lies in a free block; carried in `BuddyStorage` beside the tags (32 KiB per GiB of RAM). `free_run` tests the bits of the freed range, a word at a time, in place of the tag scan and the upward probes; `take` clears a run's bits and `insert_range` sets them, a word at a time. `Buddy::check` gains the invariant (I7: a bit is set exactly for the pages of free blocks). The defence stays exact and no longer grows with the address.
2. *One address lookup for a node, not one for an entry* (built in any case: it is the scheduled row of §9). `IndexMemory` gains `write_run(node, first_entry, count, value)`, with a default that loops `write`, so the host models need no change; `write_run_entries` and the release step's entry clearing call it once for a leaf. For reads, which cannot be batched, the lookup itself is made cheap: when the index memory is installed, every initial allocator range is checked once to lie in the direct map, and `word` then adds the base after comparing the address against the direct map's lowest and highest mapped byte (two comparisons in place of the search). The safety argument does not change: the trait's contract is already that the role manager passes only pages it owns.
3. *Narrow writers for the role slot* (built if `vacate` or `occupy` is still above 150 instructions): `vacate` for a slot whose role is known not to be of PML4 level and to carry no table count, and the matching `occupy`, as §12.6 gave the readers. `slot.rs` stays the only code that touches a slot's fields.
4. *The language of the buddy's free and allocate paths.* Chosen from part 1's table by a rule fixed now: C is taken only if it costs at least 10% and at least 50 instructions less per free than the better of the two Rust forms. Otherwise the better Rust form is taken (the unchecked one only if it wins by the same margin over the checked one; unchecked indexing in the allocator is its own risk). The reason for a margin: C in the allocator means a second language and an FFI boundary in the code every frame passes through, a C compile in the kernel's and the host tests' pinned builds, and the loss of the borrow checker over the allocator's arrays. If C is taken, the design of its build (flags, the host build, how the pinned clang is named, the lint and review rules for the file) is written and approved before it is merged.

*What part 2 is held to* (one CPU under `--icount`):
- a finalize step costs no more than 635 thousand instructions on the workload §9 measured, the figure of the kernel before M7;
- a commit step costs no more than it did before the index (§9: 1,600 to 2,000 instructions a step less than now);
- writing an index entry while building costs 20 instructions or fewer, so a building step of 32,768 pages is under one million;
- reported, not gated: the fragmented object's extra per page on map, protect and unmap, and the three paths' counts.
A miss of one of the first three is sized in instructions and reported before anyone acts on it; none of them is a bound that grows.

**Part 3: page migration is not built unless Mike decides otherwise.** The coordinator's recommendation is (a).

- *(a) M7 closes without compaction.* §6.8's third item is recorded as having no trigger: no request needs contiguity, the check shows a guest working in fully fragmented memory, and what fragmentation costs is the per-run overhead above, which part 2 lowers directly. Three things would reopen it, and on this choice each is written into §9 so it is not lost: user mappings with 2 MiB pages; a device that needs contiguous DMA with no IOMMU; or a per-run cost that part 2 cannot remove and that a job is shown to feel.
- *(b) Build migration now, as background defragmentation.* What it takes, as a sketch and not a design (the pins named here were not read for this section): a migration of one object page takes a destination frame; finds every mapping of the page through the object's leases; makes each not present through the journal and shoots the TLBs down on every CPU running that address space; copies; rewrites the page's run in the object's chain or index (a run of several pages must first be split); republishes the mappings; and frees the source. It must refuse a page that an in-flight call has pinned (an output pin held across holds, an atomic wait's pin, a parked call's translations), a page an IOMMU table names, and a page being committed. Each refusal is a place where a missed case is a use after free in another process's memory. It would buy back at most the last three rows of the table above for an object whose memory was fragmented when it was created, at the cost of a copy and a shootdown for each page moved.
- *Why (a).* The mechanism in (b) is the riskiest kind the kernel has (it changes what a live mapping names, on several CPUs, around every kind of pin), and nothing asks for it. CLAUDE.md §6 keeps a kernel mechanism for a need that exists.

**After step 4, M7 is at its exit:** the buddy allocator, the smaller role table in pieces, the index, stepped allocation, and the cheaper paths. Its milestone-tier run is named when Mike closes it.

**Decisions this asked of Mike, and his answer (2026-11-16: "Approve all four").** Each is approved as written, and for the fourth the choice is (a).
1. Approve part 1, the count, including the C build of the buddy's free and allocate paths in a throwaway lane.
2. Approve the language rule of part 2 item 4 (C only for a saving of at least 10% and 50 instructions per free), or set another.
3. Approve part 2 being built from the counts by the rules above without another approval round, coming back first only if the counts point somewhere the candidates do not cover.
4. Compaction: (a) close M7 without it, with the three reopening conditions in §9 (recommended), or (b) have the migration design written next.

**Part 1 as counted (2026-11-16).** By an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) in the throwaway lane `lane/m7-count-paths` (HEAD `18d109b`, never merged), from the coordinator's brief. One CPU under `--icount`; every figure is guest instructions from probe pairs, with the probes' own cost taken out (an empty probe reads 4 and costs the code around it 49; calibrated in each boot). The coordinator recomputed the free path's rows from the `[count]` lines of the three logs and they agree. Boots, all passing: `.tmp/dev-boot/1794875706146221115` and `…6028895599452` (Rust as it is, twice: per-call figures agree within 1%), `…6342148414581` (Rust without bounds checks), `…6662982119313` (C). The 1,510 kernel tests pass for each of the three, the C paths included: the host build compiled the C file with the pinned host clang.

- *Freeing one page* (1.38 million calls in on-demand finalize steps), per call:

  | | Rust as it is | Rust, no bounds checks | C |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `free_run`, whole | 705 | 599 | 459 |
  | finding the range (`range_index`) | 40 | 41 | 39 |
  | the double-free check (`overlaps_free_in`) | 439 | 389 | 237 |
  | merging (`insert_range`) | 186 | 125 | 154 |
  | the rest | 41 | 45 | 29 |
  | whole, without the check | 267 | 211 | 222 |

  The check is 62%, 65% and 52% of a free. Why the C check is cheaper than Rust's without bounds checks is not measured.
- *Allocating one page* (1.2 million calls in commit steps): 252, 198 and 240.
- *The role slot.* `vacate` 110 to 128, `occupy` 180 to 203, `free_index_slot` 109 of its own.
- *Index node access.* `DirectMapIndexMemory::word` costs 70 instructions from a read and 69 from a write, every call. Writing one entry while building costs 126 in all (69 for the address, 47 in `write_run_entries` itself, the rest probes' neighbours); §12.11's inferred 92 was low.
- *A walk* (`index_walk`) costs 520 instructions on the fragmented object, 288 of them its own, 140 the two address lookups, 92 the two entry checks. **A map or unmap step walks three times for each page and a protect step four times** (1,542 and 2,060 walks for a step of 512 pages). The on-demand finalize step walks once for each entry it frees (509 walks and 481 frees a step).
- *Where a step's instructions are* (own instructions of the probed functions, per step):
  - on-demand finalize: the double-free check 211 thousand, walks 150, merging 89, address lookups 112, `vacate` 62, `free_index_slot` 52, entry checks 48;
  - commit: zeroing and taking the frame 11.3 thousand, walks 7.2, allocation 3.7, address lookups 4.4, `occupy` 3.0;
  - map on the fragmented object: walks 444 thousand, address lookups 216, entry checks 142;
  - building: address lookups 360 thousand, `write_run_entries` 245, `occupy` 87, allocation 77.
- *What the probes changed.* A probed finalize step costs 15% more than the probes' own cost explains, and a fragmented map step 5%; the worker supposes inlining and did not show it. The tables say where the cost is, not its exact size, as in §12.6.
- *The C build.* One file of 358 lines, compiled by the build's clang (22.1.8, the pinned host clang's binary) with the kernel target's flags read from the target specification (kernel code model, no red zone, no SSE or x87, soft float, PIE); no stack-clash protection (clang does not offer it for this target). It depends on a Rust slice field being laid out as pointer then length, which Rust does not promise; the worker checks it at seed time.

**What the counts select, by §12.12's rules.**
1. *The free bitmap is built:* the check is 439 instructions of a one-page free, against the rule's 100.
2. *The node access change is built* (it was unconditional): in progress in `lane/m7-node-access`.
3. *No narrower slot writer is built.* `occupy` is above the rule's 150, but the narrow `occupy_slot` and `vacate_slot` the rule had in mind already exist (§12.6 item 3); what is left of `occupy` is the start index and the packing, which the count does not split. It is 87 thousand instructions of a building step and 3 thousand of a commit step.
4. *The language is not chosen yet.* Read to the letter the rule takes C: 459 against 599. But 237 of C's 459 and 389 of Rust's 599 are the check that item 1 replaces whatever the language, and on the rest C (222) is behind Rust without bounds checks (211), as it is on allocation (240 against 198). The rule was written for the path that ships, so the three are being counted again with the bitmap in place, and the rule is applied to that table.
5. *Two things the candidates do not cover,* put to Mike before they are built (decision 3):
   - **A place in the index for `BackingCursor`.** The journal and the publisher already resolve pages in order through a cursor that makes a chain cost one step for each run crossed; for an index root `resolve_at` ignores it and walks from the root for every page. The cursor would remember the leaf and the run it last resolved; the next page then costs a check of that run, or of that leaf and one entry read, in place of a walk. As now it would hold no borrow and grant nothing: each use checks the leaf and the run by identity against the head. Not measured: by the counts above a walk is 520 instructions and its replacement about two entry checks and a read.
   - **The release step walks once for a leaf,** not once for each entry it frees, inside its one exclusive call.

**Mike's correction of the measure (2026-11-16).** On the tables above: "You keep counting raw instructions like that matters above performance. I don't care if C takes 1k instructions to do what rust does in 500 if it's better/faster". He is right, and it changes how this step decides.

- *What an instruction count is good for.* Finding where work is, and bounds that grow with size (§12.11). It is not speed: cache misses, branch mispredictions and how the CPU overlaps the work decide time. The tables of part 1 stay as a map of where the work is. Nothing in them is a cost in time, and no rule or target of this step is stated in instructions any more.
- *The language rule, as Mike then set it* (2026-11-16): "C gets picked if it meets or beats Rust, Rust only gets picked if it beats C. I dont care by what margin, it could be 2%." So the buddy's free and allocate paths are built in C unless a Rust form is faster by measured time on real hardware; a tie goes to C and there is no margin. This replaces the 10% margin of item 4, which the coordinator had first carried over into time. The first table's reading ("C behind Rust without bounds checks once the check is gone") was an instruction count and decides nothing.
- *How time is measured.* QEMU TCG wall time does not compare builds (§12.5), so the check boots give no time. Two ways exist:
  - *natively on the host,* for code that builds there: the allocator's three forms are being benchmarked on the development machine (an AMD Ryzen 7 9800X3D), the same sources at the kernel's optimisation level, a million or more operations a batch on an allocator of 4 GiB and more, pinned to one core, in nanoseconds per operation with cycle, cache-miss and branch-miss counts where `perf` allows. What a hosted build cannot match of the kernel's (no SSE, the kernel code model) is reported with it;
  - *the guest under KVM,* for whole steps: `/dev/kvm` is not open to the development user (it is group `kvm`), so at first that meant the designated VM (CLAUDE.md §10) or Mike opening `/dev/kvm`. Mike added the user to the `kvm` group the same day. `dev-boot` gets a `--kvm` mode for timing (QEMU's `-accel kvm` with the host's CPU; the emulated IOMMU's interrupt remapping needs the split irqchip), and until the user's session is renewed the harness is run through `sg kvm`.
- *The targets of part 2* (a finalize step, a commit step, an index entry written) were instruction counts. They become: each change is shown faster, or not slower, by time where time can be measured, and is otherwise reported as work removed, not as speed.


**Node access as built (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `65d03fe` merges `19f6f1c` (part 2 item 2), by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5); reviewed and re-verified by the coordinator on `19f6f1c`: fmt, clippy (236 / 86), kernel-check, 1,512 host tests, and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots. `IndexMemory::write_run` writes or clears a leaf segment with one node lookup, and `DirectMapIndexMemory` checks once, when it is made, that every initial allocator range is in the direct map and then reaches a node by address. Under `--icount` the fragmented create fell from 106,832 to 96,928 thousand instructions and the longest step from 4,806 to 4,526: work removed. It has not been timed on hardware by itself.

**The timing mode as built (2026-11-16).** Wyrmroot `da9115b` merges `c7b22e7` and `24fe5b1`, by an Opus worker; reviewed and re-verified by the coordinator (fmt, clippy, 92 + 5 `xtask` tests, one `dev-boot --check --kvm` boot of Deepwyrm `65d03fe`). `dev-boot --kvm` runs QEMU with `-accel kvm -machine q35,kernel-irqchip=split -cpu host,migratable=no,+invtsc`; it is refused with `--icount`, needs `/dev/kvm` read-write, prints step means in nanoseconds and ends its pass line in `kvm=1`. Two things were found on the way:

- *The clock.* With `-cpu host` alone the guest saw no invariant TSC, the kernel timed by the ACPI PM timer, and short steps read slower than under the emulator (a fill step 29 µs). With the invariant TSC shown the kernel reports `clock=tsc` and the same step reads 1.6 µs. That each PM-timer read is expensive under KVM fits this and was not measured.
- *Four CPUs.* `dev-boot --check --kvm --smp` stops after `[smp] cpu=0 apic=0 state=online`: the second CPU never reports, on either clock (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794881321730218743`, `…1879813707650`). The same boot passes under the emulator. Not investigated; scheduled in §9.

The first real step times, one boot of `65d03fe` on one CPU (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794882147744368233`), mean per step: fill 1.6 µs, commit 17.5 µs, teardown 21 µs, finalize 54 µs, map 161 µs, unmap 241 µs, protect 332 µs, create 367 µs; the longest step 1.25 ms (a commit); the fragmented object's first allocation hold 4 µs.

**The allocator's three forms, timed (2026-11-16).** By the same worker in `lane/m7-count-paths` (HEAD `e9a51dc`, never merged), each form with the free bitmap of part 2 item 1: Rust as on main (bounds-checked), Rust with the bounds checks removed, and C (clang 22.1.8). The three produce the same allocator state after every region on the host (free count and free-block digest).

- *On the host,* twice. The first session (4 GiB and 16 GiB of pages, 15 repetitions, interleaved, pinned) had unchecked Rust faster than C in seven burst workloads by 5 to 18% and C faster in two alternating allocate-and-free workloads by 8 and 30%. The second session, after the workloads moved into a shared function, had unchecked Rust faster than C in ten of eleven; C's time on the half-held pair went from 10.85 to 18.22 ns while both Rust forms moved under 5%. Which change moved it was not measured. A hosted build also differs from the kernel's (SSE on, red zone, small code model). The host figures therefore decide nothing.
- *In the kernel, under KVM.* A boot-time benchmark, built with the kernel's flags (`-C opt-level=3 -C panic=abort --target x86_64-unknown-none`; C with `-O2 -mcmodel=kernel -mno-red-zone -mno-sse… -msoft-float`), runs on the first CPU with interrupts off, through the real allocator, 2^17 operations a region, timed by `lfence; rdtsc` and converted with the boot's calibrated TSC rate. Three boots per form, interleaved, 15 repetitions a boot; ns per operation, median of 45:

| Region | Rust, checked | Rust, unchecked | C |
|---|---|---|---|
| allocate single pages | 24.26 | 12.56 | 23.16 |
| allocate, no split | 20.92 | 9.34 | 19.82 |
| allocate 11-page runs | 30.85 | 21.46 | 28.63 |
| free in allocation order | 16.72 | 15.09 | 18.98 |
| free, no merge | 13.56 | 12.67 | 16.42 |
| free, every free merges | 18.43 | 16.70 | 20.29 |
| free in random order | 25.82 | 23.53 | 27.59 |
| free 11-page runs | 29.88 | 24.50 | 30.26 |
| pair, from the boot state | 35.49 | 28.45 | 34.71 |
| pair, half the pages held | 35.39 | 28.16 | 35.11 |

  Per-boot medians agree within 1.3% in every cell (boots `.tmp/dev-boot/1794882280871191661` to `…3063938862882`; the coordinator computed the table from the serial logs and the worker's agrees). Not accounted for: checked Rust and C both cost about 11 ns more per allocation than unchecked Rust in the kernel, far more than on the host; C is slower than both Rust forms in every free region in the kernel and between them on the host. Neither was investigated.

**The language, by Mike's rule: Rust without the bounds checks.** It is faster than C in every region timed in the kernel's own build, by 15 to 53%, so Rust "beats C" and is picked; no C file enters the kernel. The bounds-checked form on main is replaced: it is the slowest or within 3% of the slowest in every region.

**Part 2 items 1 and 4 as specified for the build (2026-11-16, read at `65d03fe`).** One change to `memory/physical/buddy.rs` and its storage, by an Opus worker, ported from the measured code (`lane/m7-count-paths` `e9a51dc`: the `deepwyrm_buddy_unchecked` variant and the bitmap) without the probes, the variant switch, the C file, the static bitmap and the benchmark.

1. *The free bitmap, with real storage.* `BuddyStorage` gains `free_bits: &'static mut [u64]`, exactly `tags.len().div_ceil(64)` words. The grown layout (`frame_roles/storage.rs`, `grown_layout`) gains its offset and word count after the tags, 8-aligned and inside `bytes`, carved zeroed with the other parts; `accepts_buddy_storage` and `adopt_buddy` check its length. It costs one bit per allocator page (32 KiB per GiB). I7: a page's bit is set exactly while the page is in a free block, and bits past the last page are clear. `seed` sets the bits of every seeded range, `free_run` tests the run's bits (any set is `DoubleFree`, with nothing changed), inserts and then sets them, `take` clears the bits of the run it returns, and `overlaps_free` reads them. `overlaps_free_in` and the tag scan it used are deleted. `check` verifies I7.
2. *The paths without bounds checks.* `range_index`, `position`, `page_at`, `push`, `unlink`, `insert_block`, `insert_range`, `take`, the bit helpers, `allocate_run`, `allocate_run_below` and `free_run` index with `get_unchecked`. There is one copy of each helper, not a checked and an unchecked one; `seed` and `check` use the same helpers.
   - *What makes it sound.* Every index is either a position below `tags.len()`, an order at most `MAX_ORDER`, or a range index below `bases.len()`. Positions come from the lists and tags, which only this module writes (I1 to I7), or from a caller's range that `range_index` has placed inside one initial range. The one fact the module cannot establish itself is that `ranges` is the slice it was seeded with. So `Buddy`'s methods that take `ranges` become `unsafe fn` with that as their stated requirement, and `PhysicalFrameAllocator`, whose `initial` array is written only in its constructors, discharges it at each call with a `SAFETY` comment. `PhysicalFrameAllocator`'s own interface stays safe and unchanged.
   - *What still fails loudly.* `free_run` keeps its real checks: the range lies in one initial range, and its bits are clear. The helpers carry a `debug_assert!` for each precondition, so the host tests (built with debug assertions) check every one on every path they reach; the kernel build has none.
   - *Lints.* Every `unsafe` block has its `SAFETY` comment (the ratchet stays 236 / 86), and the module's `unsafe_code` allowance carries its reason.
3. *Tests (host, behaviour).* The lane's `the_free_bitmap_refuses_every_overlap_with_a_free_block`, shown failing against a check that looks only at the first page; adoption refuses a bitmap of the wrong length; the grown layout holds the bitmap inside its pages for a page count that is not a multiple of 64; a long random sequence of allocations and frees, with ceilings, calls `check` (so I7) after every operation; the existing 1,512 pass unchanged except where they build a `BuddyStorage`.
4. *Checks and measurement.* fmt, clippy, kernel-check, the host tests, and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots. Then time: three `dev-boot --check --kvm` boots of `65d03fe` and three of the candidate, interleaved, comparing the step means of finalize, commit and create and the guest's lines, in nanoseconds. The change is kept if no step is slower beyond the boots' own spread; what it gains is reported as measured.
5. *Review.* The coordinator reviews the diff and does the security review: this removes bounds checks from the frame allocator, so an invariant broken elsewhere would become a wild write where it is a panic today. Each unchecked access is checked against the invariant its comment names.

**Part 2 items 1 and 4 as built and timed (2026-11-16).** Deepwyrm `5eebd74` merges `ab78d3b` (the free bitmap with carved storage) and `8b73d2d` (the paths without bounds checks), by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the specification above; the coordinator reviewed every unchecked access against its invariant and found no defect, and re-ran on `8b73d2d` fmt, clippy (236 / 86), kernel-check, 1,515 host tests and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots.

- *Beyond the specification, and kept:* `seed` is `unsafe` too and now really checks that each boot free range lies inside one initial range (a panic at boot otherwise); `free_run` also requires a non-empty, aligned range, which `validate_candidate` checks before every call (a reversed range would otherwise reach unchecked indexing).
- *Not done:* Miri was not run; the unchecked accesses are exercised through the host tests' debug assertions only.
- *Timed under KVM,* one CPU, 12 boots each of `189caa2` (the code of `65d03fe`) and `8b73d2d`, interleaved; 11 of each passed their check (the two failures are the row added to §9 today, one on each tree). Medians, with the range over the boots:

| Measure | Before | After | Change |
|---|---|---|---|
| finalize step | 53.4 µs (53.2–53.4) | 47.9 µs (47.9–48.1) | −10.1% |
| close, fragmented 448 MiB | 183 µs (181–189) | 37 µs (36–38) | −80% |
| close, unfragmented 448 MiB | 29 µs (28–36) | 26 µs (25–26) | −10% |
| release step | 200 ns (198–202) | 189 ns (185–194) | −5.5% |
| fill step | 1.59 µs (1.58–1.61) | 1.56 µs (1.55–1.57) | −1.8% |
| commit step | 17.8 µs (17.6–18.3) | 18.0 µs (17.6–18.3) | +0.8%, same range |
| map, unmap, protect steps | 167, 242, 369 µs | 166, 242, 368 µs | within 0.6% |
| create step | 345 µs (340–353) | 355 µs (342–368) | +2.9%, ranges overlap |
| create of the three timed objects | 25.3, 27.1, 28.0 ms | 24.3, 27.1, 27.8 ms | −3.8%, +0.2%, −0.6% |

  The create step mean is taken over every create in a boot, most of them the churn probe's, whose number varies from boot to boot (443 to 596 steps); the three creates the guest times itself did not move. Whether any create step is slower was not established, and the cause of the higher mean was not measured. Why the fragmented close gains so much more than the finalize step was not looked at either.
- *The earlier estimate* ("the language moves a finalize step by about 1%") was about Rust against C with the bitmap in both; the 10% here is the bitmap and the unchecked paths together against main.

**Not built.** Item 3 (narrow slot writers) exists since §12.6.

**Item 5: the two index changes, approved and specified (2026-11-16, read at `d5fa047`).** Mike: "Approve the two index proposals, time them under KVM". Two commits in one lane, by an Opus worker, so each is timed by itself.

*What the code does today.* `resolve_at` (`frame_roles/chain.rs`) is called for one page at a time by the journal (`authorize_leaf` and its check when the leaf is staged), the publisher (`page_descriptor`), the DMA adapter and the boot write, each keeping a `BackingCursor` across pages. For a chain the cursor makes a page cost a check of the run it names. For an index root the cursor is ignored: `index_resolve` calls `index_walk`, which checks the root and then, for each level, reads an entry and checks the slot it names. `release_index_step` (`frame_roles/index.rs`) calls `index_walk` once for every entry it visits.

*5a. A place in the index for the cursor.*

1. `BackingCursor` gains the leaf it last used (`leaf`, an identity, and `leaf_first`, the first object page the leaf covers), empty at `new`. `run` and `run_page` keep their meaning: under an index root they name the run (eager) or the committed page (on demand) last resolved and the object page it starts at, or the head when there is none. The cursor still holds no borrow and grants nothing.
2. Under an index root `resolve_at` first takes the root as now (`index_root(head, RootUse::Live)`, the page below `root.pages`), then tries, in order:
   - *the same run:* if the cursor names a run, its slot is live, it is an `ObjectPage` whose owner is the head and whose place is `run_page`, and the page lies in `[run_page, run_page + its pages)`, the answer is that run at that offset. No node is read.
   - *the same leaf:* else, if the root has more than one level, the cursor names a leaf, the page lies in its 512 pages, and its slot is live and is an `IndexNode` whose owner is the head and whose place is `node_place(leaf_first, 0)`, one entry is read from it. A zero entry under an on-demand root is `Absent`. A non-zero entry is checked with `index_entry` exactly as the walk's last level does, and the cursor's run becomes it.
   - *the walk:* else, or when any check above fails (a stale place is never an error by itself), `index_walk` as now, and the cursor takes the leaf and the run it found. `IndexStep` carries the identity of a node below the root so the walk can hand the leaf's back.
3. *Why a place grants nothing.* Each use checks the slot the place names by identity (slot and generation), its role, its owner and its place. Under a root that `RootUse::Live` takes, the slots owned by a root are exactly the nodes and frames its tree reaches, each at its place: `check_indexes` counts both and `index_count` checks each entry, an eager root's runs gapless and in order. A root being built, not yet zeroed or being freed is refused before any place is tried, as it is today. A freed frame's identity is dead, so a decommitted page falls back to the walk and is `Absent`.
4. *Tests (host, behaviour).* For an on-demand root of one, two and three levels and an eager root whose runs cross leaves: every page resolved in order, in reverse and in a fixed pseudo-random order with one cursor gives the run and address a fresh cursor gives. A page decommitted after the cursor resolved it is `Absent`; committed again it resolves to the new frame; a frame whose slot another backing has reused is not returned. A cursor whose head is one backing and whose leaf and run name another backing's (built through a test-only constructor) resolves the first backing's page. A cursor that holds a place in an eager root is refused once a release step has begun, and one in a root being built or not yet zeroed is refused. With a counting `IndexMemory`: the pages of one eager run after its first are resolved with no read, and consecutive pages of one leaf of a two-level on-demand root with one read each.

*5b. A release step walks once for a leaf.*

1. `release_index_step` walks to the cursor's leaf once and then handles the leaf's entries from the cursor directly, reading each and checking it with `index_entry` as the walk's last level does, until the leaf ends or the budget is spent. The three eager cases and the on-demand cases are as now, as is the freeing of emptied nodes upward when the cursor reaches a node's end, done from the steps of that one walk. It then walks again for the next leaf.
2. *Nothing observable changes:* for every cursor and budget the step returns the same cursor, pages freed and entries visited as today, and leaves the same state. A step may still visit up to `INDEX_MAX_LEVELS - 1` entries past its budget.
3. *Tests (host, behaviour).* Every existing release-step test passes unchanged. New: for an on-demand root sparse and dense over three leaves, an eager root with runs that cross leaves, end with a leaf and end inside one, and a root that is its own leaf, and for a spread of budgets, one step of budget `b` from a cursor gives the same cursor, freed and visited, and the same state by the invariant check, as steps of budget 1 repeated until they have visited at least `b`. With a counting `IndexMemory`, releasing a full leaf of an on-demand root in one step reads the root's entry for that leaf a bounded number of times, not once per page.

*Checks and measurement.* fmt, clippy (236 / 86), kernel-check, the host tests, and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots. Then time under KVM on one CPU, interleaved, four boots each of the lane's base, 5a, and 5a with 5b: the guest's map, protect, unmap and close lines for the fragmented and unfragmented objects and the step means, in real time. Each change is kept if nothing is slower beyond the boots' own spread; what it gains is reported as measured.

*Review.* The coordinator reviews both and does the security review of 5a: it changes how the frames a mapping, a DMA mapping and a boot write name are found, so each fast path is checked against item 3.

*Not in this change.* `decommit_on_demand_range` walks once for each entry too, as the release step did; it is the same change and is not built until asked for.

**Item 5 as built and timed (2026-11-17).** Deepwyrm `2fd831d` merges `9a1f928` (5a) and `5dae6e5` (5b), by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5) from the specification above. The coordinator reviewed both diffs, with the security review of 5a against item 3, and found no defect; it re-ran on `5dae6e5` fmt, clippy (236 / 86), kernel-check, 1,521 host tests and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots, and on the merged tree the four checks (1,524 tests), an `--smp` boot and four `--kvm` boots.

- *Timed under KVM,* one CPU, six interleaved rounds of `189caa2` (main before the allocator build), `9a1f928` and `5dae6e5`; 6, 6 and 5 boots passed their check (the failure is the §9 row of 2026-11-16). Medians, with the range over the boots:

| Measure | Base | 5a | 5a and 5b |
|---|---|---|---|
| finalize step | 53.3 µs (53.3–53.4) | 39.9 µs (39.8–39.9) | 27.2 µs (27.1–27.2) |
| protect step | 368 µs (366–371) | 327 µs (325–329) | 328 µs (325–329) |
| unmap step | 242 µs (241–243) | 225 µs (224–226) | 226 µs (217–226) |
| map step | 167 µs (164–174) | 156 µs (154–161) | 158 µs (155–167) |
| commit step | 17.9 µs (17.6–18.1) | 16.7 µs (16.6–16.8) | 16.9 µs (16.6–17.6) |
| fill step | 1.58 µs (1.58–1.61) | 1.57 µs (1.56–1.59) | 1.53 µs (1.52–1.55) |
| fragmented 448 MiB: map | 97.2 ms (96.9–98.9) | 79.2 ms (78.7–80.4) | 80.7 ms (78.9–82.5) |
| fragmented 448 MiB: protect | 102.7 ms (102.5–103.5) | 78.5 ms (78.4–79.8) | 78.7 ms (76.8–79.0) |
| fragmented 448 MiB: unmap | 79.7 ms (79.4–82.2) | 69.3 ms (68.2–70.8) | 70.2 ms (60.6–70.3) |
| fragmented 448 MiB: close | 183 µs (181–195) | 144 µs (143–164) | 112 µs (111–121) |

  The unfragmented and the eager 256 MiB objects, which are chains, and the create, teardown and release steps are within about 1%. The number of finalize steps differs from boot to boot with the churn probe (8,792 to 12,132 here), so the step mean is over a varying mix; the time all finalize steps of a boot take together was about 500 ms on the base, 410 ms with 5a and 290 to 330 ms with both.
- *With the allocator build (the merged tree).* Two interleaved rounds of `5eebd74` and `2fd831d`, and two more boots of `2fd831d`: the finalize step is 48.0 µs before the merge and 22.0 µs after it (all finalize steps of a boot together: about 437 ms, then 236 to 275 ms); protect 351 to 365 µs, then 317 to 328 µs; unmap 238 µs, then 217 µs; the fragmented object's map 98 ms, then 79 ms.
- *The close of the fragmented object reads 62 µs slower in the merged tree; tracked, and accepted by Mike on 2026-11-17 ("Accepted").* It takes 98 to 100 µs in all four boots of `2fd831d` against 36 and 37 µs in the two of `5eebd74`; the unfragmented close is 26 µs in both. What a throwaway probe (lane `m7-close-probe`, by an Opus worker, never merged; 36 KVM boots) showed:
  - The close queues the object and runs one finalize step in the same hold, so the guest's close time is that one step and the call around it.
  - The object is laid out in one of two ways, the same in every boot of a build. In the builds that read 36 to 37 µs its first step frees about 500 pages of small runs (4.0 µs); in `2fd831d` and the first probe build on it the object begins with a run of 32,768 pages, which the first step frees (11.5 µs).
  - With the same layout the two trees are the same: from the close's entry to the thread's next entry into the kernel takes about 23 µs in 2 boots of the tree before the merge and 22 of the tree after it, and every part of the hold reads the same. All 66 finalize steps of the object take 1.02 to 1.05 ms before the merge and 0.51 to 0.57 ms after it.
  - Under `--icount` the close does the same work in both trees (118 and 115 units).
  - *Not found:* where the other 54 µs of the long-run layout's close goes (the step is 7.5 µs of the 62), since that layout never came up with the hold's parts timed; and what picks the layout. It is not the kernel's footprint alone: 18 builds padded by 0 to 64 pages all gave the small-run layout. By reading, an eager object's runs are free blocks in list order (`allocate_run` takes the head of the smallest sufficient order), and neighbouring blocks make one run only when they come in rising address order; that this is what differs was not shown.
  - *What follows for measuring:* the fragmented object's `close-us` is one finalize step on whatever shape the object got, and does not compare builds. The time all of the object's finalize steps take does.
- *A host effect on create times, found while timing.* From 23:01 on 2026-11-16 every KVM boot showed one create step of 30 to 90 ms (0.84 to 1.17 ms in the 39 passing boots before it) and a fragmented create of 230 to 550 ms (about 27 ms before). It is not the index changes: `189caa2` and `5eebd74` booted on 2026-11-17 show the same (create steps of 31 to 42 ms, fragmented creates of 330 to 422 ms). In those boots the host had about 2.5 GiB free of 62 with 49 GiB in its page cache, and its `allocstall_movable` and `compact_stall` counters rose by 75 to 140 and 60 to 150 during each boot; `compact_stall` stood at 5 before the first long step. Map, protect, unmap, close and the finalize step read as before in the same boots. The timing script (`.tmp/coord/time-kvm.sh`) now prints the host's free memory and those counters around each boot; a create time from a boot in which they rise is not a measurement of the kernel.
- *The twelve further boots* of `5dae6e5` that were to show whether the long create step recurs ran in 16 minutes (11 passed; the failure is the same §9 row) and all fell after 23:01, so they show the host effect and nothing about the change.

**Item 5, the decommit path, as built and timed (2026-11-17).** Mike, asked whether to make `decommit_on_demand_range` walk once for a leaf too: "test the decommit_on_demand_range before we see about ripping it out (unless it's redundant, then rip it out)", then, with the measurement, "Build it".

- *Not redundant.* It has one caller, `release_unmapped_step_within`, and is the only way the pages of a live on-demand object are freed when part of it is unmapped: the path a large `free` takes through the SDK.
- *What it cost.* The step report could not say: a release step that runs inside an unmap step is counted in that step. A throwaway probe around the release step (lane `m7-decommit-probe`, by an Opus worker, never merged) gave, in three KVM boots of `815f2a3`, 148 release steps a boot, each freeing 512 pages in 26.1 µs (51 ns a page), 3.87 ms a boot.
- *The change.* Deepwyrm `702f176` merges `bd77633`, by an Opus worker (Claude Opus 5.5): the function walks to the cursor's entry once and, at a leaf, reads the leaf's following entries directly, each checked with `index_entry` as the walk's last level checks it, until the leaf or the range ends or the budget is spent. Nodes stay and nothing observable changes. Six host tests: one call of budget `b` against calls of budget 1 for one, two and three levels over many ranges and budgets, the reads of a leaf's parent entry, and a decommit across leaves.
- *Review and checks.* The coordinator reviewed the diff with its security review (a frame is freed only after its entry is checked for identity, owner and place; the loop stops where the old one did) and found no defect; on `bd77633`, whose tree is the merge's: fmt, clippy (236 / 86), kernel-check, 1,530 host tests, and the plain, `--smp` and `--icount` boots.
- *Timed under KVM,* one CPU, four interleaved boots each of `815f2a3` and `bd77633` with the probe: a release step of 512 pages takes 26.1 to 26.3 µs before and 22.2 to 22.4 µs after (51.1 and 43.6 ns a page), and a boot's 148 steps 3.87 ms before and 3.30 ms after.

### 12.13 M7 as built: the final boots and the stage's state (2026-11-17)

**The stage.** M7's four steps (§12.1) are built at the development tier, each with its record: the buddy allocator (§12.2); the smaller role slot (§12.3, §12.6) and the role table in pieces mapped at one range (§12.9); the index for fragmented eager backings and a fragmented guest in the check (§12.10); a fragmented backing allocated and cancelled in steps (§12.11); and step 4 (§12.12): node access, the free bitmap, the allocator's paths without bounds checks, and one walk for a leaf in lookups, release steps and decommits. Page migration is not built, by Mike's decision; §9 holds what reopens it. The BSP's timer re-arm fault found on the way is fixed (§12.7).

**The final triple.** Deepwyrm `702f176` (code; the checkout is at this record's commit after it), Wyrmroot `da9115b`, SDK `56eb579`. Wyrmroot keeps its pin of Deepwyrm `23972de`: `abi/` and `crates/` are unchanged since it (`git diff 23972de 702f176 -- abi crates` is empty), and the pin's ABI fingerprint is the one the kernel reports.

**Final boots (development tier, by the coordinator, 2026-11-17).** `dev-boot --check --sdk` on the triple, one at a time, host load 0.5 to 1.6 on 16 cores: once on one CPU, 20 times with `--smp`, once with `--icount`, and 8 times with `--kvm` (`.tmp/dev-boot/1794929436294431061` to `…1794932363549411524`). All 30 passed; no kernel panic in any serial log.

| | One CPU and 20 `--smp` (QEMU TCG, not hardware times) | 8 `--kvm` (one CPU, real time) |
|---|---|---|
| Boots passing | 21 of 21 | 8 of 8 |
| Longest step | 24 to 32 ms (commit or fill; the same on the trees before step 4's last merges) | 1.14 to 1.18 ms (commit) |
| Teardown owed, longest stretch | 0.04 to 0.26 s | 0.09 to 0.10 s |
| Finalization owed, longest stretch | 0.16 to 1.34 s | 0.10 s |
| Longest finalize gap | 3.5 to 7.8 ms | 5.1 ms |

Under KVM, medians of the 8 boots with their ranges: step means map 163 µs (153 to 167), unmap 228 µs (227 to 230), protect 334 µs (333 to 337), commit 17.0 µs (16.5 to 17.4), create 354 µs (337 to 373), teardown 20.4 µs, finalize 21.9 µs (21.8 to 22.0), fill 1.51 µs. The 448 MiB eager object in one run: create 26.6 ms, map 82.6 ms, protect 79.7 ms, unmap 62.7 ms, close 26 µs. The same object in 10,276 runs: create 27.4 ms, map 80.6 ms, protect 80.4 ms, unmap 71.7 ms, close 35 µs. The fragmented object's figures depend on the shape it gets, which differs from build to build (§12.12): its unmap was 62 ms at `2fd831d`, where it began with a long run, and 70 to 72 ms on the trees either side of the decommit change. Under `--icount` a finalize step is 277 thousand instructions (635 thousand before M7, §9) and a commit step 52.6 thousand.

**What M7 leaves, each with its stage in §9.** The commit step's 1,600 to 2,000 instructions over the kernel before the index: M8. Under KVM the second CPU never starts, and a KVM boot has lost the last line of `hello on-demand`: M8's entry. Where 54 µs of the close of an object that begins with a long run goes, and what picks an object's shape: accepted by Mike (§12.12). Compaction: not scheduled, with its three reopening conditions.

**Tooling.** M7 added nothing that closes with it: its probes are part of `dev-boot --check`, and `--icount` and `--kvm` are standing modes of `dev-boot`. The benchmark and probe lanes were throwaway and are not in any tree.

**The host.** On 2026-11-17 the disk images and product folders of 1,113 boot directories under `.tmp/dev-boot` were deleted on Mike's word (184 GiB; their serial logs stay, and the 35 directories this plan cites are whole). They had filled the host's page cache: with them gone the host has 40 GiB free, its reclaim and compaction counters did not move across the boots above, and create times read as before 23:01 on 2026-11-16 (§12.12).

**Lanes.** Two throwaway probe lanes remain until Mike says to delete them, each holding the release-step probe that is in no tree: `lane/m7-decommit-probe` (`e089fae`) and `lane/m7-decommit-time` (`33f216a`, on `bd77633`). `worktree-lanes.py audit` also names the unregistered directory `.worktrees/deepwyrm/.tmp`, which predates M7. Nothing of M7 since `8d04ed8` is pushed at the time of this record.
